the intersection of the following hyperplane and half-spaces

\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\sum_{i\in[n]}x_{i}={\left(\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{n+1}{2}\right)}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{I\in\mathcal{I}}\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}\geq{\left(\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{|I|+1}{2}\right)}\right\},

where
\mathcal{I}=\{I\subsetneq[n]\,:\,\exists\text{ a\penalty\ $\delta$-permutree with edge cut }(I\,\|\,[n]\setminus I)\}
.

See Figure
2.22
for some examples of
\delta
-permutreehedra.

Proposition 2.3.20
.

Let
\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{w_{0}})-(\mathbf{e})=(n-1,n-3,\ldots,-n+3,-n+1)={(2i-n-1)}_{i\in[n]}
. The
1
-skeleton of the
\PPT(\delta)
oriented with the vector
\mathbf{v}
is isomorphic to the Hasse diagram of the
\delta
-permutree rotation lattice.

Figure 2.22:
The permutreehedra
\PPT(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}})
(left) and
\PPT(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}})
(right).

Remark 2.3.21
.

Consider
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\}
. Although the
\delta
-permutree lattice might not be isomorphic to the
\delta^{\prime}
-permutree lattice, the
\delta
-permutreehedron is isomorphic to the
\delta^{\prime}
-permutreehedron. That is, all these permutreehedrons are associahedrons.

2.4  Finite Coxeter Groups

In this section we present the basic theory of finite Coxeter groups basing ourselves on
[
Hum90
]
and
[
BB06
]
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
S
be any set together with integers
m_{s,t}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
for all pairs
(s,t)\in S\times S
such that

—

m_{s,t}=1
if and only if
s=t
,

—

m_{s,t}=m_{t,s}
for all
s,t\in S
.

The
Coxeter graph
is the graph with vertex set
S
and edges
(s,t)
where
m_{s,t}\geq 3
. If
m_{s,t}\geq 4
the edges are labeled with
m_{s,t}
, otherwise they are unlabeled. The corresponding
Coxeter group
is the group generated as
W=\langle S\,|\,(st)^{m_{s,t}}=e\rangle
where
e
is the identity element. If
m_{s,t}\geq 3
we call
(st)^{m_{s,t}}=e
a
braid relation
.

The pair
(W,S)
is called
Coxeter system
,
S
is the set of
Coxeter generators
and
|S|
is the
rank
of
W
. The system
(W,S)
is said to be
irreducible
if its Coxeter graph is connected. Figure
2.23
contains the Coxeter graphs of all irreducible finite Coxeter groups.

Proposition 2.4.2

(
[
Cox35
]
)
.

An irreducible Coxeter Group is finite if and only if its Coxeter graph appears in Figure
2.23
.

Figure 2.23:
The Coxeter graphs of all irreducible finite Coxeter groups. The infinite family
A_{n}
is defined for
n\geq 1
,
B_{n}
for
n\geq 2
,
D_{n}
for
n\geq 4
, and
I_{n}
for
n\geq 5
.

Remark 2.4.3
.

Since
m_{s,s}=1
, we have that
s^{2}=e
for all generators. Thus, the relation
(st)^{m_{s,t}}=e
implies

\underbrace{ststst\cdots st}_{\text{$m_{s,t}$ times}}=\underbrace{tststs\cdots ts}_{\text{$m_{s,t}$ times}}.

Remark 2.4.4
.

If the Coxeter graph of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
consists of the connected components
G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}
, then
W
is the direct product of the Coxeter groups
W_{1}\times\cdots\times W_{m}
of each connected component.

Example 2.4.5
.

Consider a graph
G
of
n
isolated vertices. This signifies that
m_{s,t}=2
for all
s,t\in S
and the corresponding Coxeter group
W=A_{1}\times\cdots\times A_{1}
is the group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Example 2.4.6
.

Consider the Coxeter system of type
(I_{n},\{s,t\})
where
m_{s,t}=n
. Any word in this group is expressed via these generators and thus only a product of
s
and
t
. Sending
s
and
t
to the appropriate reflections of an
n
-gon, one can see that the Coxeter group
I_{n}=\langle s,t\,|\,s^{2}=t^{2}=(st)^{n}=e\rangle
is isomorphic to
D_{n}=\langle s,r\,|\,s^{2}=(sr)^{2}=(r)^{n}=e\rangle
.

Definition 2.4.7
.

Given a Coxeter system
(W,S)
, its
reflections
are
T=\{wsw^{-1}\,:\,s\in S,\,w\in W\}
. In this way the generators in
S
are also called the
simple reflections
of
W
and the
right and left inversion sets
of
w\in W
are the sets

\begin{split}I_{R}(w)=&\{t\in T\,:\,l(wt)<l(w)\},\\
I_{L}(w)=&\{t\in T\,:\,l(tw)<l(w)\}.\end{split}

Definition 2.4.8
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system. Considering the elements
w\in W
written as products of generators
w=s_{1}\cdots s_{k}
for
s_{i}\in S
, the
length
of
w
is the minimum
k
such that
w=s_{1}\cdots s_{k}
, is denoted as
l(w)
, and
s_{1}\cdots s_{k}
is said to be a
reduced word
or
reduced expression
of
w
.

Remark 2.4.9
.

Due to Definition
2.4.1
of Coxeter groups, an element
w\in W
has a set of reduced words all related by a sequence of braid moves. On one hand, this signifies that the length is well-defined. On the other, this proves that if a transposition
s_{i}
is in a reduced word of
w
, then
s_{i}
is in every reduced word of
w
.

Proposition 2.4.10
.

Some useful properties of the length function include :

—

l(ws)=l(w)\pm 1
,

—

l(w)=|I(w)|
,

—

l(w)=l(w^{-1})
,

—

|l(u)-l(w)|\leq l(uw)\leq l(u)+l(w)
.

Definition 2.4.11
.

Given an element
w\in W
of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
, the
(right) descent set
and
(left) descent set
are

\begin{split}D_{R}(w)=&\{s\in S\,:\,l(ws)<l(w)\},\\
D_{L}(w)=&\{s\in S\,:\,l(sw)<l(w)\}.\end{split}

We call their elements the right (resp. left)
descents
of
w
.

Proposition 2.4.12
.

Let
s\in S
and
w\in W
. Then

—

s\in D_{R}(W)
if and only if there exists a reduced word of
w
ending with the letter
s
.

—

s\in D_{L}(W)
if and only if there exists a reduced word of
w
starting with the letter
s
.

Definition 2.4.13
.

Let
J\subseteq S
of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
. The subgroup of
W
generated by
J
is a
parabolic subgroup
of
W
and is denoted by

W_{J}
. The
quotient
corresponding to
J
is

W^{J}
=\{w\in W\,:\,l(ws)>l(w)\text{ for all }s\in J\}
The Coxeter graph of
W_{J}
is the subgraph of the Coxeter graph induced by
J
. In this context we note by

W_{\langle s_{i}\rangle}
the parabolic group generated by all adjacent transpositions except
s_{i}
.

Proposition 2.4.14
.

For an element
w\in W
,
w\in W^{J}
if and only if no reduced expression of
w
ends with a generator in
J
.

Proposition 2.4.15
.

Given
w\in W
, there is a unique
w^{J}\in W^{J}
and
w_{J}\in W_{J}
such that
w=w^{J}w_{J}
. Moreover,
l(w)=l(w^{J})+l(w_{J})
and
w^{J}
is the unique element of minimal length in the coset
wW_{J}
.

2.4.1 Weak Order

For any Coxeter system we can define a myriad of orders. Here we are interested only in the right and left weak orders.

Definition 2.4.16
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system,
s\in S
, and
w\in W
. The
right (resp. left) weak order

\leq
is the transitive closure of the cover relations
w\lessdot ws
(resp.
w\lessdot sw
) if and only if
l(w)<l(ws)
(resp.
l(w)<l(sw)
).

Figure 2.24:
The right weak order of the Coxeter groups
A_{1}\times A_{2}
,
I_{5}
, and
B_{3}
.

Figure
2.24
shows some examples of the weak order. In our case of finite Coxeter groups, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 2.4.17
.

Let
W
be a finite group. There exists an element
w_{0}\in W
such that
w<w_{0}
for all
w\in W
. We call
w_{0}
the
longest element
of
W
.

The longest element is crucial for the finite case of Coxeter groups. We list some of its properties.

Proposition 2.4.18
.

Let
w_{0}
be the longest element of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
. Then

—

w_{0}^{2}=e
,

—

l(w_{0})=|T|
.

In full generality, the weak order is only a complete semi-lattice. However, in our finite case Proposition
2.4.17
together with Proposition
1.1.12
gives us the lattice property.

Proposition 2.4.19
.

The poset
(W,\leq)
is a lattice.

Unless stated otherwise,
\leq
refers to the right weak order.

Proposition 2.4.20
.

The weak order has the following properties.

1.

The reduced words of an element
w\in W
are in bijection with maximal chains of the interval
[e,w]
.

2.

u\leq w
if and only if
l(u)+l(u^{-1}w)=l(w)
.

3.

u\leq w
if and only if any reduced word of
u
is a prefix of a reduced word of
w
.

4.

If
s\in D_{L}(u)\cap D_{L}(w)
, then
u\leq w
if and only if
su\leq sw
.

5.

u\leq_{R}w
if and only if
I_{L}(u)\subseteq I_{L}(w)
.

Certain morphisms of Coxeter systems are of particular usefulness to us.

Proposition 2.4.21
.

Consider a Coxeter system
(W,S)
.

—

The maps
w\to ww_{0}
and
w\to w_{0}w
are antiautomorphisms of
W
.

—

The map
w\to w_{0}ww_{0}
is an automorphism.

—

The map
w\to w^{-1}
is an automorphism sending the right weak order to the left weak order.

2.4.2 Type
A

Coxeter groups of type
A
are of particular interest for us since they are precisely the symmetric groups.

Consider
S=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}
where identifying generators with adjacent transpositions as
s_{i}=(i\,\,i+1)
. Given an adjacent transposition
s
and a reduced word
x
of a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, the word
xs
(resp.
sx
) corresponds to the permutation
\pi\cdot s_{i}
(resp.
s_{i}\cdot\pi
). Whenever we consider there is no room for confusion we use interchangeably a permutation
\pi
instead of a reduced word
x
.

Moreover, a quick calculation shows that the generators
S
satisfy the relations
(s_{i}s_{i+1})^{3}=e
and
(s_{i}s_{j})^{2}=e
if
|j-i|>1
. This gives the intuition for the following result.

Proposition 2.4.22
.

(\mathfrak{S}_{n},S)
is a Coxeter system of type
A_{n-1}
.

This construction leads to a reframing of certain characteristics of permutations in terms of Coxeter groups.

Proposition 2.4.23
.

Let
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Then,

—

l(\pi)=|\inv(\pi)|
,

—

D_{R}(\pi)=\{s_{i}\in S\,:\,\pi_{i}>\pi_{i+1}\}
.

We finish with a useful lemma given by Proposition
2.4.20
in the case of permutations.

Lemma 2.4.24
.

A permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
permutes the values
j
and
j+1
if and only if it has a reduced word starting with
s_{j}
.

Démonstration.

Recall that
\pi
permuting
j
and
j+1
is equivalent to
\pi
containing the inversion
(j\,\,j+1)
. Proposition
2.4.20
(5) gives us that this is equivalent to
s_{j}\cdot e<\pi
where
e
is the identity permutation. Proposition
2.4.20
(3) tells us that this occurs if and only if
s_{j}
is a prefix of a reduced word of
\pi
as desired.
∎

2.4.3 Type
B

For an easier reading we will use the notation

\overline{i}
for the negative integer
-i
.

Definition 2.4.25
.

Let

\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
be the group of permutations on
[\pm n]:=\{\overline{n},\ldots,\overline{1},1,\ldots,n\}
satisfying
\pi(\overline{i})=\overline{\pi(i)}
for all
i\in[n]
. This group is called the group of all
signed permutations
on
[n]
. Given
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
we write
w=w(\overline{n})\cdots w(\overline{1})w(1)\cdots w(n)
in the usual
1
-line permutation notation or
w=[w(1)\cdots w(n)]
in what is called the
window notation
of
w
.

Remark 2.4.26
.

Notice that
\mathfrak{S}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
naturally by identifying
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with the signed permutations
w
such that
w([n])=w([n])
.

Similar to the definitions for permutations given in Definition
2.1.31
, signed permutations have a cycle decomposition as now exemplified.

Example 2.4.27
.

If
w=5\,\overline{3}\,\overline{2}\,\overline{1}\,4\,\overline{4}\,1\,3\,2\,\overline{5}
, then
w=[\overline{4}\,1\,3\,2\,\overline{5}]
and one of its cycle decompositions is
(1\,\overline{4}\,\overline{2}\,\overline{1}\,4\,2)(3)(\overline{3})(5\,\overline{5})
.

Similar to Definition
2.1.5
, cycle decompositions give us adjacent transpositions.

Definition 2.4.28
.

In the context of signed permutations the
adjacent transpositions
are
s_{i}:=(i\,\,i+1)(\overline{i}\,\,\overline{i+1})
for
i\in[n-1]
and
s_{n}:=(\overline{n}\,\,n)
.

Proposition 2.4.29
.

The group of signed permutations
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
is generated by the adjacent transpositions
S^{B}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
.

Definition 2.4.30
.

We say that
(i,j)\in[n]^{2}
is a

B
-inversion
of
\pi
if
i<j
and
\pi^{-1}_{i}>\pi^{-1}_{j}
or
i\leq j
and
\pi^{-1}_{\overline{i}}>\pi^{-1}_{j}
and denote by

\inv_{B}(\pi)
the set of
B
-inversions of
\pi
. We define the length of
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
by
\ell_{B}(w):=|\inv_{B}(w)|
.

Proposition 2.4.31
.

(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B},S^{B})
is a Coxeter group of type
B
.

2.4.4 Type
D

Definition 2.4.32
.

Let

\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
be the subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
of signed permutations having an even number of negative values in their window notation. This group is called the group of all
even signed permutations
of
[n]
.

As a subgroup of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
, we can describe a generating set of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
in the following way.

Proposition 2.4.33
.

The group of signed permutations
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
is generated by the adjacent transpositions
S^{D}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
where
s_{i}:=(i\,\,i+1)(\overline{i}\,\,\overline{i+1})
for
i\in[n-1]
and
s_{n}:=(n\,\,\overline{n-1})(n-1\,\,\overline{n})
.

Definition 2.4.34
.

We say that
(i,j)\in[n]^{2}
is a

D
-inversion
of
\pi
if
i<j
and
\pi^{-1}_{i}>\pi^{-1}_{j}
or
i<j
and
\pi^{-1}_{\overline{i}}>\pi^{-1}_{j}
and denote by

\inv_{D}(\pi)
the set of
D
-inversions of
\pi
. We define the length of
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D}
by
\ell_{D}(w):=|\inv_{D}(w)|
.

Proposition 2.4.35
.

(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{D},S^{D})
is a Coxeter group of type
D
.

2.4.5 Automata

As the theory of Coxeter groups is based on words, it is natural that the theory of automata has seen applications on it (see
[
BH93
]
and
[
HNW16
]
). Here we concentrate ourselves on the automaticity of Coxeter groups of
[
BH93
]
.

The automaticity was given in general for all finitely generated Coxeter groups using small roots. Since we do not delve into the geometric aspect of Coxeter groups, we rephrase their results using inversion sets. As a disclaimer, we can only do this since we are working with finite Coxeter groups.

Proposition 2.4.36
.

Let
(W,S)
be the Coxeter system of a finite Coxeter group
W
. The language of reduced words of
W
is regular. Moreover, it is recognized by the DFA with states
Q=\{I(w)\,:\,w\in W\}
and transitions
I(w)\to I(ws_{i})
labeled by
s_{i}
if and only if
s_{i}\notin D_{R}(w)
. The starting set is the empty set and all states are final states.

Example 2.4.37
.

The language of reduced words of
(\mathfrak{S}_{3},\{s_{1},s_{2}\})
is recognized by the automaton in Figure
2.25
.

Figure 2.25:
A DFA recognizing the language of reduced words of
(\mathfrak{S}_{3},\{s_{1},s_{2}\})
.

2.4.6 Coxeter Sorting

In this section we present the theory of
c
-sorting following
[
Rea07b
]
. As a starting point we define Cambrian congruences for any Coxeter group.

Definition 2.4.38
.

Let
W
be a Coxeter group and
\overrightarrow{G}
be a complete orientation of the Coxeter diagram
G
. The
Cambrian congruence
associated to
\overrightarrow{G}
is the smallest lattice congruence that identifies the elements of the interval
[t,tsts\cdots]
(
m_{s,t}-1
letters) for all
s\to t
.

Figure
2.26
shows an example of a Coxeter graph and the corresponding Cambrian congruence.

Figure 2.26:
A complete orientation of the Coxeter graph of
B_{4}
and the lattice congruence it specifies. The minimal elements of the lattice congruence are in black and the congruence classes are shown in bolded blue. Notice that we do not draw the squares given by the commuting relations between
\{s_{1},s_{3}\},\,\{s_{1},s_{4}\}
, or
\{s_{2},s_{4}\}
as any congruence relation of rank
2
in these cases would force a congruence relation of rank
1
following Remark
1.1.22
.

Remark 2.4.39
.

Notice that in type
A
the Cambrian congruences correspond to the
\delta
-permutree congruences
\equiv_{\delta}
where
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\}^{n}
as in Proposition
2.3.15
.

Definition 2.4.40
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system. A
Coxeter element
is a word of length
n=|S|
where each generator appears exactly once.

Remark 2.4.41
.

Notice that Coxeter elements codify complete orientations of the Coxeter graph and thus Cambrian congruences. Indeed, given a Coxeter element
c=s_{1}\cdots s_{n}
we orient
j\to j-1
(resp.
j-1\to j
) if
j
appears before (resp. after)
j-1
in
c
.

Definition 2.4.42
.

Given a Coxeter element
c
together with a fixed reduced word
c=s_{1}\cdots s_{n}
, its
infinite word
is
c^{\infty}=s_{1}\cdots s_{n}|s_{1}\cdots s_{n}|s_{1}\cdots s_{n}|\cdots
where “
|
” are dividers that mark the separation of each instance of
c
.

The

c
-sorting word
of an element
w\in W
is the lexicographically first subword of
c^{\infty}
that is a reduced word of
w
. We denote it as

w(c)
. The
c
-sorting word
w(c)
can be divided into finite factors
L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{n}
where each factor
L_{i}
is a set of letters of
w(c)
appearing between the dividers
i
and
i+1
. We say that an element
w\in W
is

c
-sortable
or
Coxeter-sortable
if the factors satisfy
L_{1}\supseteq L_{2}\supseteq\ldots\supseteq L_{n}
.

Example 2.4.43
.

Let
\pi:=3421
and take the Coxeter element
c:=s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}
. Consider the infinite word
c^{\infty}={\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}c}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}c}\cdots={\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}}\cdots.
Then the
c
-sorting word of
\pi
is
\pi(c)={\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s_{2}\cdot s_{3}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}L_{1}}\supset{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{2}}\supset{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{3}}=\emptyset
,
\pi
is
c
-sortable.

Remark 2.4.44
.

Notice that
c^{\infty}
does not actually need to be infinite. Indeed, the choosing of a Coxeter element implies the choosing of a reduced word of the largest element
w_{0}
.

Remark 2.4.45
.

Although the
c
-sorting word depends heavily on the choosing of the reduced word of
c
, the property of being
c
-sortable does not. Thus, we refer to
c
-sortable elements without specifying the reduced word of
c
.

Coxeter sorting is of interest to us as it recovers the classical notion of stack-sorting
[
Knu73
]
via another lens.

Proposition 2.4.46
.

For the Coxeter word
c=s_{1}\cdots s_{n-1}
, the
c
-sortable permutations are precisely the stack-sortable permutations of
[
Knu73
]
.

The following are some simple lemmas showing the relationship between
c
-sorting and the weak order.

Lemma 2.4.47

(
[
Rea07b
, Lems.2.1 & 2.2]
)
.

Consider a Coxeter element of the form
c=s_{\ell}\cdot d
and let
w\in W
. Then

—

if
w=s_{\ell}\cdot u
with
\ell(w)=\ell(u)+1
, then
w(c)=s_{\ell}\cdot u(d\cdot s_{\ell})
,

—

otherwise,
w(c)=w(d\cdot s_{\ell})
.

Lemma 2.4.48
.

Fix a reduced expression of a Coxeter element
c
. Consider a
c
-sortable element
w\in W
and
s_{i},s_{j}\in S
two distinct generators appearing in the
c
-sorting word
w(c)
.

1.

if
s_{i}
appears before
s_{j}
in
c
, then
s_{i}
appears before
s_{j}
in
w(c)
,

2.

if
s_{j}
does not appear in
w(c)
between two occurrences of
s_{i}
, then it does not appear afterwards.

Démonstration.

We prove both statements separately.

1.

As both
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
appear in
w(c)
, it is immediate from the construction of
w(c)
from
c
.

2.

Notice that each generator can appear at most once in each factor
L
. Since
w
is
c
-sortable, the factors are weakly decreasing via inclusion. Thus, if
s_{j}
appeared after two occurrences of
s_{i}
, it would have to appear between them.
∎

Coxeter-sorting has a close relationship with lattice congruences of the weak order.

Proposition 2.4.49
.

Let
c
be a Coxeter element of a Coxeter group
W
.
The
c
-sortable elements are precisely the bottom elements of the congruence classes of the
c
-Cambrian congruence
\equiv_{c}
associated to the complete orientation of
G
where
s\to t
if
s
appears before
t
in
c
.

Proposition 2.4.50
.

The map
w\to ww_{0}
sends the lattice congruence
\equiv_{c}
to the lattice congruence
\equiv_{c^{-1}}
.

Proposition 2.4.51

(
[
Rea07a
, Thm.9.1]
)
.

The amount of
c
-sortable elements of a Coxeter group
W
of rank
n
is the
W
-Catalan number.

The
W
-Catalan numbers are presented in Table
2.1
.

Table 2.1:
The
W
-Catalan numbers.

Following Proposition
2.4.49
we can define a particular subset of
c
-sortable elements which help define polytopes whose oriented
1
-skeleton correspond to the
c
-Cambrian lattice obtained from the lattice congruence
\equiv_{c}
.

Definition 2.4.52

(
[
HLT11
]
)
.

Let
c
be a Coxeter element. A
c
-sortable element
w\in W
is said to be a

c
-singleton
if any of the following equivalent events occurs :

—

w
is the only element in its congruence class under
\equiv_{c}
,

—

w
has a reduced word that is a prefix of
w_{0}(c)
up to commutations,

—

w
is
c
-sortable and
ww_{0}
is
c^{-1}
-sortable.

Proposition 2.4.53
.

Given a Coxeter element
c
, the intersection of all defining half-spaces of
\PPerm_{n}
whose corresponding facet contains a
c
-singleton is a polytope whose oriented
1
-skeleton is the
c
-Cambrian lattice.

2.4.7 Permutree Perspectives

Having all tools in hand, we present the main problematic of this thesis. Following Remark
2.4.39
, one can define permutree congruences for any Coxeter type as follows.

Definition 2.4.54
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter system with its Coxeter diagram
G_{W}
. We call a
multi-orientation

\overrightarrow{G_{W}}
an endowing of each edge of
G_{W}
with either none, one, or both possible orientations. The
permutree congruence
associated to
\overrightarrow{G_{W}}
is the smallest lattice congruence that identifies the elements of the interval
[t,tsts\cdots]
(
m_{s,t}-1
letters) if
s\to t
.

As each orientation can have none, one, or two orientations, an identification of each edge with a coordinate gives an encoding of each permutree congruence by a decoration
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{|E(G_{W})|}
. That is, we have that

—

\delta_{st}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
if the edge
(s,t)
is not oriented,

—

\delta_{st}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}
if the edge
(s,t)
is oriented
s\to t
,

—

\delta_{st}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}
if the edge
(s,t)
is oriented
t\to s
,

—

\delta_{st}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}
if the edge
(s,t)
has both orientations.

Figure
2.27
shows an example for this in type
B
. In the cases of types
A,\,B,\,I,\,F
, and
H
these vectors can be simply indexed by
[n]
where
n=|S|
. In types
D
and
E
one must be careful as there are non-commuting braid relations between non-consecutive indices.

Figure 2.27:
A multi-orientation of the Coxeter graph of
B_{4}
and the
-permutree congruence it specifies. The minimal elements of the lattice congruence are in black and the congruence classes are shown in bolded blue.

Although Cambrian lattices are well understood in any finite type Coxeter group via their connections with finite type cluster algebras (see
[
FZ02
]
,
[
FZ03
]
,
[
PS13
]
,
[
PS15
]
), permutree lattices are well understood only in type
A

[
PP18
]
and partially understood in type
B

[
PPR22
]
.

Perspective 2.4.55
.

Are there other combinatorial families or methods through which we can study permutree lattices for any finite Coxeter group ?

2.5
s
-Decreasing Trees

In this section we present another generalization of permutations that we study in Part
III
. This section is based on
[
CP19
]
and
[
CP23
]
. Let
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
be a
weak-composition
(i.e. a vector with non-negative integer entries) and

|s|
=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}
.

2.5.1
s
-Weak Order

Definition 2.5.1
.

An

s
-decreasing tree
is a rooted plane tree (i.e. with a concrete embedding) on
n
internal vertices (called nodes), labeled by
[n]
, such that the node labeled
i
has
s_{i}+1
children and any descendant
j
of
i
satisfies
j<i
. We denote by

T_{0}^{i},\ldots,T_{s_{i}}^{i}
the subtrees of node
i
from left to right, and by

\mathcal{T}_{s}
the set of
s
-decreasing trees.

Figure 2.28:
A
(0,1,0,2)
-decreasing tree (left) and a
(1,2,1)
-decreasing tree (right).

Remark 2.5.2
.

Since the node
1
only has leaves as children, the first entry of
s
can be arbitrary and does not influence the combinatorics or geometry of
s
-decreasing trees. See Figure
2.28
for some examples.

By its construction the root of an
s
-decreasing tree is always the number of internal nodes. Moreover, their decreasing aspect allow for a direct calculation of their cardinality. That is, the number of
s
-trees is given by a generalization of the formula for factorials as follows.

Proposition 2.5.3
.

Given a weak composition
s
, the number of
s
-decreasing trees is

|\mathcal{T}_{s}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(1+s_{n-i+1}+s_{n-i+2}+\cdots+s_{n}\right).

Démonstration.

There is only one
(s_{n})
-decreasing tree with one node labeled
n
and
s_{n}+1
leaves. The next node
n-1
together with its
s_{n-1}+1
leaves can be placed in any of the
s_{n}+1
leaves from before. This results in
(1+s_{n})
possible
(s_{n-1},s_{n})
-decreasing trees. Continuing inductively on the length of
s
, we get that at step
k
the number of
(s_{k},\ldots,s_{n})
-decreasing trees with labels in
[k,n]
is

|\mathcal{T}_{s}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n-k}\bigl(1+\sum_{j=n-i+1}^{n}s_{j}\bigr)
.
We can now place the node labeled
k-1
in any of the
\bigl(1+\sum_{r=k}^{n}s_{r}\bigr)
leaves of any such tree. We finish when we place the node with label
1
. This gives the desired formula.
∎

Definition 2.5.4
.

Let
T
be an
s
-decreasing tree and
1\leq a<c\leq n
. We say that
a
is
left
(resp.
right
) of
c
if there is a node
d
such that
a<d
,
c<d
,
a\in T^{d}_{x}
, and
c\in T^{d}_{y}
where
x<y
(resp.
x>y
). We denote by

\inv(T)
the multiset of
inversions
of
T
formed by pairs
(c,a)
with multiplicity (also called cardinality)

\emph{\color[rgb]{0,0.445,0.695}|(c,a)_{T}|}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&\text{ if }a\text{ is left of }c,\\
i,&\text{ if }a\in T^{c}_{i},\\
s_{c},&\text{ if }a\text{ is right of }c.\end{array}\right.

Similar to Definition
2.1.14
and Proposition
2.4.20
, this allows to define an analogue of the weak order.

Definition 2.5.5
.

Let
R,T
be
s
-decreasing trees. We say that
R\trianglelefteq T
if we have that
\inv(R)\subseteq\inv(T)
. We call
(\mathcal{T}_{s},\trianglelefteq)
the

s
-weak order
.

Figure 2.29:
The
(1,2,2)
-weak order on
(1,2,2)
-decreasing trees. Figures based on
[
CP23
]
.

As before with Coxeter groups, the
s
-weak order enjoys several properties coming from the weak order of permutations. A key one for us being the lattice property which can be seen in Figure
2.29
.

Theorem 2.5.6

(
[
CP19
, Thms.3.2 & 3.3]
,
[
CP23
, Prop.1.35]
)
.

The
s
-weak order on
s
-decreasing trees is a polygonal lattice. The join of two
s
-decreasing trees
R
and
T
is the
s
-decreasing tree
R\vee T
that satisfies
\inv(R\vee T)=\big(\inv(R)\cup\inv(T)\big)^{tc}
.

Notice that the transitive closure requires attention since it is being done over multisets. We postpone the complete definition of the transitive closure for a particular case. Still, the following characterization of multisets of inversions for
s
-decreasing trees gives a hint about it.

Definition 2.5.7

(
[
CP23
, Def. 1.5, Prop. 1.6]
)
.

The multisets of inversions of
s
-decreasing trees are exactly the multisets
I
that satisfy :

—

Transitivity
: if
a<b<c
, then
|(b,a)_{T}|=0
or
|(c,a)_{T}|\geq|(c,b)_{T}|
.

—

Planarity
: if
a<b<c
, then
|(b,a)_{T}|=s_{b}
or
|(c,b)_{T}|\geq|(c,a)_{T}|
.

Definition 2.5.8
.

Let
T
be an
s
-decreasing tree. An
ascent
(resp.
descent
) of
T
is a pair
(a,c)
such that

1.

a\in T^{c}_{i}
for some
0\leq i<s_{c}
(resp.
0<i\leq s_{c}
),

2.

if
a<b<c
and
a\in T^{b}_{i}
, then
i=s_{b}
(resp.
i=0
),

3.

if
s_{a}>0
, then
T^{a}_{s_{a}}
(resp.
T^{a}_{0}
) is empty (i.e. only a leaf).

Notice that in the case where
s
has no zeros, the ascents (resp. descents) of
T
are in bijection with the leaves of
T
that are rightmost (resp. leftmost) of their parent, excepting the rightmost (resp. leftmost) leaf of
T
. The ascents and descents allow us to define the rotations on
s
-decreasing trees, which characterize the cover relations of the
s
-weak order.

Definition 2.5.9

(
[
CP23
]
)
.

Let
T
be an
s
-decreasing tree with an ascent
(a,c)
. The
rotation
of
T
along
(a,c)
is the
s
-decreasing tree
T+\{(a,c)\}
corresponding to the transitive closure multiset of inversions obtained from
\inv(T)
after increasing
|(a,c)|_{T}
by 1. If
A
is a subset of ascents of
T
, we denote by

T+A
the
s
-decreasing tree with inversion set
\big(\inv(T)+A\big)^{tc}
. See Figure
2.30
for an example of a rotation.

Proposition 2.5.10

(
[
CP23
, Thm.1.32]
)
.

The cover relations of the
s
-weak order correspond to rotations of
s
-decreasing trees along ascents.

Figure 2.30:
The rotation of the
(1,1,2,1,3,1,2)
-decreasing tree
T
along the ascent
(5,7)
giving
T^{\prime}
.

With rotations in hand we can define the analogue of the permutahedron for the
s
-weak order as a combinatorial complex as follows.

Definition 2.5.11
.

The

s
-permutahedron
is the combinatorial complex

\text{Perm}_{s}
with faces
(T,A)
where
T
is an
s
-decreasing tree and
A
is a subset of ascents of
T
. The face
(T,A)
is contained in
(T^{\prime},A^{\prime})
if and only if
[T,T+A]\subseteq[T^{\prime},A^{\prime}]
as intervals in the
s
-weak order.
In particular, the vertices of
\text{Perm}_{s}
are the
s
-decreasing trees and the edges correspond to the
s
-tree rotations.

Definition
2.5.11
portrays
\text{Perm}_{s}
as combinatorial complex. In the original article
[
CP19
]
the authors gave the following conjecture on the geometric structure of
\text{Perm}_{s}
.

Conjecture 2.5.12

(
[
CP19
, Conjecture 1]
)
.

Let
s
be a weak composition.
\text{Perm}_{s}
can be realized as a polyhedral subdivision of a polytope which is combinatorially isomorphic to the zonotope
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}s_{j}(\mathbf{e_{i}}-\mathbf{e_{j}})
.

In Chapter
6
we give a positive answer to this conjecture for when
s
is a composition.

2.5.2 Stirling
s
-Permutations

From here onwards
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
denotes a
composition
(i.e.
s_{i}>0
for all
i\in[n]
). This is required for us to study the
s
-weak order with objects more akin to permutations that we now define.

Definition 2.5.13
.

Let
s
be a composition. A
Stirling
s
-permutation
is a permutation of the word
1^{s_{1}}2^{s_{2}}\ldots n^{s_{n}}
that avoids the pattern
121
.
We denote by
\mathcal{W}_{s}
the set of all Stirling
s
-permutations.

Proposition 2.5.14
.

Stirling
s
-permutations are in bijection with the set of
s
-decreasing trees by reading the labels of the nodes of an
s
-decreasing tree in in-order. Moreover, this bijection induces a correspondence between leaves of an
s
-decreasing tree and prefixes of the corresponding Stirling
s
-permutation.

See Figure
2.31
for an example of the bijection between a Stirling
(2,1,1,2)
-permutations and a
(2,1,1,2)
-decreasing tree.

Figure 2.31:
A
(2,1,1,2)
-decreasing tree with vertices labeled via in-order. The corresponding Stirling
s
-permutation is
w=241143
.

Remark 2.5.15
.

Notice that in the case
s=(k,\ldots,k)
, Stirling
s
-permutations are exactly the Stirling
k
-permutations of
[
JKP11
]
. Since decreasing trees can be naturally transformed into increasing trees, the bijection of Proposition
2.5.14
recovers the classical bijection of Gessel between
(k+1)
-ary increasing trees and Stirling
k
-permutations in this case (see
[
CG19
,
\mathsection
5]
,
[
JKP11
]
, and
[
GS78b
]
).

To describe the covering relations of the
s
-weak order in terms of Stirling
s
-permutations we need the following.

Definition 2.5.16
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation.
For
a\in[n]
, the

a
-block
B_{a}
of
w
is the shortest substring
u
of
w
containing all
s_{a}
occurrences of
a
.

Remark 2.5.17
.

Some quick facts about
a
-blocks in a Stirling
s
-permutation include :

—

An
a
-block of
w
necessarily starts and ends with
a
and contains only letters in
[a]
.

—

For
a<b
we have that either
B_{a}\subset B_{b}
or
B_{a}\cap B_{b}=\emptyset
. Otherwise, a partial intersection would imply that
w
contains the pattern
121
.

—

For
a<c
,
w
contains the substring
ac
if and only if it is of the form
w=u_{1}B_{a}cu_{2}
where
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are words on
[n]
.

Following Figure
2.31
we have that
B_{1}=11
,
B_{2}=2
,
B_{3}=3
, and
B_{4}=4114
.

Definition 2.5.18
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation.
A pair
(a,c)
with
1\leq a<c\leq n
is an
ascent
(resp.
descent
) of
w
if
ac
(resp.
ca
) is a substring of
w
.

If
w
is of the form
w=u_{1}B_{a}cu_{2}
where
a<c
, the
transposition
of
w
along the ascent
(a,c)
is the Stirling
s
-permutation
u_{1}cB_{a}u_{2}
.
We denote by

\inv(w)
the multiset of inversions formed by pairs
(c,a)
with multiplicity

|(c,a)_{w}|
\in[0,s_{c}]
the number of occurrences of
c
that precede the
a
-block in
w
.

If
A
is a subset of ascents of
w
, we denote by

w+A
the Stirling
s
-permutation with inversion set
\big(\inv(w)+A\big)^{tc}
.

Lemma 2.5.19
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation,
T(w)
its corresponding
s
-decreasing tree and
1\leq a<c\leq n
.

1.

The pair
(a,c)
is an ascent (resp. descent) of
T(w)
if and only if it is an ascent (resp. descent) of
w
.

2.

|(c,a)_{T(w)}|=|(c,a)_{w}|
.

Moreover, suppose
(a,c)
is an ascent of
T=T(w)
so that
w
is the of the form
w=u_{1}B_{a}cu_{2}
.
Then
T^{\prime}
is the
s
-tree rotation of
T
along
(a,c)
if and only if
T^{\prime}=T(w^{\prime})
where
w^{\prime}=u_{1}cB_{a}u_{2}
.

Démonstration.

The proofs follow easily from the definitions and Proposition
2.5.14
.
∎

Corollary 2.5.20
.

Let
w
and
w^{\prime}
be Stirling
s
-permutations.
Then
w^{\prime}
covers
w
in the
s
-weak order if and only if
w^{\prime}
is the transposition of
w
along an ascent.

The analogue of Figure
2.29
showing the
s
-weak order on Stirling
s
-permutations can be found in Figure
2.32
.

Figure 2.32:
The
(1,2,2)
-weak order on Stirling
(1,2,2)
-permutations.

Example 2.5.21
.

Let
s=(1,1,2,1,3,1,2)
and consider the
s
-permutation
w=33725455716
.
The transposition of
w
along the ascent
(5,7)
switches the
5
-block
B_{5}=5455
of
w
with the
7
immediately after it and gives
w^{\prime}=3372{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}7}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5455}16
. The corresponding rotation in terms of
s
-decreasing trees is shown in Figure
2.30
.

This translation of the
s
-weak order on
s
-decreasing trees to Stirling
s
-permutations allows us to consider the
s
-permutahedron
\text{Perm}_{s}
as the combinatorial complex with faces
(w,A)
where
w
is a Stirling
s
-permutation and
A
is a subset of ascents of
w
. It is with this definition that we study Conjecture
2.5.12
in Chapter
6
.

We finish this section by stating some tools on the transitivity of multisets which are of help to us when working with the faces of the
s
-permutahedron. The following comes mostly from
[
GMP+23a
]
.

Definition 2.5.22

(
[
CP23
, Def.1.14]
)
.

Let
I
be a multiset of inversions. A
transitivity path
between two values
c>a
is a list of values
a=b_{1}<\cdots<b_{k}=c
such that
(b_{i+1},b_{i})_{I}>0
for all
i\in[k-1]
. In this way the transitive closure
I^{tc}
of
I
is the multiset of inversions with cardinalities

|(c,a)_{I^{tc}}|:=\max\big(|(c,b_{k-1})_{I}|\,:\,a=b_{1}<\cdots<b_{k}=c\text{ is a transitivity path of }(c,a).\big)

Definition 2.5.23

(
[
GMP+23a
]
)
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation,
A
a subset of ascents of
w
and
1\leq a<c\leq n
such that
|(c,a)_{w}|<s_{c}
. We say that the pair
(a,c)
is

A
-dependent
in
w
if there is a sequence
a\leq b_{1}<\cdots<b_{k}<b_{k+1}=c
such that :

—

b_{1}
is the greatest letter strictly smaller than
c
such that
B_{a}\subseteq B_{b_{1}}
,

—

for all
i\in[k-1]
, the
b_{i}
-block
B_{b_{i}}
is directly followed by
B_{b_{i+1}}
,

—

B_{b_{k}}
is directly followed by an occurrence of
c
,

—

(b_{i},b_{i})\in A
for all
i\in[k]
.

Example 2.5.24
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation and
A
a subset of ascents of
w
.

—

If
(a,c)\in A
, then
(a,c)
is
A
-dependent taking
b_{1}=a
and
b_{2}=c
.

—

Let
w=33725455716
and
A=\{(2,5),(5,7),(1,6)\}
. The pair
(2,7)
is
A
-dependent through the sequence
b_{1}=2
,
b_{2}=5
and
b_{3}=7
. Meanwhile,
(2,6)
is not
A
-dependent as the only block containing
B_{2}
is
B_{7}
but
7>6
and
B_{7}
is followed by
B_{1}
before the next occurrence of
6
.

Theorem 2.5.25

(
[
GMP+23a
, Prop.3.12]
)
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation and
A
a subset of its ascents. Then

\inv(w+A)=\begin{cases}|(c,a)_{w}|+1&\text{if\penalty\ $(a,c)$ is\penalty\ $A$-dependent in }w\\
|(c,a)_{w}|&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Example 2.5.26
.

Let
w=33725455716
and
A=\{(2,5),(5,7),(1,6)\}
. Augmenting these inversions gives the Stirling
s
-permutation
w+A=33775245561
. The pairs whose multiplicity in the multiset of inversion has been increased by
1
are
\{(5,2),(6,1),(7,2),(7,4),(7,5)\}
.

Démonstration.

Let be
I
the multiset of inversions defined as

|(c,a)_{I}|:=\begin{cases}|(c,a)_{w}|+1&\text{if\penalty\ $(a,c)$ is\penalty\ $A$-dependent in }w\\
|(c,a)_{w}|&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Notice that for an
A
-dependent pair
(a,c)
and an integer
d>c
we have that
|(d,c)_{I}|=|(d,a)_{I}|
. Moreover,
(a,d)
is
A
-dependent if and only if
(c,d)
is also
A
-dependent. We need to verify that
I
is transitive and that it is the smallest transitive multiset of inversions containing
\inv(w)+A
. We begin with the latter statement.

First, it is clear that
\inv(w)+A\subset I
from the definition of
I
as every pair in
A
is
A
-dependent. Now let us show that any transitive multiset of inversions
I^{\prime}
that contains
\inv(w)+A
necessarily contains
I
. Since
\inv(w)+A\subseteq I^{\prime}
is clear that for any pair we have
|(c,a)_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(c,a)_{w}|
.
Let
(a,c)
be an
A
dependent pair in
w
through a sequence
a\leq b_{1}<\cdots<b_{k}<b_{k+1}=c
. We proceed by induction on the length
k
of the sequence. For
k=1
we have that

—

either
b_{1}=a
and
(a,c)\in A
. Giving us that
|(c,a)_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(c,a)_{w}|+1
,

—

or
a<b_{1}<c
. In this case
|(b_{1},a)_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(b_{1},a)_{w}|>0
by since Definition
2.5.16

B_{a}\subset B_{b_{1}}
. Now we get that

|(c,a)_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(c,b_{1})_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(c,b_{1})_{w}|+1=|(c,a)_{w}|+1

(2.2)

where the first inequality comes from transitivity, the second from the previous case as
(b_{1},c)\in A
and the last equality from
(a,b_{1})
being
A
-dependent.

Suppose that
k>1
. The induction hypothesis tells us that
|(b_{k},a)_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(b_{k},a)_{w}|+1>0
. Applying transitivity to
a<b_{k}<c
and using that
(b_{k},c)\in A
and that
(a,b_{k})
is
A
-dependent (such that there is no occurrence of
c
between
a
and
b_{k}
) gives us the inequalities
|(c,a)_{I^{\prime}}|\geq|(c,b_{k})|_{I^{\prime}}\geq|(c,b_{k})_{w}|+1=|(c,a)_{w}|+1
. Thus,
I\subseteq I^{\prime}
.

Lastly let us see that
I
is transitive in the following cascade of cases. Let
1\leq a<b<c\leq n
.

—

If
|(b,a)_{I}|=0
then there is nothing to prove for this pair.

—

If
|(b,a)_{I}|>0
we need to see that
|(c,a)_{I}|\geq|(c,b)_{I}|
.

—

If
|(b,a)_{w}|=0
, then
(a,b)
is
A
-dependant and
|(c,a)_{I}|=|(c,b)_{I}|
by our observation at the start of the proof.

—

Suppose that
|(b,a)_{w}|>0
.

—

If
|(c,b)_{I}|=|(c,b)_{w}|
, then by the inclusion
\inv(w)\subset I
and the transitivity of
\inv(w)
we have that
|(c,a)_{I}|\geq|(c,a)_{w}|\geq|(c,b)_{w}|=|(c,b)_{I}|
.

—

Suppose that
|(c,b)_{I}|=|(c,b)_{w}|+1
(i.e.
(b,c)
is
A
-dependent). If
|(c,a)_{w}|\geq|(c,b)_{w}|+1
, we have
|(c,a)_{I}|\geq|(c,a)_{w}|\geq|(c,b)_{w}|+1=|(c,b)_{I}|
. Otherwise, we have
|(c,a)_{w}|=|(c,b)_{w}|=:i
. From our assumption that
|(b,a)_{w}|>0
we know that the
a
-block
B_{a}
appears in
w
between the first occurrence of
b
and the
i
-th occurrence of
c
. Thus,
(b,c)
being
A
-dependant implies that
(a,c)
is also
A
-dependant as the sequence of
(a,c)
is included in the sequence of
(b,c)
in
w
. These two
A
-dependencies together with the transitivity of
w
for
a<b<c
concludes that
|(c,a)|_{I}=|(c,a)|_{w}+1\geq|(c,b)|_{w}+1=|(c,b)|_{I}
as wished. ∎

partie II
Permutrees

Chapitre 3  Inversion and Cubic Vectors for Permutrees

In this chapter we present two generalizations of the bracket vectors of binary trees for permutrees based on
[
Tam23
]
. The first generalization which we call the inversion vectors helps prove in a constructive manner the lattice property for
\delta
-permutree rotation posets. The second generalization called the cubic vectors allows for the construction of an embedding of these lattices onto a cube.

3.1  Inversion Vectors

We begin defining inversion vectors for
\delta
-permutrees with the aim of proving the lattice property of
\delta
-permutrees rotation posets (Proposition
2.3.13
) in a constructive manner.

Recall that
j\to i
if
v_{j}
is a descendant of
v_{i}
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Consider
T\in\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)
to be a
\delta
-permutree. Its
inversion set
and
inversion components
are

\begin{split}B(T)&:=\{(i,j)\,:\,i<j\text{ and }j\to i\},\\
{B(T)}_{i}&:=\{j\in[n]\,:\,(i,j)\in B(T)\}.\end{split}

That is, all
j>i
such that
v_{j}
is a descendant of
v_{i}
. An inversion set has an associated
inversion vector

\vec{b}(T)=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n-1})
such that
b_{i}=|{B(T)}_{i}|
.

Since
RD_{n}=\emptyset
, its component does not alter the combinatorics of inversion sets and thus, we do not consider it. Figure
3.1
contains the inversion vectors for all
-permutrees.

Figure 3.1:
The rotation lattice of
-permutrees (left) with their respective inversion sets represented via their components (right). The elements in brown correspond to the permutrees that are not extremal.

Example 3.1.2
.

Let
\hat{1},\,\hat{0},\,T_{l},
and
T_{r}
respectively be the top, bottom, middle left, and middle right elements of the lattice of
-permutrees as in Figure
3.1
. Then

\begin{split}B(\hat{1})&=\{(1,2),(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4),(3,4)\},\\
B(\hat{0})&=\emptyset,\\
B(T_{r})&=\{(2,4),(3,4)\},\\
B(T_{l})&=\{(1,2),(3,4)\}.\end{split}

Lemma 3.1.3
.

Let
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
. The set of inversion sets
\{B(T)\,:\,T\in\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)\}
is the set of all subsets
E\subseteq\{(i,j)\in[n]^{2}\,:\,1\leq i<j\leq n\}
such that

1.

E
is transitive,

2.

E
is cotransitive,

3.

if
\delta_{j}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
,
(i,j)\notin E
, and
(j,k)\in E
, then
(i,k)\notin E
,

4.

if
\delta_{j}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
,
(i,j)\in E
, and
(j,k)\notin E
, then
(i,k)\notin E
.

Démonstration.

Let
T
be a
\delta
-permutree and
E:=B(T)
. If
(i,j),(j,k)\in E
, then
j\rightarrow i,\,k\rightarrow j
, evidently
k\rightarrow i
. That is,
(i,k)\in E
and
E
is transitive. The fact that
E
is cotransitive follows a similar argument. For property 3, the facts that
(i,j)\notin E
and
(j,k)\in E
respectively mean that
v_{i}\in LD_{j}
and
v_{k}\in RD_{j}
. Thus,
v_{k}
is not a child of
v_{i}
and
(i,k)\notin E
. Property 4 follows a similar argument.

For the opposite direction we wish to construct a
\delta
-permutree
T(E)
in accordance with the elements in
E
. Let
E_{i}=\{j\in[n]\,:\,(i,j)\in E\}
be the components of
E
. Notice that
E_{n}=\emptyset
and that if
(i,j)\in E_{i}
, and
(j,k)\in E_{j}
then
(i,k)\in E_{i}
due to
E
being transitive. With this in mind, we can construct
T(E)
in the following way. Take an
n\times n
grid. As step
0
, place vertex
v_{n}
anywhere in the last column. Now for step
i
, place the vertex
v_{n-i}
in the
n-i
-th column and at the height such that it is above (resp. below) all
j
such that
(n-i,j)\in E
(resp.
(n-i,j)\notin E
). If such height was already used by another vertex, move the placed vertices up or down as required maintaining the relative others established in the previous steps. After step
n-1
we get a permutation table. Decorate each vertex
v_{i}
with the decoration
\delta_{i}
. Following the insertion algorithm (see Definition
2.3.4
) we obtain a
\delta
-permutree
T(E)
. Notice that the placement of the vertices in the grid ensures transitivity and cotransitvity and that the red walls from the decorations in the insertion algorithm accomplish properties 3 and 4. See Figure
3.2
for an example.
∎

Figure 3.2:
The construction of the
-permutree corresponding to the inversion set
\{(1,2),(3,4),(3,6),(3,7),(4,6),(4,7),(5,6),(5,7),(6,7)\}
.

Remark 3.1.4
.

In the case of cover relations
T\lessdot T^{\prime}
between
\delta
-permutrees, that is, an
ij
-edge rotation from
T
to
T^{\prime}
, Definition
2.3.9
tells us that such a rotation only affects the edge between
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
while the rest of the tree remains the same. In terms of inversions this means that the rotation only turns into inversions the pairs of the form
(i,x)
where
x\in RD_{j}(T)
and if
\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
, also all the pairs that depend on these in a transitive manner. That is,
B(T^{\prime})=(B(T)\cup\{(i,j)\})^{tc}
no matter the decoration
\delta
.

Remark 3.1.5
.

The characterization of inversion sets was already given in
[
CPP19
, Section 2.3.2]
where they are called
IPIP
(
PIP
meaning permutree interval poset). With Lemma
3.1.3
, not only we have characterized inversion sets for permutrees but also described how to recover the permutree given its inversion set which is not done in
[
CPP19
]
.

To use inversion sets as a tool we need first to show that we can describe the
\delta
-permutree rotation order via their containment.

Lemma 3.1.6
.

Let
T,T^{\prime}
be two
\delta
-permutrees. Then
T<T^{\prime}
if and only if
B(T)\subset B(T^{\prime})
.

Démonstration.

Suppose that
T<T^{\prime}
. Since the rotation order on permutrees is the transitive closure of the covering relations given by the rotations in Figure
2.19
, it is enough to prove this in the case that
T^{\prime}
covers
T
. Remark
3.1.4
tells us that in such a case
B(T^{\prime})=(B(T)\cup\{(i,j)\})^{tc}
and thus
B(T)\subset B(T^{\prime})
.

Before moving to the other direction let
\hat{0}
be the minimal
\delta
-permutree in the rotation lattice. The fact that
T\lessdot T^{\prime}
implies that
B(T)={(B(T)\cup\{(i,j)\})}^{tc}
tells us that for any chain
\hat{0}=T_{0}\lessdot T_{1}\lessdot\cdots\lessdot T_{l}\lessdot T_{l+1}=T
in the interval
[\hat{0},T]
, we have that a
sequence
of inversions
(i_{x},j_{x})
such that
B(T_{x})={(B(T_{x-1})\cup\{(i_{x},j_{x})\})}^{tc}
for all
x\in[l]
. In this way we say that a sequence
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{l},j_{l})
generates
T
. It is easy to see that
T<T^{\prime}
if and only if for every sequence
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{l},j_{l})
that generates
T
there exists a sequence that generates
T^{\prime}
of the form
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{l},j_{l}),(i_{l+1},j_{l+1}),\ldots,(i_{l^{\prime}},j_{l^{\prime}})
. Take notice that the length of the chains in an interval of permutrees might not always be the same.

Now suppose that
B(T)\subset B(T^{\prime})
and let
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{l},j_{l})
be a sequence of
T
. We claim that for all
x\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
the sequence
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{x},j_{x})
is the start of sequence of
T^{\prime}
. Let
T_{x}
correspond to the
\delta
-permutree corresponding to the claim corresponding to
x
. Notice that the claim for
x=l
amounts to proving
T<T^{\prime}
. We proceed by induction on the length of the chain which is given by
x
. If
x=0
then
T_{0}=\hat{0}
and the claim is trivial. Now suppose that the claim holds for
x-1
and
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{x-1},j_{x-1})
is the start of a sequence of
T^{\prime}
, that is,
T_{x-1}<T
. Suppose as well that the
T_{x}\nless T^{\prime}
. Since
T_{x}<T^{\prime}
, this means that with the
i_{x}j_{j}
-edge rotation,
T_{x}
obtained an inversion that
T^{\prime}
does not possess. This is a contradiction since
B(T_{x})={(B(T_{x-1})\cup\{(i_{x},j_{x})\})}^{tc}\subset B(T)\subset B(T)
as all sets
B
are transitive. Thus,
T_{x}<T
for all
x\in\{0,\ldots,l\}
and
T<T^{\prime}
.
∎

Theorem 3.1.7
.

Given two
\delta
-permutrees
T,T^{\prime}
on
n
vertices, there exists a
\delta
-permutree
T\wedge T^{\prime}
under the
\delta
-permutree rotation order. Moreover, it satisfies

B(T\wedge T^{\prime})=B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})\cap\{(i,j)\,:\,\forall\,i<l<j,\,(i,l)\text{ or }(l,j)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})\}.

(3.1)

Example 3.1.8
.

Figure
3.3
presents the meet operation between
\delta
-permutrees. Taking the last set of the right-hand side of Equation (
) as
I
, we have that the corresponding inversion sets in this case are

\begin{split}B(T)&=\{(2,3),(2,4),(2,5),(3,4)\}\\
B(T^{\prime})&=\{(1,2),(1,4),(1,5),(2,4),(2,5),(3,4),(3,5),(4,5)\}\\
I&=\{(1,2),(1,3),(1,4),(2,3),(2,4),(3,4),(3,5),(4,5)\}\\
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})&=\{(2,4),(3,4)\}\end{split}

and satisfy Theorem
3.1.7
. Translating this into the components of the inversion sets we have that

\begin{split}\emptyset\cap\{2,4,5\}\cap\{2,3\}&=\emptyset\\
\{3,4,5\}\cap\{4,5\}\cap\{3,4\}&=\{4\}\end{split}

\begin{split}\{4\}\cap\{4,5\}\cap\{4,5\}&=\{4\}\\
\emptyset\cap\{5\}\cap\{5\}&=\emptyset\\
\end{split}

Figure 3.3:
The meet of two
-permutrees.

Remark 3.1.9
.

Notice that inversion sets of
\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}^{n}
-permutrees are bracket sets of binary trees as in Definition
2.2.4
. We recover Proposition
2.2.6
whenever
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}^{n}
as
B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
is contained in the last set of Equation (
). To see this, notice that if
(i,j)\in B(T)
then
v_{l}\in RD_{i}
for all
i<l<j
. If not, it would contradict that
\delta_{j}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}
. Thus,
(i,l)\in B(T)
. The same argument applies if
(i,j)\in B(T^{\prime})
and thus in this case
(i,j)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
implies
(i,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
giving us the desired inclusion.

Remark 3.1.10
.

Our meet operation is similar to the
tdd
operation defined in
[
CPP19
, Section 1.2.2]
on integer posets. In this context, Theorem
3.1.7
can be seen as
[
CPP19
, Corollary 2.39]
through the
tdd
and our Lemma
3.1.3
. Nevertheless, we give here a direct proof without relying on the results of
[
CPP19
]
.

We proceed to prove Theorem
3.1.7
.

Démonstration.

First let us see that
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
3.1.3
and thus defines
T\wedge T^{\prime}
as a
\delta
-permutree.

Assume that
(i,j),(j,k)\in B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
. As
(i,j),(j,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
, the transitivity of these sets tells us that
(i,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. We just need to see that
(i,k)
is also in the last set of Equation
. Let
l
such that
i<l<k
. If
i<l<j
, then as
(i,j)\in B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
we have that either
(i,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
or
(l,j)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. In the former case we are done. In the latter, as
(j,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
, using transitivity we get that
(l,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
, and we are done. If instead
l=j
, we immediately finish as by assumption
(i,j),(j,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. Finally, suppose that
j<l<k
. In this case since
(j,k)\in B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
, either
(j,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
or
(l,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. In the latter case we finish. For the former, as
(i,j)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
, using transitivity we get that
(i,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. Thus, we conclude that
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
is transitive.

To see that
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
is cotransitive notice that its complement is the transitive closure of
B(T)
and
B(T^{\prime})
. That is,
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})^{c}=(B(T)^{c}\cup B(T^{\prime})^{c})^{tc}
. By definition of transitive closure it is immediate that
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
is cotransitive.

Now suppose that
\delta_{j}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
,
(i,j)\notin B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
, and
(j,k)\in B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
. The last assumption tells us that
(j,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
and for all
l
such that
j<l<k
, either
(j,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
or
(l,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. On the other hand, that
(i,j)\notin B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
means that either
(i,j)\notin B(T),\,(i,j)\notin B(T^{\prime})
, or there exists
i<l^{*}<j
such that
(i,l^{*}),(l^{*},j)\notin B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. If either
(i,j)\notin B(T)
or
(i,j)\notin B(T^{\prime})
, then because of Property 3 of Lemma
3.1.3
and the fact that
(j,k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
we have that
(i,k)\notin B(T)
and
(i,k)\notin B(T^{\prime})
respectively. That is,
(i,k)\notin B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
and we are done in this case.

Consider then that
(i,j)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
and there exists
i<l^{*}<j
such that
(i,l^{*}),(l^{*},j)\notin B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. For contradiction’s sake suppose that
(i,j)\in B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
. By definition of
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
this means that either
(i,l^{*})\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
or
(l^{*},k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. The former case is a contradiction with the condition on which
l^{*}
exists, thus either
(i,l^{*})\in B(T)
or
(i,l^{*})\in B(T^{\prime})
and the latter case happens. Without loss of generality suppose that
(i,l^{*})\in B(T)
. As
(l^{*},k)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})\subset B(T)
, Property 3 of Lemma
3.1.3
tells us that
(i,k)\notin B(T)
and thus
(i,k)\notin B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
as we wanted. The final Property of Lemma
3.1.3
follows a similar proof, and thus we omit it. We conclude that
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
indeed corresponds to a permutree
T\wedge T^{\prime}
.

Let us now see that
T\wedge T^{\prime}
is in fact the meet of
T
and
T^{\prime}
. Since
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})\subset B(T)
and
B(T\wedge T^{\prime})\subset B(T^{\prime})
Lemma
3.1.6
tells us that
T\wedge T^{\prime}<T
and
T\wedge T^{\prime}<T^{\prime}
. Now suppose that there is a
\delta
-permutree
S
such that
S<T
and
S<T^{\prime}
. We claim that
S\leq T\wedge T
. Because of Lemma
3.1.6
we know that
B(S)\subset B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. Let
(i,j)\in B(S)\subset B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
and
i<l<j
. Notice that if both elements
(i,l),(l,j)\notin B(S)
, then
(i,j)\notin B(S)
as it is cotransitive, and we would have a contradiction. Without loss of generality suppose
(i,l)\in B(S)
. As
B(S)\subset B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
, we have that
(i,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. Thus, for all
i<l<j
either
(i,l)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
or
(l,j)\in B(T)\cap B(T^{\prime})
. Meaning that,
(i,j)\in B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
and we conclude that
B(S)\subseteq B(T\wedge T^{\prime})
. By Lemma
3.1.6
we get that
S\leq T\wedge T^{\prime}
as we wished.
∎

Corollary 3.1.11
.

\mathcal{PT}(\delta)
is a lattice for any decoration
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{n}
.

Démonstration.

The
\delta
-tree rotation poset has a meet thanks to Theorem
3.1.7
. Since it is a bounded poset, Proposition
1.1.12
tell us that it is a lattice.
∎

3.2  Cubic Vectors

Having inversion vectors in hand, the reader might ask if it is the case that inversion vectors also give a cubic embedding of
\delta
-permutree lattices. This is not the case as can be seen in Figure
3.4
.

Figure 3.4:
The rotation lattice of
-permutrees (left) together with its geometric realizations using inversion vectors (middle) and cubic vectors (right). Taking
T
to be the left
-permutree, we have
\vec{b}(T)=(1,0)
and
\vec{c}(T)=(2,0)
.

One can still manage to get such an embedding, it suffices to slightly relax the definition of our sets.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Consider
T\in\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)
to be a
\delta
-permutree. Its
cubic set
is

C(T):=\left\{(i,j)\,:\,\begin{array}[]{cc}i<j\text{ and }v_{j}\in D_{i}&\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\},\\
v_{j}\in RD_{i}&\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\},\end{array}\right\}

and its
cubic components
are
{C(T)}_{i}=\{j\in[n]\,:\,(i,j)\in C(T)\}
. A cubic set has an associated
cubic vector

\vec{c}(T)=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n-1})
such that
c_{i}=|{C(T)}_{i}|
.

Remark 3.2.2
.

Like in Remark
3.1.4
, we have that for a covering relation of
\delta
-permutrees
T\lessdot T^{\prime}
the respective cubic sets satisfy
C(T^{\prime})={(C(T)\cup\{(i,j)\})}^{tc}
. The key difference between the transitive closures of cubic vectors against inversion vectors is that the transitive closure turns into inversions the pairs of the form
(i,x)
where
x\in RD_{j}(T)
and nothing else. This is a consequence of the replacement of the condition
j\to i
in inversion sets to
j\in RD_{i}
(resp.
i<j
and
j\in D_{i}
) in cubic sets.

Definition 3.2.3
.

Let
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)
. We say that there is an edge between
\vec{c}(T)
and
\vec{c}(T^{\prime})
if and only if
T\lessdot T^{\prime}
. The convex hull of the cubic vectors together with this collection of edges is called the
cubical realization

\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
of
(\mathcal{PT}(\delta),\leq)
.

Example 3.2.4
.

If
\delta=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}
(resp.
\delta=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
), the cubic vector reduces to the bracket vector of binary trees (resp. to the Lehmer code of permutations), and we recover the cubic realization of the Tamari lattice in
[
Knu93
]
and
[
Com22
]
(resp. of the weak order of
[
BF71
]
and
[
RR02
]
). See Figure
3.5
for these cubic realizations and other examples.

Figure 3.5:
The cubical realization
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
of several permutreehedra.

We now enunciate several properties of cubical realization which culminate in showing that
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
is an embedding of
\PPT_{n}(\delta)
into the cube
Q_{n}=[0,n-1]\times\cdots\times[0,1]
.

Theorem 3.2.5
.

If
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)
are
\delta
-permutrees such that
T<T^{\prime}
in the
\delta
-permutree rotation lattice, then
\vec{c}(T)<_{lex}\vec{c}(T^{\prime})
and the edges of
C_{\delta}
have directions
\mathbf{e_{i}}
.

Démonstration.

We prove it for covering relations
T\lessdot T^{\prime}
as the
\delta
-rotation order is the transitive closure of the relations in Figure
2.19
. Following Remark
3.2.2
we have that the inclusion
C(T^{\prime})=(C(T)\cup\{(i,j)\})^{tc}
and the relations between components
C(T)_{i}\subset C(T^{\prime})_{i}
and
C(T)_{j}=C(T^{\prime})_{j}
if
j\neq i
. This tells us that
\vec{c}(T^{\prime})-\vec{c}(T)=(0,\ldots,0,c(T^{\prime})_{i}-c(T)_{i},0,\ldots,0)
. Therefore,
\vec{c}(T)\leq_{lex}\vec{c}(T^{\prime})
and the edge
[\vec{c}(T),\vec{c}(T^{\prime})]
has direction
\mathbf{e_{i}}
.
∎

Theorem 3.2.6
.

\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
is normal equivalent to
\PCube_{n-1}
(i.e. has the same normal fan).

Démonstration.

Let
Q_{n-1}:=[0,n-1]\times\cdots\times[0,1]
. First note that
0\leq c(T)_{i}\leq n-i
for all
i\in[n-1]
meaning that
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}\subset Q_{n-1}
. To see the reverse inclusion it is enough to prove that all vectors
\vec{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n-1})
where
r_{i}\in\{0,n-i\}
, have a preimage through the function
f:\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)\to\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
such that
f(T)=\vec{c}(T)
. We call such preimages
extremal

\delta
-permutrees.

Take any such
\vec{r}
. We now present how to construct a
\delta
-permutree in the preimage
f^{-1}(\vec{r})
. Consider an
n\times n
grid. At step
1
place
v_{1}
at
(1,1)
(resp.
(1,n)
) if
r_{1}=0
(resp.
r_{1}=n-1
). At step
i
place
v_{i}
at
(i,d)
(resp.
(i,n-u)
) where
d:=|\{j\in[n]\,\>j<i\text{ and }r_{j}=0\}|
(resp.
u:=|\{j\in[n]\,\>j<i\text{ and }r_{j}=n-j\}|
). After step
n-1
place
v_{n}
in the only coordinate of column
n
that shares no vertex horizontally. Thus, we get a permutation table. Decorate each vertex
v_{i}
with the decoration
\delta_{i}
. Following the insertion algorithm (see Definition
2.3.4
) we obtain a
\delta
-permutree
T(\vec{r})
.

Notice that in
T(\vec{r})
, for each vertex
v_{i}
we have either
|RD_{i}|=0
(resp.
|\{j\in[n]\,:\,i<j\text{ and }v_{j}\in D_{i}\}|=0
) or
|RD_{i}|=n-i
(resp.
|\{j\in[n]\,:\,i<j\text{ and }v_{j}\in D_{i}\}|=n-i
) That is, the values corresponding to
\vec{r}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}=Q_{n-1}
and normal equivalent to
\PCube_{n-1}
.
∎

Remark 3.2.7
.

Notice that since the interior of
Q_{n-1}
has no integer points, we have that all cubic coordinates are on the surface of
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
.

Figure
3.6
shows an example of the construction of extremal permutrees described in the proof of Theorem
3.2.6
. In Figure
3.1
the extremal
-permutrees are colored in black while the
2
that are extremal are colored in brown. We now show that these preimages are unique as a part of the following bigger result.

Figure 3.6:
The construction of the extremal
-permutree corresponding to the corner
(6,0,0,0,2,1)\in Q_{6}
.

Theorem 3.2.8
.

The map
f:\mathcal{PT}_{n}(\delta)\to\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
sending a
\delta
-permutree to its cubic vector is injective.

Démonstration.

Consider
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{PT}_{\delta}
two different
\delta
-permutrees. Due to them being different, there is a maximal vertex
i
such that
RD(T)_{i}\neq RD(T^{\prime})_{i}
(resp.
\{j\in[n]\,:\,i<j\text{ and }v_{j}\in D(T)_{i}\}\neq\{j\in[n]\,:\,i<j\text{ and }v_{j}\in D(T^{\prime})_{i}\}
). If
c(T)_{i}=|RD(T)_{i}|\neq|RD(T^{\prime})_{i}|=c(T^{\prime})_{i}
(or the equivalent in the
D_{i}
case) we are done. Otherwise, there exists a maximal vertex
r\in RD(T)_{i}\cap RD(T^{\prime})_{i}
such that
RD(T)_{r}\neq RD(T^{\prime})_{r}
which contradicts the existence of
i
. Therefore,
\vec{c}(T)\neq\vec{c}(T^{\prime})
.
∎

Theorem 3.2.9
.

\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
is an embedding of the
\delta
-permutreehedron. In particular, maximal cells of
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
are in bijection with facets of the
\delta
-permutreehedron.

Démonstration.

Recall from Proposition
2.3.19
that the facets of the
\delta
-permutreehedron are in bijection with the proper subsets
I\subsetneq[n]
such that there is a
\delta
-permutree that admits
(I\,\|\,{[n]}\setminus{I})
as an edge cut. Let
J:=[n]\setminus I
. As
\delta
-permutrees are connected, edge cuts partition a
\delta
-permutree
T
into a
\delta_{I}
-permutree
T_{I}
and a
\delta_{J}
-permutree
T_{J}
which as subtrees are connected only via an edge
(i,j)
such that
i\to j
and
v_{i}\in T_{i}
and
v_{j}\in T_{j}
.

Take an edge-cut
(I\,\|\,J)
. We proceed to construct a cell
K
of
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
containing all cubic vectors
\vec{c}(T)
of
\delta
-permutrees
T
that admit said edge-cut. Consider the minimal elements
\hat{0}_{\delta_{I}}
and
\hat{0}_{\delta_{J}}
(resp. maximal elements
\hat{1}_{\delta_{I}}
and
\hat{1}_{\delta_{J}}
). Connecting them via the insertion algorithm gives us the
(I\,\|\,J)
-admitting
\delta
-permutree
\underline{T}
given by
\underline{T}_{I}:=\hat{0}_{\delta_{I}}
and
\underline{T}_{J}:=\hat{0}_{\delta_{J}}
(resp.
\overline{T}
given by
\overline{T}_{I}:=\hat{1}_{\delta_{I}}
and
\overline{T}_{J}:=\hat{1}_{\delta_{J}}
). Notice that
\underline{T}
(resp.
\overline{T}
) is the minimal (resp. maximal)
\delta
-permutree that admits
(I\,\|\,J)
as an edge cut. This in turn shows that
\vec{c}(\underline{T})
(resp.
\vec{c}(\overline{T})
) is the lexicographical minimal (resp. maximal) cubic vector that relate with this edge-cut. Thus, we define our cell as
K:=\{\vec{c}(T)\in\mathcal{C}_{\delta}\,:\,\underline{T}\leq T\leq\overline{T}\}
.

Let us see that
K
is maximal by showing it is contained in a hyperplane. Suppose that
n\in J
. In such case, for any
\delta
-permutree
T
such that
\underline{T}\leq T\leq\overline{T}
we have that
RD(T)_{\max(I)}=\emptyset
(resp.
\{j\in[n]\,:\,\max(I)<j\text{ and }v_{j}\in D(T)_{\max(I)}\}=\emptyset
) and we conclude that get that
K
is in the hyperplane
x_{\max(I)}=0
. If instead
n\in I
, then we obtain that
K
is in the hyperplane
x_{\max(J)}=n-\max(J)
following a similar argument. Finally, note that all other
n-2
entries of the cubic vectors change between
\underline{T}
and
\overline{T}
through rotations between the vertices
I
or
J
. This together with Theorem
3.2.5
gives us that
K
is a maximal cell of
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
.

The conjunction of Theorems
3.2.5
,
3.2.6
, and
3.2.8
and our bijection between facets and cells gives us that
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
is an embedding of the
\delta
-permutreehedron.
∎

Chapitre 4  Permutree Sorting

In this chapter we present a way to study permutree congruences via the theory of automata. This chapter is based directly on the article
[
PPT23
]
.

Recall from Subsection
2.4.2
that the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
together with the set
S=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}
of simple reflections
s_{i}=(i\;\;i+1)
is a Coxeter system of Type
A
. In this way each permutation can be represented by a set of reduced words in the generators
S
. We begin by rephrasing
\delta
-permutrees in a notation that is more convenient for our purposes.

Definition 4.0.1
.

Let
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{n}
. We denote
U:=\{j\in[2,n-1]\,:\,\delta_{j}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}\}
and
D:=\{j\in[2,n-1]\,:\,\delta_{j}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}\}
. In this context and following Proposition
2.3.15
, we call

(U,D)
-permutree minimal
the minimal permutations of
\equiv_{\delta}
.

As a rephrasing, this chapter is dedicated to study how to discern the reduced words of
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutations.

Remark 4.0.2
.

Following Definition
2.4.54
, the sets
U
and
D
can also be defined as
j\in U
(resp.
j\in D
) if
j\to j-1
(resp.
j-1\to j
).

4.1  Single Automata

We begin by studying the case where
U=\emptyset
and
D=\{j\}
(resp.
U=\{j\}
and
D=\emptyset
) for some
j\in[2,n-1]
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Consider
U=\{j\}
(resp.
D=\{j\}
) for some
j\in[2,n-1]
and the set of generators
S
as an alphabet. We define the automaton

\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.

\mathbb{D}(j)
) recursively following Figure
4.1
with automata
\mathbb{U}(n)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(0)
) defined for consistency. As our automata are complete with each node having the
n-1
transitions labeled by adjacent transpositions
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}
, all missing transitions in our figures are meant to be loops. Figure
4.2
shows the complete automata
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
.

Figure 4.1:
The automata
\mathbb{U}(j)
(left) and
\mathbb{D}(j)
(right) defined recursively.

Figure 4.2:
The complete automata
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
.

Remark 4.1.2
.

As our automata are complete, it is immediate from their definition that the languages
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{U})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D})
are infinite. Thus, we narrow our scope and instead of asking what is the language recognized by them, we ask which reduced words are accepted (read from left to right) by them and in which states they are accepted. This leads us to the following definition.

Definition 4.1.3
.

We say that a state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) is
healthy
,
ill
, or
dead
, if it is respectively in the top, middle, bottom row of the automaton. The sequence of healthy states of
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) is called the
spine
the automaton.

Remark 4.1.4
.

One didactic way of seeing our automata is the following. Given an orientation
j\to j-1
(resp.
j-1\to j
), the spine of
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) can be built taking a walk on the Coxeter graph starting from the start of the orientation (i.e.
j
(resp.
j-1
)) to the end opposite to the orientation. Afterwards the other states can be built recursively following the pattern given in the Figure
4.1
.

With this definition our final/accepting states are partitioned into healthy or ill states while rejecting/non-final states are dead states. To explain why these states receive these names right now is a spoiler. First, we need to redefine slightly what it means to avoid a pattern in this chapter.

Definition 4.1.5
.

Fix a
j\in[2,n-1]
. We say that a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

avoids
the pattern
jki
(resp.
kij
) if for all
1\leq i<j
and
j<k\leq n
, the word
jki
(resp.
kij
) is not a subword of
\pi
.

Remark 4.1.6
.

We bring special attention to the reader to notice that since
j
is fixed in Definition
4.1.5
, this pattern avoidance is different from the usual as in Definition
2.1.7
.

Example 4.1.7
.

The permutation
42135
avoids
2ki
,
3ki
, and
4ki
(and thus the pattern
231
), but contains
ki3
(and thus the pattern
312
) since it contains the subsequence
423
.

4.1.1 Properties of
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)

Since our automata read reduced words from left to right, it is convenient to us to study how the event of
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) accepting or rejecting a reduced word changes when we multiply on the left said word by the transposition
s_{j}
,
s_{j-1}
, or
s_{i}
with
i\notin\{i,j-1\}
. The following Lemmas and Examples do this while studying in parallel how our fixed pattern avoidance changes under the same circumstances. The following proofs are written for the case of
\mathbb{U}(j)
and the pattern
jki
to ease reading. The proofs for the cases of
\mathbb{D}(j)
and the pattern
kij
are similar.

Lemma 4.1.8
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
\sigma([j-1])=[j-1]
,
\sigma(j)=j
, and
\sigma([n]\setminus{[j]})=[n]\setminus{[j]}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
\ell(\sigma\cdot\tau)=\ell(\sigma)+\ell(\tau)
, then :

1.

\tau
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) if and only if
\sigma\cdot\tau
posses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
),

2.

\tau
avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
) if and only if
\sigma\cdot\tau
avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
).

Démonstration.

We prove both events separately as follows.

1.

The constraints on
\sigma
imply that the transpositions
s_{j}
and
s_{j-1}
do not appear in any of its reduced words (see Remark
2.4.9
). Therefore, any reduced word of
\sigma
ends in the initial state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
, and it is trivially accepted. Thus, the accepting or rejecting of
\sigma\cdot\tau
depends uniquely on
\tau
. The result immediately follows.

2.

The constraints on
\sigma
show that the assumption
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
implies that
1\leq\sigma(i)<\sigma(j)=j<\sigma(k)\leq n
. Thus,
\tau
contains the pattern
jki
if and only if
\sigma\cdot\tau
contains the pattern
j\sigma(k)\sigma(i)
.
∎

Example 4.1.9
.

Consider
j:=4
and the permutations
\sigma:=312465=s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{5}
,
\tau_{1}:=143256=s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{3}
, and
\tau_{2}:=124536=s_{3}\cdot s_{4}
.
Multiplying we obtain
\sigma\cdot\tau_{1}=342165
and
\sigma\cdot\tau_{2}=314625
. Following the order of Lemma
4.1.8
we have that :

1.

\mathbb{U}(4)
accepts all reduced words of both
\tau_{1}
and
\sigma\cdot\tau_{1}
on its first ill state, and rejects all reduced words of both
\tau_{2}
and
\sigma\cdot\tau_{2}
on its first dead state,

2.

both
\tau_{1}
and
\sigma\cdot\tau_{1}
avoid
4ki
, while both
\tau_{2}
and
\sigma\cdot\tau_{2}
contain
4ki
.

Lemma 4.1.10
.

Let
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be a permutation with a reduced word starting with
s_{j-1}
(resp.
s_{j}
) that is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
), then

1.

\tau
does not permute
j
and
j+1
(resp.
j-1
and
j
),

2.

\tau
avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
).

Démonstration.

Consider a reduced word
w
starting with
s_{j-1}
and accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Since
w
starts with
s_{j-1}
and is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
, it is accepted in the first ill state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Such state contains only loops and the transitions
s_{j}
to a dead state. Since
w
does not arrive to said dead state, we know that
w
does not contain the transposition
s_{j}
. Following Remark
2.4.9
we know that no word of
w
contains
s_{j}
. We conclude our claims as follows :

1.

Since
s_{j}=(j\;\;j+1)
, not having
s_{j}
in
w
implies that
\tau
does not permute
j
and
j+1
.

2.

As
\tau
does not contain
s_{j}
in any reduced word,
\tau([j])=[j]
and
\tau([n+1]\setminus{[j]})=[n+1]\setminus{[j]}
. Thus,
\tau
avoids the subword
ki
with
i<j<k
and by consequence avoids as well
jki
.
∎

Example 4.1.11
.

Consider
j:=4
and
\tau:=413265
, with reduced word
s_{3}\cdot s_{5}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}
accepted by
\mathbb{U}(4)
. We get that

1.

\tau
does not permute
4
and
5
,

2.

\tau
avoids
4ki
.

Lemma 4.1.12
.

Let
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be a permutation that does not permute
j
and
j+1
(resp.
j-1
and
j
), then

1.

s_{j}\cdot\tau
(resp.
s_{j-1}\cdot\tau
) possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) if and only if
\tau
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j-1)
),

2.

s_{j}\cdot\tau
(resp.
s_{j-1}\cdot\tau
) avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
) if and only if
\tau
avoids
(j+1)ki
(resp.
ki(j-1)
).

Démonstration.

We deal with the two statements separately again :

1.

Consider
w
a reduced word for
\tau
accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
. Since
\tau
does not permute
j
and
j+1
, we know that
s_{j}
does not appear in
w
, and
s_{j}\cdot w
is a reduced word for
s_{j}\cdot\tau
. By construction
s_{j}\cdot w
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
.

Now suppose that
s_{j}\cdot\tau
possesses a reduced word
w
accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Notice that
w
cannot start by
s_{j-1}
due to Lemma
4.1.10
but contains
s_{j}
since
s_{j}\cdot\tau
permutes
j
and
j+1
. Using Lemma
4.1.8
we can also assume that
w
begins with
s_{j}
as any other possibilities yield only loops. Thus, after reading
s_{j}
, the corresponding accepted suffix is a reduced word for
\tau
that is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
.

2.

Notice that since
(j\;\;j+1)
is an inversion of
s_{j}\cdot\tau
but not of
\tau
, the value
j+1
cannot be used as
k
to form the pattern
jki
in
s_{j}\cdot\tau
and the value
j
cannot be used as
i
to form the pattern
(j+1)ki
in
\tau
. Since multiplying on the left by
s_{j}
only exchanges the values
j
and
j+1
the result follows.
∎

Example 4.1.13
.

Consider
j:=4
and the permutations
\tau_{1}:=142536
and
\tau_{2}:=142563
that do not permute
4
and
5
.
Multiplying we obtain
s_{4}\cdot\tau_{1}=152436
and
s_{4}\cdot\tau_{2}=152463
. In this scenario we have that

1.

the reduced word
s_{4}\cdot s_{3}\cdot s_{4}\cdot s_{2}
of
s_{4}\cdot\tau_{1}
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(4)
and the reduced word
s_{3}\cdot s_{4}\cdot s_{2}
of
\tau_{1}
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(5)
, while all reduced words of
s_{4}\cdot\tau_{2}
are rejected by
\mathbb{U}(4)
and all reduced words of
\tau_{2}
are rejected by
\mathbb{U}(5)
,

2.

s_{4}\cdot\tau_{1}
avoids
4ki
and
\tau_{1}
avoids
5ki
, while
s_{4}\cdot\tau_{2}
contains
463
and
\tau_{2}
contains
563
.

We now have in our hands the tools required to prove the hinted relationship between our automata and pattern avoidance.

Theorem 4.1.14
.

Fix
j\in[2,n-1]
and
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. The following statements are equivalent :

—

\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by the automaton
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
),

—

\pi
avoids the pattern
jki
(resp.
kij
) with
i<j<k
.

Démonstration.

We proceed by an induction on the length of the permutations. The base case is trivial due to the construction of our automata and the only permutation of length
0
being the identity
12\cdots n
.

Let
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that it has a reduced word
w
accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Supposing that
w
begins with
s_{i}
, let
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
\pi=s_{i}\cdot\tau
.

—

If
i\notin\{j-1,j\}
,
\tau
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
by Lemma
4.1.8
(1), so that
\tau
avoids
jki
by induction. Thus,
\pi=s_{i}\cdot\tau
avoids
jki
by Lemma
4.1.8
(2).

—

If
i=j-1
, then
\pi
avoids
jki
by Lemma
4.1.10
(2).

—

If
i=j
, then
\tau
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
by Lemma
4.1.12
(1). We obtain by induction that
\tau
avoids
(j+1)ki
. Thus,
\pi=s_{j}\cdot\tau
avoids
jki
by Lemma
4.1.12
(2).

No matter the case, we see that
\pi
avoids
jki
.

Now for the reverse direction let
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be a permutation avoiding the pattern
jki
. We get the following two cases.

—

There exists
m
minimal such that
j<m
and
\pi^{-1}(j)>\pi^{-1}(m)
. In such a case,
\pi^{-1}(l)>\pi^{-1}(m)
for all
l\in[j,m-1]
. Following Lemma
2.4.24
,
\pi
possesses a reduced word starting with
s_{m-1}s_{m-2}\cdots s_{j+1}s_{j}
. Let
\sigma=s_{m-1}s_{m-2}\cdots s_{j+1}
giving us the factorization
\pi=\sigma\cdot s_{j}\cdot\tau
for some
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Using Lemma
4.1.8
(2) and then Lemma
4.1.12
(2) we obtain that
\tau
avoids
(j+1)ki
. By induction, we obtain that it possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
. By Lemmas
4.1.8
(1) and
4.1.12
(1), we conclude that
\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
.

—

For all
m\in[j+1,n]
we have that and
\pi(j)<\pi(m)
. Let
w
be a reduced word of
\pi
which by the previous sentence Lemma
2.4.24
cannot begin by
s_{m}
for
m\in[j+1,n]
. If it is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
, we are done. Otherwise,
w
is rejected and up to commutations with transpositions
s_{c}
with
c\in[1,j-2]
we have that
w
starts with
s_{j-1}
and then followed by
s_{j}
. Call
i
and
k
the two elements that are exchanged when the reduced word first uses
s_{j}
. We have that
i<j<k
and
\pi
contains the pattern
jki
(because
j
and
k
are not exchanged in
\pi
, and
i
and
k
are already exchanged, so they remain exchanged in
\pi
). This is a contradiction with our assumption that
\pi
avoids
jki
.

Thus, in any case we get that
\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
.
∎

4.1.2 The Structure of Accepted Reduced Words of
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)

Via Proposition
2.3.15
, Theorem
4.1.14
allows us to distinguish when a permutation is minimal in its permutree class for a congruence given through a single orientation of the Coxeter graph. Still, as the number of reduced word of permutations grows very quickly (for the longest word
w_{0}
there are
\frac{\binom{n}{2}!}{\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}(2k-1)^{n-k}}
reduced expressions
[
OEI23
, A005118]
), trying all reduced words on our automata in hopes of finding one that is accepted is not practical.
