Therefore, we now move to present certain properties of the set of accepted reduced words of our automata with the aim of describing how to efficiently propose a single candidate reduced word in the context of Theorem
4.1.14
.

Remark 4.1.15
.

Given the structure of our automata, a permutation may possess reduced words
w,w^{\prime}
such that
w
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
while
w^{\prime}
is rejected by
\mathbb{U}(j)
. For example consider
\pi=321
with reduced words
w=s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
and
w^{\prime}=s_{1}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}
. Then
w
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(2)
while
w^{\prime}
is rejected by
\mathbb{U}(2)
.

However, we do have the following nice properties on the set of reduced words.

Theorem 4.1.16
.

The set of reduced words accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) is closed by taking prefixes.

Démonstration.

This follows from the fact that any path to an accepting state in
\mathbb{U}(j)
begins in the initial state and contains only accepting states. Thus, any prefix of a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
is also accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Since all prefixes of a reduced word are also reduced words, we obtain the result.
∎

Theorem 4.1.17
.

Let
\ell\in[n-1]
be distinct from
j-1
(resp.
j
).
A permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
that avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
) and reverses
\ell
and
\ell+1
possesses a reduced word starting with
s_{\ell}
and accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
).

Démonstration.

Since
\pi
reverses
\ell
and
\ell+1
, it possesses a reduced word of the form
\pi=s_{\ell}\cdot\tau
(see Lemma
2.4.24
). To finish proving the proposition we have the following cases :

—

if
\ell=j
, then
\tau
does not reverse
j
and
j+1
and thus avoids
(j+1)ki
by Lemma
4.1.12
(2). Hence,
\tau
has a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
by Theorem
4.1.14
, and we conclude by Lemma
4.1.12
(1).

—

if
\ell\notin\{j-1,j\}
. In this case
\tau
avoids
jki
by Lemma
4.1.8
(2) and thus,
\tau
has a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
by Theorem
4.1.14
. We conclude by Lemma
4.1.8
(1).
∎

Theorem 4.1.18
.

Given a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, all the reduced words for
\pi
accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) are accepted at the same state.

It is possible to give a direct proof of this theorem by verifying that commutations and braid moves between accepted reduced words do not change the state on which the reduced words are accepted. One can also classify which expressions of reduced words are accepted at each state. We prefer going for the following stronger result which characterizes where the reduced words of a permutation land depending on its inversions. For simplicity, we state it in terms of
\mathbb{U}(j)
, although a similar statement holds for the automaton
\mathbb{D}(j)
.

Theorem 4.1.19
.

Given a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, partition the inversions of
\pi
as into the following two sets :

\begin{split}\inv^{j}(\pi)&=\{(i,j)\,:\,i<j\text{ and }\pi^{-1}(i)>\pi^{-1}(j)\}\\
\inv_{j}(\pi)&=\{(j,k)\,:\,j<k\text{ and }\pi^{-1}(j)>\pi^{-1}(k)\}\end{split}

Then we have the following properties :

1.

if
|\inv^{j}(\pi)|=0
, then all reduced words for
\pi
end at the same healthy state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
,

2.

if
|\inv_{j}(\pi)|=0
, then all reduced words for
\pi
end at the same state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
. The state is healthy if
\pi
avoids
ji
, ill if
\pi
contains
ji
but avoids
jki
, or dead if
\pi
contains
jki
,

3.

if
|\inv^{j}(\pi)|\neq 0\neq|\inv_{j}(\pi)|
, all accepted reduced words for
\pi
end at the same ill state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
while the rejected reduced words may end at distinct dead states of
\mathbb{U}(j)
.

Démonstration.

We proceed by induction on the length of a permutation. Notice that the base case is trivial as the identity has no reduced words and corresponds to our initial state which is always accepting. Consider an arbitrary reduced word
w
for
\pi
. Letting it begin by
s_{l}
let
w=s_{l}\cdot w^{\prime}
and
\pi=s_{l}\cdot\tau
where
w^{\prime}
is a reduced word of
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Depending on the value of
l
we have the following cases :

—

if
\ell\notin\{j-1,j\}
, then
s_{\ell}
is a loop in
\mathbb{U}(j)
giving us the equalities
|\inv^{j}(\pi)|=|\inv^{j}(\tau)|
and
|\inv_{j}(\pi)|=|\inv_{j}(\tau)|
,

—

if
\ell=j
, then
s_{j}
goes to the initial state of
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
, and we have that
|\inv^{j}(\pi)|=|\inv^{j+1}(\tau)|
and
{|\inv_{j}(\pi)|=|\inv_{j+1}(\tau)|+1}
,

—

if
\ell=j-1
, then
s_{j-1}
goes to the first ill state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
|\inv^{j}(\pi)|=|\inv^{j+1}(\tau)|+1
and
|\inv_{j}(\pi)|=|\inv_{j+1}(\tau)|
.

By induction, we obtain that the reduced word
w^{\prime}
for
\tau
ends as predicted in the statement.
The previous observations ensure that the reduced word
w
for
\pi
also does.
∎

Example 4.1.20
.

We provide examples for each of the three cases of Theorem
4.1.19
.

1.

For
\pi:=4312
, we have that
|\inv^{2}(\pi)|=0
and all of its
5
reduced words end at the third healthy state of
\mathbb{U}(2)
.

2.

For
\pi:=32145
(resp.
\pi:=43215
, resp.
\pi:=43251
), we have
|\inv_{4}(\pi)|=0
and all its
2
(resp.
16
, resp.
35
) reduced words end at the first healthy (resp. ill, resp. dead) state of
\mathbb{U}(4)
.

3.

For
\pi:=4321
, we have
|\inv^{2}(\pi)|=|\{(1,2)\}|=1
and
|\inv_{2}(\pi)|=|\{(2,3),(2,4)\}|=2
. Among the
16
reduced words of
\pi
, the automaton
\mathbb{U}(2)
accepts
7
at its third ill state, rejects
7
at its first dead state, and rejects the other
2
at its second dead state.

Notice that Theorem
4.1.17
allows us to algorithmically construct a candidate reduced word to verify if a permutation
\pi
is
(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree minimal. This would be done by accumulating transpositions
s_{\ell}
depending on if the values
\ell
and
\ell+1
are permuted and verifying that the resulting reduced word is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
.

Here we go for a sorting approach as in
[
Knu73
]
, meaning that we construct a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
which is a reduced word of
\pi
if and only if
\pi
avoids the pattern
jki
. We call the following algorithm the

(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting
.

Input :
a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and an integer
j\in[n]

Output :
a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
, candidate reduced word for
\pi

w:=\varepsilon

1

repeat

2

if

\exists\;\ell\neq j-1
such that
\ell
and
\ell+1
are reversed in
\pi

then

3

\pi:=s_{\ell}\cdot\pi
,
w:=w\cdot s_{\ell}

4

if

\ell=j

then

j:=j+1

5

6

7

if

j-1
and
j
are reversed in
\pi

then

8

\pi:=s_{j-1}\cdot\pi
,
w:=w\cdot s_{j-1}

9

w:=w\cdot w^{\prime}\cdot w^{\prime\prime}
where
w^{\prime}
sorts
\pi_{[j]}
and
w^{\prime\prime}
sorts
\pi_{[n]\setminus{[j]}}

10

11

return

w

Algorithm 1

(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting

Example 4.1.21
.

Table
4.1
shows the
(\{2\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting algorithm in action for the permutations
\pi_{1}:=3421
and
\pi_{2}:=4231
. Each row contains the permutation
\pi_{i}
, the reduced word
w_{i}
, and the values of
j_{i}
and
\ell_{i}
in use at each step of the algorithm.

Notice that for
\pi_{1}
the algorithm ends with the identity and is thus
(\{2\},\emptyset)
-sortable, which coincides with the fact that it avoids
2ki
. In contrast, for
\pi_{2}
the algorithm ends with the permutation
1243
, meaning that
\pi_{2}
is not
(\{2\},\emptyset)
-sortable, which coincides with the fact that
\pi_{2}
contains
2ki
.

Table 4.1:
The
(\{2\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting of
3421
(left) and
4231
(right).

Corollary 4.1.22
.

For any permutation
\pi
and
j\in[2,n-1]
, Algorithm
1
returns a reduced word
w
accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
with the property that
w
is a reduced word for
\pi
if and only if
\pi
avoids
jki
.

Démonstration.

First notice that Algorithm
1
creates a reduced word following
\mathbb{U}(j)
. It begins prioritizing healthy states in lines 2 to 5 by considering transpositions that are loops following Lemma
4.1.8
and then changing
j
to
j+1
following Lemma
4.1.12
. This repeats for as many transitions as possible until we have to consider
s_{j-1}
and go to an ill state in line 6. At this point we cannot use
s_{j}
as this would lead to an ill state. After this point, we can use as many transitions in
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{j-1}\}
(resp.
\{s_{j+1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}
) to sort
[j]
(resp.
[n]\setminus{[j]}
).

The resulting reduced word is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
as we never use
s_{j}
after an ill state. If it happens that
w
is a reduced word of
\pi
, then by Theorem
4.1.14

\pi
avoids
jki
. Conversely, if
\pi
avoids
jki
, by Theorem
4.1.17
we have that
w
started being constructed with the respective transpositions
s_{l}
and lines 2 to 5 of the algorithm, and then forced to end in lines 6 to 9 in an expression unique up to commutations. Thus, it is a reduced word of
\pi
.
∎

Remark 4.1.23
.

Notice it is line 8 of Algorithm
1
which makes it a sorting algorithm as in
[
Knu73
]
. Indeed,
w
becomes a reduced word for
\pi
at the end of the algorithm if and only if
\pi
becomes the identity permutation. We say that
\pi
is then
(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree sortable. If one does not want to go so far one can just verify at line 8 if
\pi([j])=[j]
and
\pi([n]\setminus{[j]})=[n]\setminus{[j]}
.

4.1.3 Generating Trees

Similar to how Theorem
4.1.17
has an algorithmic consequence, Theorem
4.1.16
and Theorem
4.1.18
have a combinatorial consequence. Namely, they define generating trees for
(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree minimal permutations as subgraphs of the Hasse diagram of the weak order on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. To construct them without ambiguity let
\prec
be a total order on
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}
which we call a
priority order
on adjacent transpositions. Given a
(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree minimal permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, let

\pi(\{j\},\emptyset,\prec)
be the
\prec
-lexicographic minimal reduced word for
\pi
that is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
and

\mathcal{R}(n,\{j\},\emptyset,\prec):=\big\{\pi(\{j\},\emptyset,\prec)\,:\,\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ is }(\{j\},\emptyset)\text{-permutree minimal}\big\}.

Theorem 4.1.24
.

The set
\mathcal{R}(n,\{j\},\emptyset,\prec)
is closed by taking prefixes.

Démonstration.

Consider a reduced word
w=u\cdot v
where
u\notin\mathcal{R}(n,\{j\},\emptyset,\prec)
.
If
u
is rejected by
\mathbb{U}(j)
, then
w
is rejected as well due to Theorem
4.1.16
. Otherwise, there exists a reduced word
u^{\prime}
representing the same permutation as
u
, accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
such that it is
\prec
-lexicographic smaller than
u
.
Due to Theorem
4.1.18
,
u
and
u^{\prime}
end at the same state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
and thus if
w=u\cdot v
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
, so is
u^{\prime}\cdot v
. Since
u^{\prime}\cdot v
is
\prec
-lexicographically smaller than
u\cdot v
and represents the same permutation, we have that
w
is not in
\mathcal{R}(n,\{j\},\emptyset,\prec)
.
∎

Theorem
4.1.24
gives us a generating tree where the root is the empty reduced word and the parent of a reduced word is obtained by deleting the last letter. Taking this tree as the sequence of associated transpositions in the weak order, we obtain a generating tree on
(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree minimal permutations as a sublattice of the weak order on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Figure
4.3
shows all possible trees for
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
and priority order
s_{1}\prec s_{2}\prec s_{3}
.

Figure 4.3:
Generating trees for the
(\{j\},\emptyset)
and
(\emptyset,\{j\})
-permutree minimal permutations of
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
, with priority order
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s_{1}}\prec{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s_{2}}\prec{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}s_{3}}
.

4.2  Multiple Automata

We now move on to the case where
U
and
D
are arbitrary subsets of
[2,n-1]
. In this context Theorem
4.1.14
tells us that the following are equivalent statements for a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
:

1.

\pi
is a
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutation,

2.

letting
i<j<k
,
\pi
avoids the pattern
jki
(resp.
kij
) if
j\in U
(resp.
j\in D
),

3.

for each
j\in U
(resp.
j\in D
)
\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
).

Statements (1) and (2) happen simultaneously while statement (3) gives a reduced word for each element of
U
and
D
. Unfortunately the reduced word that is accepted by an automaton might be rejected by another. Such is the case of the following example.

Example 4.2.1
.

Let us revisit Remark
4.1.15
in more generality. Let
j\in[2,n-1]
and
U=D=\{j\}
. Consider the permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with
s_{j}\cdot s_{j-1}\cdot s_{j}
and
s_{j-1}\cdot s_{j}\cdot s_{j-1}
as its only reduced words. Notice that
s_{j}\cdot s_{j-1}\cdot s_{j}
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
and rejected by
\mathbb{D}(j)
while
s_{j-1}\cdot s_{j}\cdot s_{j-1}
is accepted by
\mathbb{D}(j)
and rejected by
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Thus,
\pi
has no reduced word accepted by both automata.

This gives us the following remark.

Remark 4.2.2
.

If
U
and
D
are not disjoint, there exists permutations who do not possess a reduced word accepted by all the respective automata
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
.

Hope is not lost though, as this is not the case when
U
and
D
are not disjoint as the following theorem shows.

Theorem 4.2.3
.

Let
U
and
D
be disjoint subsets of
[2,n-1]
and
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. The following statements are equivalent :

—

\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D
.

—

\pi
avoids the patterns
jki
for
j\in U
and
kij
for
j\in D
with
i<j<k
.

Démonstration.

If
\pi
possess a reduced word accepted at the same time by all
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D
, Theorem
4.1.14
gives us our desired pattern avoidance. For the opposite direction consider
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
\pi
avoids the patterns
jki
for
j\in U
and
kij
and
j\in D
with
i<j<k
. Let
U^{\prime}=\{j\in U\,:\,\inv_{j}(\pi)\neq\emptyset\}
and
D^{\prime}=\{j\in D\,:\,\inv^{j}(\pi)\neq\emptyset\}
. Using Theorem
4.1.19
we have that any reduced word of
\pi
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U\setminus{U^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D\setminus{D^{\prime}}
. Since these cases are trivial we can assume without loss of generality that
U=U^{\prime}
,
D=D^{\prime}
and one of the two is not empty. Say
U=U^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

The following is an adaptation of the proof of Theorem
4.1.14
by induction. Let
j_{\circ}:=\max(U)
and
m
minimal such that
j_{\circ}<m
and
\pi^{-1}(j_{\circ})>\pi^{-1}(m)
. By said minimality we have that
\pi^{-1}(l)>\pi^{-1}(m)
for all
l\in[j,m-1]
. Therefore,
\pi
contains the corresponding subwords
mj_{\circ}l
and
l\notin D
for
l\in[j+1,m-1]
. Notice as well that
l\notin U
by maximality of
j_{0}
. Now, due to Lemma
2.4.24
the minimality of
m
tells us that
\pi
can be written as
\pi=s_{m-1}s_{m-2}\cdots s_{j_{\circ}}\cdot\tau
.

Lemma
4.1.8
(2) and Lemma
4.1.12
(2) give us that

—

\tau
avoids
jki
for all
j\in U\setminus{\{j_{\circ}\}}
and
kij
for all
j\in D\setminus{\{m\}}
,

—

\tau
avoids
(j_{\circ}+1)ki
,

—

\tau
avoids
kij_{\circ}
if
m\in D
.

By our induction step, we have that
\tau
possesses a reduced word
w
that is accepted by all
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) for
j\in U\setminus{\{j_{\circ}\}}
and
j=j_{\circ}+1
(resp.
j\in D\setminus{\{m\}}
and
j=j_{\circ}
if
m\in D
). Lemma
4.1.8
(1) and Lemma
4.1.12
(1) give us that
s_{m-1}\cdots s_{j_{\circ}}\cdot w
is a reduced word for
\pi
accepted by all
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D
as we wished.
∎

4.2.1 Multiplying Automata

The current statement of Theorem
4.2.3
uses an automaton per element of
U
and
D
. We can however, use the product of all of them to rephrase the theorem in a more compact way. See Definition
1.3.4
for the details on the product of automata. We denote by

\mathbb{P}(U,D)
the automaton resulting from the product of all automata
\mathbb{U}(j)
if
j\in U
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
if
j\in D
.

Corollary 4.2.4
.

Let
U
and
D
be disjoint subsets of
[2,n-1]
. The following statements are equivalent :

—

\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
,

—

\pi
avoids the patterns
jki
for
j\in U
and
kij

j\in D
with
i<j<k
.

Definition 4.2.5
.

Following Definition
4.1.3
we say that a state of
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
is
healthy
if its corresponding states in all
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U
and all
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D
are healthy. We say that a state of
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
is
ill
if its corresponding sets have at least one ill (resp. dead) state and no dead states. A state of
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
is said to be
dead
if it contains at least one dead state.

Figure
4.4
illustrates the automata
\mathbb{P}(\{4\},\{2\})
when
n=5
(left),
\mathbb{P}(\{3\},\{2\})
for
n=4
(middle), and
\mathbb{P}(\{2\},\{4\})
for
n=5
(right).
For the first two automata, we draw the complete automata on top, and their skeletons on the bottom.
Here, we call
skeleton
a simplification of the automaton that recognizes the same reduced words.
It is obtained using the fact that the word is rejected as soon as we reach a dead state, and that the automata
\mathbb{U}(n)
and
\mathbb{D}(1)
accept all reduced words.
For the last automaton, the complete product is too big, so we only draw the reachable healthy states.
We color the transitions in red, blue, or purple depending on whether only
\mathbb{U}
, only
\mathbb{D}
, or both
\mathbb{U}
and
\mathbb{D}
change state.

Figure 4.4:
The automaton
\mathbb{P}(\{4\},\{2\})
for
n=5
and its skeleton (left), the automaton
\mathbb{P}(\{3\},\{2\})
for
n=4
and its skeleton (middle), and the healthy states of the automaton
\mathbb{P}(\{2\},\{4\})
for
n=5
(right).

4.2.2 The Structure of Accepted Reduced Words of
\mathbb{P}(j)

Following Section
4.1.2
, we can extend Theorems
4.1.16
,
4.1.17
, and
4.1.18
to
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
as follows.

Theorem 4.2.6
.

The set of reduced words accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
is closed by taking prefixes.

Theorem 4.2.7
.

A permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
that avoids
jki
for
j\in U
and
kij
for
j\in D
and possesses a reduced word starting with
s_{\ell}
such that the transposition
s_{l}
leads to a healthy state of
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
, possesses a reduced word starting with
s_{\ell}
and accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
.

Theorem 4.2.8
.

Given a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, all the reduced words for
\pi
accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
are accepted at the same state.

The proofs of these theorems are left out as they are obtained directly from their corresponding single automata versions and the definition of the product of automata.

4.2.3 Permutree Sorting

Corollary
4.2.4
tells us that the set of
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutations having reduced words accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
is non-empty in the case of
U,D
being disjoint subsets of
[2,n-1]
. With this in hand, following the intuition developed in Subsection
4.1.2
and taking into account that the accepted reduced words are closed by prefix as in Theorem
4.2.6
, Theorem
4.2.7
allows us to think of sorting algorithms following only
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutations.

Definition 4.2.9
.

A

(U,D)
-permutree sorting algorithm
is a sorting algorithm such that

—

applied to a
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutation
\pi
, it only passes through
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutations and ends with the identity permutation,

—

it fails to sort a non
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutation
\pi
.

Example 4.2.10
.

The stack sorting algorithm
[
Knu73
]
is a
(\{2,\dots,n-1\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting algorithm.

Clearly, we recover Algorithm
1
as a
(\{j\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting algorithm for any
j\in[2,n-1]
. We now generalize it to a
(U,D)
-permutree sorting algorithm in a recursive way. As before, the algorithm follows the automaton
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
without explicitly constructing it. Since
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
uses transitions composed by tuples of transpositions, we use the following notation

\moveU(U,\ell)=\begin{cases}U&\text{ if }\ell\notin U,\\
(U\setminus{\{\ell\}})\cup\{\ell+1\}&\text{ if }\ell\in U,\end{cases}

\hskip 21.05519pt\moveD(D,\ell)=\begin{cases}D&\text{ if }\ell+1\notin D,\\
(D\setminus{\{\ell+1\}})\cup\{\ell\}&\text{ if }\ell+1\in D.\end{cases}

Function

permutreeSort
(\pi,U,D)

1

Input :
a permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and two disjoint subsets
U
and
D
of
[n]

Output :
a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
, candidate reduced word for
\pi

if

\exists\;\ell\in[n-1]
such that
\ell
and
\ell+1
are reversed in
\pi
, and
\ell+1\notin U
and
\ell\notin D

then

2

return

s_{\ell}\cdot\text{permutreeSort}(s_{\ell}\cdot\pi,\;\moveU(U,\ell),\;\moveD(D,\ell))

3

4

if

\exists\;\ell\in[n-1]
such that
\ell
and
\ell+1
are reversed in
\pi
, and

(
\ell+1\in U
and
\pi([\ell+1])=[\ell+1]
) or (
\ell\in D
and
\pi([\ell-1])=[\ell-1]
)

then

5

return

s_{\ell}\cdot\text{permutreeSort}(s_{\ell}\cdot\pi,\;\moveU(U\setminus{\{\ell+1\}},\ell),\;\moveD(D\setminus{\{\ell\}},\ell))

6

7

return

\varepsilon

8

9

Algorithm 2

(U,D)
-permutree sorting

Example 4.2.11
.

Table
4.2
, has the
(\{3\},\{2\})
-permutree sorting of
3214
,
1324
and
1342
. In Table
4.3
, we show the
(\{2\},\{4\})
-permutree sorting of
54213
and
15342
. Each table contains the permutation
\pi_{i}
, the reduced word
w_{i}
, the sets
U_{i}
and
D_{i}
and the values of
\ell_{i}
in use at each step of the algorithm together with the value
k_{i}
for which we need to check that
\pi([k_{i}])=[k_{i}]
. A red cross signifies that this last condition has failed. Figure
4.4
contains the corresponding automata
\mathbb{P}(\{3\},\{2\})
and
\mathbb{P}(\{2\},\{4\})
.

Table 4.2:
The
(\{3\},\{2\})
-permutree sorting of
3214
(left),
1324
(top right), and
1342
(down right).

Table 4.3:
The
(\{2\},\{4\})
-permutree sorting of
54213
(left) and
15342
(right).

Corollary 4.2.12
.

For any permutation
\pi
and disjoint subsets
U,D
of
[2,n-1]
, Algorithm
2
returns a reduced word
w
accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
with the property that
w
is a reduced word for
\pi
if and only if
\pi
avoids
jki
for
j\in U
and
kij
for
j\in D
.

Démonstration.

First notice that Algorithm
2
creates a reduced word following
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
. It begins prioritizing healthy states in lines 2 by considering transpositions that transition to other healthy states in all the
\mathbb{U}(j)
or
\mathbb{D}(j)
following Lemma
4.1.12
. As we are in the product of said automata, this amounts to updating
\ell
to
\ell+1
when
\ell\in U
and
\ell+1
to
\ell
when
\ell+1\in D
as in line 3. This repeats for as many transitions as possible until we have to go to an ill state of at least one of the automata.

If there is
\ell+1\in U
(resp.
\ell\in D
) such that
s_{\ell}
is a descent of
\pi
and
\pi([\ell+1])=[\ell+1]
(resp.
\pi([\ell-1])=[\ell-1]
), then by Theorem
4.1.19
(i) any reduced word for
\pi
is accepted by the automaton
\mathbb{U}(\ell)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(\ell)
). We can thus start with
s_{\ell}
and forget about the automaton
\mathbb{U}(\ell)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(\ell)
) which is represented in lines 4, 5 and 6. Finally, if none of these options are possible, any reduced word for
\pi
leads to a dead state in at least one of the automata, so that
\pi
is not
(U,D)
-sortable. We return the empty reduced word in line 7 in such a case.
∎

4.2.4 Generating Trees

As in Subsection
4.1.3
, we can also obtain generating trees for the corresponding permutree minimal permutations. Let
\prec
be a priority order on
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1}\}
. Given a
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutation
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, let

\pi(U,J,\prec)
be the
\prec
-lexicographic minimal reduced word for
\pi
that is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(j)
and

\mathcal{R}(n,U,D,\prec):=\big\{\pi(U,D,\prec)\,:\,\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{ is }(U,D)\text{-permutree minimal}\big\}.

The proof given in Theorem
4.1.24
works as well for proving the following theorem, so we skip its proof.

Theorem 4.2.13
.

The set
\mathcal{R}(n,U,D,\prec)
is closed by taking prefixes.

As before, Theorem
4.2.13
gives us a generating tree where the root is the empty reduced word and the parent of a reduced word is obtained by deleting the last letter. Taking this tree as the sequence of associated transpositions in the weak order, we obtain a generating tree on
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutations as a subgraph of the Hasse diagram of the weak order on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Figure
4.5
shows all possible trees for
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
and priority order
s_{1}\prec s_{2}\prec s_{3}
.

Figure 4.5:
Generating trees for the
(U,D)
-permutree minimal permutations of
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
, with priority order
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s_{1}}\prec{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s_{2}}\prec{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}s_{3}}
.

4.3  Coxeter Sorting via Automata

Notice that in Section
4.2
, the biggest our sets
U
and
D
could go without a permutation failing to have a common reduced word accepted by all the automata was whenever
U
and
D
formed a partition of
[2,n-1]
. Recalling Remark
4.0.2
and Definition
2.4.38
, we see that this case is precisely when
(U,D)
-permutree congruences are Cambrian congruences in type
A
and the number of
(U,D)
-permutree minimal elements is the Catalan number
C_{n}
. This section is devoted to explore this connection with the definitions and technology developed in Subsection
2.4.6
.

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
c
be a Coxeter element of
(\mathfrak{S}_{n},S)
.

U_{c}
and

D_{c}
are the partition of
[2,n-1]
where
j\in U_{c}
(resp.
j\in D_{c}
) if
s_{j}
appears before (resp. after)
s_{j-1}
in
c
.

Remark 4.3.2
.

Another equivalent way of seeing the construction of
U_{c}
(resp.
D_{c}
) is that
j\in U_{c}
(resp.
j\in D_{c}
) if
c
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
but not by
\mathbb{D}(j)
(resp. is accepted by
\mathbb{D}(j)
but not by
\mathbb{U}(j)
).

Coxeter sorting and
(U_{c},D_{c})
-permutree sorting are related by the following theorem.

Theorem 4.3.3
.

Let
c
be a Coxeter element and
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. The following statements are equivalent :

(i)

\pi
is
c
-sortable,

(ii)

the
c
-sorting word
\pi(c)
is accepted by the automaton
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
,

(iii)

there exists a reduced word for
\pi
accepted by the automaton
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
,

(iv)

for each
j\in\{2,\dots,n-1\}
, there exists a reduced word for
\pi
that is accepted by the automaton
\mathbb{U}(j)
if
j\in U_{c}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
if
j\in D_{c}
,

(v)

\pi
avoids
jki
for
j\in U_{c}
and
kij
for
j\in D_{c}
.

Theorem
4.2.3
and Corollary
4.2.4
give equivalences
(iii)\iff(iv)\iff(v)
. In the following lemmas we show the equivalences
(i)\iff(ii)\iff(iii)
. As a general assumption for the rest of this section let
c
be a Coxeter element and
\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. We begin by showing
(i)\iff(ii)
.

Lemma 4.3.4
.

If
\pi
is
c
-sortable, its
c
-sorting word
\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
.

Démonstration.

Let
\pi(c)
be the
c
-sorting word. It is enough to see that
\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U_{c}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D_{c}
. We proceed to show it for
\mathbb{U}(j)
since the case for
\mathbb{D}(j)
is similar. We have two possible cases :

—

if
\pi(c)
does not contain
s_{j}
, then
\pi(c)
either remains in the first healthy state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
or ends in the first ill state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
.

—

if
\pi(c)
does contain
s_{j}
, by Lemma
2.4.48
(1) we have that
s_{j}
appears before
s_{j-1}
in
\pi(c)
, and
\pi(c)
moves onto the second healthy state of
\mathbb{U}(j)
. Notice that because of the recursive construction of
\mathbb{U}(j)
,
\pi(c)
can now only end in a dead state if it contains as a reduced subword
s_{l}V^{l}s_{l}s_{l+1}
where
V^{l}
is a reduced word with transpositions in
S\setminus{\{s_{l},s_{l+1}\}}
such that
s_{l-1}\in V^{l}
. Such containment is impossible due to Lemma
2.4.48
(2).

Thus, in both cases
\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.5
.

If the
c
-sorting word
\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
, then
\pi
is
c
-sortable.

Démonstration.

Assume that
\pi
is not
c
-sortable and let us find an automaton that rejects
\pi(c)
between the
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
that define
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
. To do this we follow an induction on the length of
\pi
and the size of
c
.

Consider
s_{\ell}
to be the first letter of
c
and write
c=s_{\ell}\cdot d
. As
s_{\ell}
appears before both
s_{\ell-1}
and
s_{\ell+1}
we have that
\ell\in U_{c}
and
\ell+1\in D_{c}
. Notice that
s_{\ell}
transitions from the initial state to the second healthy state of both
\mathbb{U}(\ell)
and
\mathbb{D}(\ell+1)
and stays in the initial state of all
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U_{c}\setminus{\{\ell\}}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D_{c}\setminus{\{\ell+1\}}
. We now have two possible cases :

—

if
\pi
reverses
\ell
and
\ell+1
and can be written as
\pi=s_{\ell}\cdot\tau
. By Lemma
2.4.47
we have that the
c
sorting word factorizes as
\pi(c)=s_{\ell}\cdot\tau(d\cdot s_{\ell})
and
\tau
is not
d\cdot s_{\ell}
-sortable. By our induction we have that
\tau(d\cdot s_{\ell})
is a reduced word rejected by an automaton between
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U_{d\cdot s_{\ell}}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D_{d\cdot s_{\ell}}
. Notice that
U_{d\cdot s_{\ell}}=U_{c}\Delta\{\ell,\ell+1\}
and
D_{d\cdot s_{\ell}}=D_{c}\Delta\{\ell,\ell+1\}
. Because of this, depending on the value for
j
for which the automaton rejects
\tau(d\cdot s_{\ell})
either Lemma
4.1.8
or Lemma
4.1.12
ensures that
\pi(c)=s_{\ell}\cdot\tau(d\cdot s_{\ell})
is also rejected by said automaton.

—

if
\pi
does not reverse
\ell
and
\ell+1
then
\pi
not being
c
sortable is equivalent to
\pi
not being
d
-sortable in
W_{\langle s_{\ell}\rangle}
. By induction on the size of
c
,
\pi(d)
is not accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{d},D_{d})
. Since
\pi(c)=\pi(d)
we have that
\pi(c)
is rejected by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
. We finish as
U_{d}\subseteq U_{c}
and
D_{d}\subseteq D_{c}
. ∎

Moving to
(ii)\iff(iii)
notice that
(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
follows immediately from the fact that
\pi(c)
is always a reduced word for
\pi
. The opposite direction is a bit more involved.

Lemma 4.3.6
.

If
\pi
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
, then its
c
-sorting word
\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
.

Démonstration.

We proceed by induction on the length of
\pi
and the size of
c
. As before, consider
s_{\ell}
to be the first letter of
c
and write
c=s_{\ell}\cdot d
. As
s_{\ell}
appears before both
s_{\ell-1}
and
s_{\ell+1}
we have that
\ell\in U_{c}
and
\ell+1\in D_{c}
. Notice that
s_{\ell}
transitions from the initial state to the second healthy state of both
\mathbb{U}(\ell)
and
\mathbb{D}(\ell+1)
and stays in the initial state of all
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U_{c}\setminus{\{\ell\}}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D_{c}\setminus{\{\ell+1\}}
. We now have two possible cases :

—

if
\pi
reverses
\ell
and
\ell+1
and can be written as
\pi=s_{\ell}\cdot\tau
. Notice that the reduced word of
\pi
that is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
might not be related with
\tau
. Still, since
\pi
has an accepted reduced word by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
, we have that
\pi
avoids the patterns corresponding to the elements of
U
and
D
. With this in hand, Theorem
4.1.17
tells us that
\pi
possesses a reduced word of the form
w=s_{\ell}\cdot v
such that
w
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U_{c}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D_{c}
. Notice that in our context the use of the Theorem
4.1.17
fails for
\mathbb{U}(\ell+1)
and
\mathbb{D}(\ell)
but as
\ell\in U_{c}
and
\ell+1\in D_{c}
these automata do not affect us.

At this point Lemma
4.1.8
(resp. Lemma
4.1.12
) gives us that
\tau
possesses a reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\notin\{\ell,\ell+1\}
(resp.
\mathbb{U}(j+1)
). This coincides with the fact that
s_{j-1}
and
s_{j}
have not changed order between (resp.
s_{\ell}
appears now after
s_{l-1}
and
s_{l+11}
after)
c=s_{\ell}\cdot d
to
d\cdot\ell
. Thus, by induction we have that
\tau(d\cdot s_{\ell})
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{d\cdot s_{\ell}},D_{d\cdot s_{\ell}})
. Using Lemma
2.4.47
gives us that
\pi(c)=s_{\ell}\cdot\tau(d\cdot s_{\ell})
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
.

—

if
\pi
does not reverse
\ell
and
\ell+1
. We claim that in this case
\pi
does not have
s_{\ell}
its reduced words. Notice that this is equivalent to showing that
\pi([\ell])=[\ell]
and
\pi([n]\setminus{[\ell]})=[n]\setminus{[\ell]}
. Suppose that the accepted reduced word
w
by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
contains
s_{\ell}
. As
\ell\in U_{c}
(resp.
\ell+1\in D_{c}
) and
\pi
does not reverse
\ell
and
\ell+1
, the fact that
w
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(\ell)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(\ell+1)
) means that
w
has
s_{\ell+1}
followed eventually by
s_{\ell}
before any
s_{\ell-1}
(resp.
s_{\ell-1}
followed eventually by
s_{\ell}
before any
s_{\ell+1}
). This implies that
\pi
contains the pattern
k\ell
for some
\ell<k
(resp.
(\ell+1)i
for some
i<\ell+1
). As
\ell
and
\ell+1
are not reversed,
\pi
contains
k\ell(\ell+1)i
contradicting twice Theorem
4.1.14
, and we can work as in
W_{\langle s_{\ell}\rangle}
via induction.

Notice that the automata corresponding to
d
are all the automata of
c
without the transitions having
s_{\ell}
and thus any reduced word accepted or rejected for
j\in U_{d}
(resp.
j\in D_{d}
) is also accepted or rejected for
j\in U_{c}
(resp.
j\in U_{c}
). Notice as well that this also aligns with
\ell,\ell+1\notin U_{d}
(resp.
\ell,\ell+1\notin D_{d}
). Since
\pi
does not use
s_{\ell}
in any reduced word, the reduced word accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
is also accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{d},D_{d})
. By induction, we have that
\pi(d)=\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{d},D_{d})
and thus also by all
\mathbb{U}(j)
for
j\in U_{c}\setminus{\{\ell\}}
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
for
j\in D_{c}\setminus{\{\ell+1\}}
. As
\pi(c)
also does not use any
s_{\ell}
, we have that
\pi(c)
is accepted in either the first healthy or ill state of both
\mathbb{U}(\ell)
and
\mathbb{D}(\ell+1)
. We conclude that
\pi(c)
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U_{c},D_{c})
. ∎

We move to give some remarks about nuances of
c
-sortability and
(U_{c},D_{c})
-permutree sorting.

Remark 4.3.7
.

The fact that a permutation
\pi
avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
) for a given
j
, does not assure that there exists a Coxeter element
c
for which
\pi
is
c
-sortable and
j\in U_{c}
(resp.
j\in D_{c}
). For example, let
\pi:=41352
that avoids
2ki
and
ki4
. Notice that
\pi
is not sortable since it contains both
3ki
and
ki3
via the respective subwords
352
and
413
.

Remark 4.3.8
.

Lemma
4.3.4
fails when a permutation
\pi
is not
c
-sortable as there might exist
j\in U_{c}
(resp.
j\in D_{c}
) for which the
c
-sorting word
\pi(c)
is not accepted by
\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.
\mathbb{D}(j)
) even if
\pi
avoids
jki
(resp.
kij
).
For example, take
c=s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}
and
\pi=4213=s_{3}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}=s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{2}=s_{1}\cdot s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}
.
Then
2\in U_{c}
, and the
c
-sorting word
\pi(c)=s_{1}\cdot s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}
is rejected by
\mathbb{U}(2)
while
s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
is accepted by
\mathbb{U}(2)
.

Remark 4.3.9
.

Notice that in the definition of
c
-sorting, the infinite word
c^{\infty}
is a sorting network as in
[
Knu73
]
. That is, Our algorithm rely on a series of transpositions to be applied at the appropriate time. With this in mind and taking into account Theorem
4.3.3
, it is important to notice that Algorithm
2
is not a sorting network. The order on which the values of
\ell
are chosen depending on the state of the automata that is being visited and the permutation itself. This demands the following perspective.

Perspective 4.3.10
.

Let
U
and
D
be disjoint subsets that do not cover
[2,n-1]
. Is it possible to find a word
\tilde{c}
replacing
c^{\infty}
such that checking for
\pi(\tilde{c})
is enough to verify if
\pi
is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
?

We finish with a remark on both positive and negative cases for Perspective
4.3.10
.

Remark 4.3.11
.

Consider the case
n=5
,
U=\{2\}
, and
D=\{4\}
. In this case one can check via computer exploration that no reduced word
\tilde{c}
of the maximal permutation
54321
can be used as a sorting network. This implies that for all general choices of
\tilde{c}
, there exists a permutation
\pi
which is accepted by
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
although its reduced word
\pi(\tilde{c})
is rejected. The healthy states of
\mathbb{P}(\{2\},\{4\})
are shown in Figure
4.4
. In this case the problem lies in that certain accepted reduced words can only start with either
s_{2}
or
s_{3}
. For example, for
54213
as shown in Example
4.2.11
all accepted reduced words start with
s_{3}
whereas for some other permutations such as
35421
, all accepted reduced words start with
s_{2}
. This gives us no possible choice for
\tilde{c}
.

Still the answer is positive in the Cambrian case with the
c
-sorting word when
U
and
D
form a partition of
\{2,\dots,n-1\}
or when
|U|+|D|=1
and we have a single automaton. In this later case,
\tilde{c}
is constructed by reading the healthy states of the automaton from left to right, adding at each state the word
(s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}})^{k}s_{j}
where
s_{i_{1}},\dots,s_{i_{k}}
are the loops and
s_{j}
is the unique transition going to the next healthy state. This process gives a prefix that can be extended in any way to obtain a proper sorting word
\tilde{c}
(that is, a reduced word of
w_{0}
). With this construction, if
U=\{2\}
, we obtain the prefix
s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}
and indeed
s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}
acts as a sorting network equivalent to
(\{2\},\emptyset)
-permutree sorting. This seems to extend to all cases where, at each healthy state of the
\mathbb{P}(U,D)
, the choices for the healthy transitions commute like for
n=5
,
U=\{4\}
and
D=\{2\}
(see Figure
4.4
). In this case the word
s_{1}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{4}\cdot s_{3}\cdot s_{2}\cdot s_{1}\cdot s_{4}\cdot s_{3}\cdot s_{2}
gives a proper sorting network for
(\{4\},\{2\})
-permutree sorting.

4.4  Permutree Sorting other Infinite Families

Based on Chapter
4.3
and
[
HLT11
]
and
[
Def22
]
we strongly believe that the results of permutree sorting can be extended from type
A
to the more general case of Coxeter Groups. In this section we present some observations on recognizing minimal elements of permutree congruences through automata in other Coxeter group based on computer exploration using SageMath
[
Sag23
]
. We first propose some automata for Types
B
and
D
and then present some definitions and directions that seem promising.

4.4.1 Type
B

For this subsection consider the Coxeter system of type
B
given by the group of signed permutations
(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B},S^{B})
. For this case we have the general definition of automata as follows.

Definition 4.4.1
.

Consider
U=\{j\}
(resp.
D=\{j\}
) for some
j\in[2,n-1]
and the set of generators
S^{B}
as an alphabet. We define the automaton

\mathbb{U}(j)
(resp.

\mathbb{D}(j)
) recursively following Figure
4.6
with automata
\mathbb{D}(0)
defined for consistency. Our automata are complete with all missing transitions being loops. Figure
4.7
shows the complete automata
\mathbb{U}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}(j)
.

Figure 4.6:
The automata
\mathbb{U}_{B}(j)
(left) and
\mathbb{D}_{B}(j)
(right) defined recursively.

Figure 4.7:
The complete automata
\mathbb{U}_{B}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}_{B}(j)
.

Remark 4.4.2
.

Like in Remark
4.1.4
, the automata
\mathbb{U}_{B}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}_{B}(j)
can be built via a walk in the Coxeter graph in the opposite direction of the defining orientation with a slight twist. The walk corresponding to
\mathbb{D}_{B}(j)
ends at
s_{1}
similarly as in type
A
while the walk corresponding to
\mathbb{U}_{B}(j)
ends at
s_{n}
which has an edge with label
4
meaning it must go back and travel the graph to the other end making
\mathbb{D}_{B}(j)
appear. As in other apparitions of Type
B
, this reflects idea that the Coxeter group
B_{n}
can be thought as folding of the Coxeter groups
A_{2n}
along the central symmetry of its Coxeter diagram (see
[
HL07
]
).

Following in Sections
4.1
and
4.2
we have verified computationally up to
n=6
that these automata correctly sort
(U,D)_{B}
-permutree minimal elements for all possible combinations of
U,D\subseteq[2,n-1]
such that
U\cap D=\emptyset
. Moreover, the sets of reduced words of accepted elements by these automata and their products also form trees inside the weak order of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}
. In the case that
U
and
D
form a partition of
[2,n-1]
we correctly recover the fact that there are
\binom{2n}{n}
elements that are
(U,D)_{B}
-minimal permutree, that is, the
B
-Catalan number.

4.4.2 Type
D

For type
D
we do not possess currently even a candidate definition for the general construction of the automata
\mathbb{U}_{D}(j)
and
\mathbb{D}_{D}(j)
. In certain cases of a single orientation in the Coxeter diagram of
D_{n}
we have found an automaton that recognizes
(U,D)_{D}
-permutree minimal elements. To construct it seems to be useful to consider the guiding ideas of Remarks
4.1.4
and
4.4.2
by considering a walk on the Coxeter graph with the twist that a fork of the walk at
s_{2}
implies a forking in the spine of the automata and that walks must bounce at least once. This forking of the spine is equivalent to considering several automata and taking their product. We choose this representation for our figures of these automata.

For the case of
D_{4}
we have a family of automata that have been computationally checked to recognize permutree minimal permutations in the corresponding congruences. Figure
4.8
shows the automata for the orientations
2\to 0
and
0\to 2
for
D_{4}
. As
D_{4}
is symmetric via rotations, the automata for the other single orientations are obtained by rotating the labels of these diagrams according to the desired orientation around the center vertex corresponding to
s_{2}
. For
n=5
certain orientations still elude us and for
n\geq 6
we have no further indications.

Figure 4.8:
The automata corresponding to the orientations
0\to 2
and
2\to 0
in the Coxeter diagram of
D_{4}
.

We finish this chapter by providing certain definitions that seem to have a hand in determining automata for general Coxeter groups.

4.4.3 Ideas and Perspectives

Taking inspiration from
[
HLT11
]
and their work on
c
-singletons, we propose the following definitions.

Definition 4.4.3
.

Let
(W,S)
be a Coxeter group of rank
n
and
\equiv_{\delta}
with
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{n}-1
the permutree equivalence class corresponding to the multi-orientation of the Coxeter graph. Consider an element
w\in W
. We say that
w
is

—

a

\delta
-singleton
if
w
is the only element in its permutree equivalence class,

—

a

\delta
-accepter
if for all
w\leq u
, we have that
u
is a
\delta
-singleton,

—

a

\delta
-minimal accepter
(abbreviated

\delta
-maccr
) if
w
is an accepter and there is no
s\in S
such that
w\lessdot w\cdot s
and
w\cdot s
is an accepter,

—

a

\delta
-smaccr
if
w
is a maccr and has the shortest length across all maccrs.

The use of
\delta
in these definitions serves only to recall that they depend on the congruence
\equiv_{\delta}
similar to how
c
-singletons depend on the congruence
\equiv_{c}
. We do not propose by this notation that
\delta
describes a reduced word of
w_{0}
from which the permutree sortable elements can be derived, as it is the case for the Coxeter element
c
. We have seen in Remark
4.3.11
that such reduced word might not always exist for the
\delta
case.

Based on computational evidence we propose the following conjectures. Let
\equiv_{\delta}
be any permutree congruence of a Coxeter group
W
.

Conjecture 4.4.4
.

There exists a unique
\delta
-smaccr in the left weak order of
W
. Moreover, the
\delta
-smaccr is the join of all the smaccrs coming from single orientations.

Conjecture 4.4.5
.

For each single orientation associated to
\delta
, each reduced word of the
\delta
-smaccr defines the start of the spine of an automaton such that the product of these automata recognizes the permutree minimal elements of this orientation.

Furthermore, the product of these automata for each single orientation associated to
\delta
is an automaton that recognizes
\delta
-permutree minimal elements.

Conjecture 4.4.6
.

Let
x\in W
be a
\delta
-smaccr. For each
\delta
-maccr
y\in W
there exists reduced words
u
and
v
of
x
and
y
such that
u
is a suffix of
v
. That is, the
\delta
-smaccr is the smallest
\delta
-maccr in the left weak order of
W
.

Given a congruence
\equiv_{\delta}
, the reduced words of the
\delta
-smaccr seem to be good candidates from which to start defining automata whose product recognizes the corresponding
\delta
-permutree minimal elements. Example
4.4.8
shows this for an orientation in
H_{3}
.

Example 4.4.7
.

In
D_{4}
the smaccr of the orientation for
2\to 4
(resp.
4\to 2
) has as set of reduced words
\{s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2},s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2}\}
(resp.
\{s_{4}s_{2}s_{1}s_{3},s_{4}s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}\}
). The spines of the automata shown in Figure
4.8
start with these reduced words.

Example 4.4.8
.

In
H_{3}
the smaccr of the orientation
2\to 3
has as set of reduced words the pair
\{s_{3}s_{2}s_{1}s_{3}s_{2}s_{3}s_{2},s_{3}s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{2}\}
. The spines of the automata shown in Figure
4.9
are formed with these reduced words plus a transition
s_{1}
.

Figure 4.9:
A single orientation of
H_{3}
and a product of automata that recognizes the minimal elements.

Remark 4.4.9
.

Let
\delta
denote a permutree congruence of a Coxeter group
W
corresponding to a single orientation in the Coxeter graph. We have verified the existence of a unique
\delta
-smaccr for the following Coxeter groups. We list them together with the amount of reduced words they have in each case.

—

In
A_{n}
(
n\leq 5
) it has
1
reduced word.

—

In
B_{n}
(
n\leq 4
) it has
1
reduced word.

—

In
D_{4}
it has
2
reduced words.

—

In
D_{5}
it can have anywhere between
2,5,46,100
reduced words.

—

In
H_{3}
it has
1
reduced word except for the orientation
1\to 2
where it has 2.

—

In
F_{4}
it has 2 reduced words except for any orientation of the middle edge, in which case it has 12.

partie III
Flow Polytopes

Chapitre 5  Flow Polytopes and Tropical Geometry

In this chapter we present the preliminaries of flows on graphs and tropical geometry needed for our work on Conjecture
2.5.12
. Each section consists of the main tools known in the corresponding literature together with certain refinements appearing in
[
GMP+23a
]
.

5.1  Flows on Graphs

This section consists of the basic notions of the combinatorics and geometry of flows on graphs. We base our presentation of these topics mainly on
[
MM19
]
and
[
MMS19
]
. In what follows, all graphs are connected loopless digraphs.

5.1.1 Graphs and Flows

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with vertex set
V=\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
, and a multiset of edges
E
where each edge
(v_{i},v_{j})\in E
is oriented
v_{i}\to v_{j}
if
i<j
. We respectively denote by

\mathcal{I}_{i}
and

\mathcal{O}_{i}
the set of incoming edges and outgoing edges of the vertex
v_{i}
.

A
netflow
is a vector
\mathbf{x}=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}=0
. Given a netflow
\mathbf{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1},-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i})
with
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, a

\mathbf{a}
-flow
of
G
is a function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\sum_{e\in\mathcal{I}_{i}}f(e)+a_{i}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{O}_{i}}f(e)

(5.1)

for all
i\in[1,n-1]
. In particular, if
f(e)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for all
e\in G
, then
f
is called an
integer flow
of
G
. We also call a function
f:E\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that satisfies Equation
5.1
an
admissible flow
. The
support
of a flow
f
is
\supp(f)=\{e\in E\,:\,f(e)\neq 0\}
.

See Figure
5.1
for an example of flows on a graph.

Remark 5.1.2
.

Due to our definition of flows, the netflows that we consider always have a unique sink being vertex
v_{n}
. As well, whenever we think of flows and netflows, our graph
G
is assumed to have a non-zero amount of incoming and outgoing edges for the vertices
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1}\}
. This assumption is out of simplicity and not a requirement. If we had such a vertex with no incoming (resp. outgoing) edges, any flow corresponding to an edge in
\mathcal{I}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{O}_{i}
) would be zero. Therefore, we can always assume we are working modulo such vertices.

Figure 5.1:
The zigzag graph
Zig_{4}
according to our notation and a pair of
(3,1,0,1,-5)
-flows on it. Only the one in the right is an integer flow.

Remark 5.1.3
.

For the reader who is not fond of working with graphs having multisets of edges, we remark that for our graphs, studying the multiedge case is equivalent to the single edges. Indeed, for a
\mathbf{a}
-flow
f
and a multiedge
(v_{i},v_{j})
of multiplicity
k
one can remove
k-1
multiplicities and replace them by the family of edges
(v_{i-l},v_{i-l+1})
and
(v_{i-l},v_{j})
for
l\in[1,k-1]
while shifting the indices of the vertices
\{v_{z}\}_{z\in[i-1]}
accordingly. This transforms
\mathbf{a}
to a netflow
\mathbf{a}^{*}
by passing the netflow of
v_{i}
to
v_{i-(k-1)}
, assigning
0
netflow to all vertices
\{v_{i-l}\}_{l\in{0,k}}
and all others remain the same. In this sense
f
is transformed to an
\mathbf{a}^{*}
-flow
f^{*}
where
f^{*}((v_{i-l},v_{j}))=f((v_{i},v_{j})_{l})
and
f^{*}((v_{i-l},v_{i-(l-1)}))=f^{*}((v_{i-(l-1)},v_{i-(l-2)}))+f^{*}((v_{i-(l-1)},v_{j}))
and all other edges
e
remain their original flow. See Figure
5.2
for an example of this. This is a particular case of
[
GHMY21
, Cor.2.13]
.

Figure 5.2:
A multiedged graph with a flow and its equivalent flow on a simple graph.

5.1.2 Flow Polytopes

Notice that in Definition
5.1.1
the admissible flows can be considered as points in
\mathbb{R}^{|E|}
. This begs for the following geometric definition.

Definition 5.1.4
.

Let
G
be a directed multigraph on
n
vertices with
m
edges and consider a netflow
\mathbf{a}
. Denote by
X_{G}
the multiset of vectors
\mathbf{e_{i}}-\mathbf{e_{j}}
for each edge
(v_{i},v_{j})
in
G
where
i<j
and by
M
the matrix with columns the vectors in
X_{G}
. The
flow polytope
of
G
with netflow
\mathbf{a}
is

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a}):=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\,:\,M\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{a}\text{ and }\mathbf{x}_{i}\geq 0\text{ for all }i\in[m]\}.

Since the points in this polytope correspond to nonnegative real flows on the edges of
G
, we usually use the following notation for describing the flow polytope

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})=\Big\{{\big(f(e)\big)}_{e\in E(G)}\,:\,f\text{ is a }\mathbf{a}\text{-flow of }G\Big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}.

We denote by

\mathcal{F}_{G}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{a})
the set of integer
\mathbf{a}
-flows of
G
. Between all possible flows, the flows
\mathbf{d}:=(0,d_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},-\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}d_{i})
where
d_{i}:=\indeg_{i}(G)-1
and
\mathbf{i}:=\mathbf{e_{0}}-\mathbf{e_{n}}=(1,0,\ldots,0,-1)
are of particular interest to us. Since any
\mathbf{i}
-flow of
G
corresponds to a route
R
of
G
, we denote such flow by the indicator function

\mathds{1}_{R}
where
\mathds{1}_{R}(e)=\begin{cases}1\text{ if }e\in R,\\
0\text{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

Example 5.1.5
.

Some examples of polytopes that are integrally equivalent to
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
include :

—

the simplex
\Delta_{n-1}
when
G
is the graph with vertex set
\{v_{0},v_{1}\}
and the multiedge
(v_{0},v_{1})
with multiplicity
n
,

—

the cube
\PCube_{n-1}
when
G
is the graph with vertex set
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and each multiedge
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
for
i\in[0,n-1]
has multiplicity
2
,

—

the type
A
Chan-Robbins-Yuen polytope (
CRY(n)

[
CRY98
]
,
[
CR99
]
) when
G
is the complete graph
K_{n+1}
,

—

all order polytopes of strongly planar posets. We refer the reader to
[
MMS19
, §3.3]
for the construction of the corresponding graph.

Figure
5.3
contains instances of each of these graphs.

Figure 5.3:
The graphs
G
whose respective flow polytopes
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
are respectively integrally equivalent to
\Delta_{4}
(top left),
\PCube_{2}
(top right),
CRY(4)
(bottom left), and
\mathcal{O}(P)
where
P
is the chain on
3
elements (bottom right).

Flow polytopes have plenty of interesting properties. Here we present just a few that are pertinent for our work. For a deeper dive we recommend
[
GS78a
]
,
[
RH70
]
,
[
FRD71
]
, and
[
Hil03
]
. Before that, we introduce some notation on paths within our graphs.

Definition 5.1.6
.

A
path
of a graph
G
is a connected sequence of edges. That is, a sequence of the form
((v_{k_{0}},v_{k_{1}}),(v_{k_{1}},v_{k_{2}}),\ldots,(v_{k_{l}},v_{K_{k+1}}))
, with
k_{0}<k_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots<k_{l}<k_{l+1}
. A maximal path of
G
is said to be a
route
of
G
.

Remark 5.1.7
.

For the context of this thesis and due to the specifications of Definition
5.1.1
all routes of
G
start at the source
v_{0}
and end at the unique sink
v_{n}
.

Proposition 5.1.8

(
[
Hil03
, Lem.2.1]
)
.

The flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})
is the convex polytope

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})=\conv\Big((f(e)){}_{e\in E(G)}\,:\,\supp(f)\text{ contains no (undirected) cycles}\Big).

In the particular case of the netflow
\mathbf{i}
one obtains that
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
is a
0/\penalty{1}
-polytope in the following way.

Proposition 5.1.9

(
[
GS78a
, Cor.3.1]
)
.

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})=\conv\Big((\mathds{1}_{R}(e)){}_{e\in E(G)}\,:\,R\text{ is a route of }G\Big).

See Figure
5.4
for an example.

Figure 5.4:
A graph
G
and its flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
. Each vertex of the flow polytope is labeled by the route that it corresponds to. Figure adapted from
[
MMS19
]
.

Proposition 5.1.10

(
[
BV08
, §.1.1]
)
.

The flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})
has dimension
|E|-|V|+1
.

Our flow polytopes have a particular connection with the root systems of Coxeter groups when describing their volumes and Ehrhart functions. We present it here making use of the following definition.

Definition 5.1.11

(
[
MM19
, Eq.2.3]
)
.

The
Kostant partition function

K_{G}:\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}\to\mathbb{Z}
is the function

K_{G}(\mathbf{a}):=\left|\left\{(b_{i})_{i\in[m]}\,:\,\sum_{i\in[m]}b_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}=\mathbf{a}\text{ and }b_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\right\}\right|

where
m:=|E|
and
\{\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{m}\}
is the multiset of vectors corresponding to the multiset of edges of
G
under the map sending an edge
(v_{i},v_{j})
to the vector
\mathbf{e_{i}}-\mathbf{e_{j}}
.
Equivalently, the Kostant partition function counts the number of ways to express
\mathbf{a}
as a linear combination of positive type
A
roots with coefficients in
\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 5.1.12

(
[
Pos14
]
,
[
Sta00
]
,
[
BV08
]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph on vertices
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and denote
d_{i}=\indeg_{i}(G)-1
. The normalized volume of the flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
can be expressed as

\vol\big(\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})\big)=K_{G}\left(0,d_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},-\sum d_{i}\right).

Example 5.1.13
.

Taking
G
as the graph in Figure
5.4
, we have that
\vol(\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i}))=2
. This coincides with the fact that
K_{G}(0,1,1,-2)=2
as
(0,1,1-2)
can be expressed either as
(\mathbf{e_{2}}-\mathbf{e_{4}})+(\mathbf{e_{3}}-\mathbf{e_{4}})
or
(\mathbf{e_{2}}-\mathbf{e_{3}})+2(\mathbf{e_{3}}-\mathbf{e_{4}})
.

Proposition 5.1.14

(
[
Sta00
]
)
.

The amount of integer points in the
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
is
K_{G}(\mathbf{i})
. Moreover, the Ehrhart polynomial of the flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
is given by
L_{\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})}(t)=K_{G}(t,0,\ldots,0,-t)
.

Although Propositions
5.1.12
is defined for the basic netflow
\mathbf{i}=(1,0,\ldots,0,-1)
, the general case can be seen to decompose nicely using the Kostant partition formula.

Definition 5.1.15
.

Let
\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{c}
be two weak compositions of an integer
N>0
. We say that
\mathbf{b}

dominates

\mathbf{c}
if
\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}
. We denote the
dominance order
by
\mathbf{b}\succeq\mathbf{c}
. Let
\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{m}{k}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{m}{k}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{m}{k}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{m}{k}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}=\binom{m+k-1}{k}
be the number of multisets of
[m]
of size
k
.

Theorem 5.1.16

(
[
BV08
, Thm.39]
)
.

Consider a graph
G
with vertices
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and a netflow
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
. Let
d_{j}=\indeg_{j}(G)-1
(resp.
o_{j}=\outdeg_{j}(G)-1
) and
m:=|E|
. The volume and Kostant partition functions of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})
decompose as

\begin{split}\vol\big(\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})\big)&=\sum_{\mathbf{j}}{\left(\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{m-n}{j_0,\ldots,j_{n-1}}\right)}a_{0}^{j_{0}}\cdots a_{n-1}^{j_{n-1}}K_{G}(j_{0}-o_{0},\ldots,j_{n-1}-o_{n-1},0),\\
K_{\mathcal{F}_{G}}(\mathbf{a})&=\sum_{\mathbf{j}}{\left(\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{a_0+o_0}{j_1}\right)}\cdots{\left(\genfrac{.}{.}{0.0pt}{}{a_{n-1}+o_{n-1}}{j_{n-1}}\right)}K_{G}(j_{0}-o_{0},\ldots,j_{n-1}-o_{n-1},0)\\
&=\sum_{\mathbf{j}}\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{a_{0}-d_{0}}{j_{0}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{a_{0}-d_{0}}{j_{0}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{a_{0}-d_{0}}{j_{0}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{a_{0}-d_{0}}{j_{0}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}\cdots\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{a_{n-1}-d_{n-1}}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{a_{n-1}-d_{n-1}}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{a_{n-1}-d_{n-1}}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{a_{n-1}-d_{n-1}}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}K_{G}(j_{0}-o_{0},\ldots,j_{n-1}-o_{n-1},0)\end{split}

where the sums go over all weak compositions
\mathbf{j}
of
m-n
such that
\sum_{i=0}^{k}j_{i}\geq\sum_{i=0}^{k}o_{i}
for all
i\in[0,n-1]
.
These are known as the
Baldoni–Vergne–Lidskii formulas
.

Remark 5.1.17
.

The Baldoni–Vergne–Lidskii formulas describe a subdivision technique on
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})
(see Subsection
5.1.4
). In
[
MM19
]
it was shown that the composition
\mathbf{j}
of each summand represents a type of cell of said subdivision. In this way the Kostant partition function describes the number of times that type of cell appears in the subdivision and the binomial coefficients give its volume.

Example 5.1.18
.

Some interesting volumes of flow polytopes include :

—

\vol\big(\mathcal{F}_{K_{n+1}}(\mathbf{i})\big)=\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}C_{i}
where
C_{i}
is the
i
-th Catalan number.

—

\vol\big(\mathcal{F}_{Zig_{n+1}}(\mathbf{i})\big)=E_{n-1}
where
E_{n}
is the number of alternating permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

—

\vol\big(\mathcal{F}_{\car_{n+1}}(\mathbf{i})\big)=C_{n-2}
where
\car_{n+1}
is the caracol graph and
C_{n-2}
is a Catalan number.

The following is a geometrical result from which the geometrical reader is invited to derive extensions of Propositions
5.1.12
and
5.1.14
to any netflow
\mathbf{a}
.

Proposition 5.1.19

(
[
BV08
, §3.4]
,
[
MM19
, Prop.2.1]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
a netflow. Then

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_{i}\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{e_{i}}-\mathbf{e_{n}}).

We finish with a crucial relation between flow polytopes and Cayley embeddings.

Definition 5.1.20
.

Let
G
be a graph on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
. The graph

G^{*}
is the graph obtained from
G
by adding a vertex
v^{*}
and edges
(v^{*},v_{i})
for
i\in[0,n-1]
. See Figure
5.5
for an example.

Proposition 5.1.21

(
[
MM19
, Prop.7.2]
)
.

The flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G*}(\mathbf{e_{0}}-\mathbf{e_{n+1}})
is the Cayley embedding
\mathcal{C}\big(\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{e_{0}}-\mathbf{e_{n}}),\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{e_{n-1}}-\mathbf{e_{n}})\big)
.

Figure 5.5:
The construction of the graph
G^{*}
whose flow polytope is the Cayley embedding of flow polytopes from
G
. Figure based on
[
MM19
]
.

Proposition 5.1.22
.

Let
G
be a graph on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
. The flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{e_{0}}-\mathbf{e_{n}})
is the Cayley embedding of the flow polytopes
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{e_{i}}-\mathbf{e_{n}})
for the
i\in[n]
such that
G
has an edge
(v_{0},v_{i})
.

We now move on to the main property of flow polytopes that is of use for our work which is how they can be subdivided into smaller polytopes. We present the techniques of two such subdivisions coming from
[
DKK12
]
and
[
Sta00
]
.

5.1.3 DKK Triangulations

For the first subdivision let us define a way to compare routes by inducing a partial order on routes from an order on in-coming and out-going edges of the vertices of
G
. We base our presentation on
[
DKK12
]
.

Definition 5.1.23
.

Let
G
be a graph on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
. A
framing
of
G
is a choice of linear orders
\preceq_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
\preceq_{\mathcal{O}_{i}}
on the sets of incoming and outgoing edges for each vertex
v_{i}
where
i\in[n-1]
. When
G
is endowed with such a framing
\preceq
, we say that
G
is
framed
. For an edge
e=(v_{i},v_{j})
we denote by
\mathcal{I}(e)
(resp.
\mathcal{O}(e)
) the position of the edge
e
in the framing order
\preceq_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
(resp.
\preceq_{\mathcal{O}_{i}}
).

Definition 5.1.24
.

Let
P,Q
be two routes of
G
that contain vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
. We denote by

Pv_{i}
the prefix of
P
that ends at
v_{i}
,

v_{i}P
the suffix of
P
that starts at
v_{i}
and

v_{i}Pv_{j}
the subroute of
P
that starts at
v_{i}
and ends at
v_{j}
.

Let
\prec
be a framing of
G
and consider
v_{i}P,v_{i}Q
(res.
Pv_{i},Qv_{i}
) a pair of paths between
v_{i}
and
v_{n}
(resp. between
v_{0}
and
v_{i}
) which coincide from
v_{i}
until a minimal vertex
v_{j}
(resp. from a maximal vertex
v_{j}
until
v_{i}
) (possibly
i=j
). Denote by
e_{p}
and
e_{Q}
the corresponding edges in
P
and
Q
with starting (resp. ending) vertex
v_{j}
. We say that

v_{i}P\preceq v_{i}Q
if
e_{P}\preceq_{\mathcal{O}_{j}}e_{Q}
(resp.

Pv_{i}\preceq Qv_{i}
if
e_{P}\preceq_{\mathcal{I}_{j}}e_{Q}
).

Definition 5.1.25
.

Consider
G
to be a graph with framing
\prec
. Let
P
and
Q
be routes of
G
that share a subroute between the vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
. We say that
P
and
Q
are
conflicting routes
at
[v_{i},v_{j}]
(possibly
v_{i}=v_{j}
) if the initial paths
Pv_{i}
and
Qv_{i}
are ordered different relative to the paths
v_{i}P
and
v_{i}Q
with respect to
\prec
. In the case that
P
and
Q
have no conflict in any common subroute we say that
P
and
Q
are
coherent routes
.

See Figure
5.6
for an example of coherent and conflicting routes on a framed graph.

Figure 5.6:
A framed graph with a pair of coherent (left) and conflicting (right) routes. Only
1
route is bolded.

Remark 5.1.26
.

Notice that one could define framing orders for
v_{0}
(resp.
v_{n}
) but since this vertex has no incoming (resp. outgoing) edges, checking for coherence is trivially true and thus not necessary. For completeness, we say that
\mathcal{O}_{1}(e)=0
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{n}(e)=0
) for any starting (resp. final) edge.

Definition 5.1.27
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph. We call a set of mutually coherent routes of
G
a
clique
. We denote by

\text{Cliques}(G,\preceq)
the set of cliques of
(G,\preceq)
, and

\text{MaxCliques}(G,\preceq)
the set of maximal collection of cliques under inclusion. If a route
P
is coherent with all other routes of
G
we say that
P
is
exceptional
.

Remark 5.1.28
.

Notice that the coherence relation between routes is reflexive and symmetric but not transitive. Take all the routes given in Figure
5.7
for a particular framed graph. The maximal cliques in this case are
\{R_{1},R_{2},R_{3},R_{5}\}
and
\{R_{1},R_{2},R_{4},R_{5}\}
. Notice that although
R_{1}
is coherent with both
R_{3}
and
R_{4}
, they are not coherent as they have a conflict in the inner vertex. The routes
R_{1},R_{2}
, and
R_{5}
are all exceptional routes.

Figure 5.7:
All possible routes (bolded in blue) of a framed graph.

Definition 5.1.29
.

Given the flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
and a clique
C\in\text{Cliques}(G,\preceq)
, we define the simplex

\Delta_{C}
corresponding to
C
as

\Delta_{C}=\conv\Big(\big(\mathds{1}_{R}(e)\big)_{e\in E}\,:\,R\text{ is a route in }C\Big).

Proposition 5.1.30

(
[
DKK12
, Thm.1 & 2]
)
.

Given a graph
G
with framing
\preceq
, the set of simplices
\{\Delta_{C}\,:\,C\in\text{MaxCliques}(G,\preceq)\}
form a regular triangulation of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
. We refer to this triangulation as the
DKK triangulation
and denote it by

\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

For the regularity of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, the authors in
[
DKK12
]
gave the following condition for a height function to be admissible where
P+Q
denotes the union of the edges of the routes
P
and
Q
.

Proposition 5.1.31

(
[
DKK12
, Lem.2]
)
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph. A function
\height
from the routes of
G
to
\mathbb{R}
is an admissible height function of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
if for any two non-coherent routes
P
and
Q
there exist routes
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
such that
P+Q=P^{\prime}+Q^{\prime}
and

\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime}).

(5.2)

We refine the condition on the non-coherence to make it necessary and sufficient. For that we need the following definition.

Definition 5.1.32
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph and
P,Q
a pair of conflicting routes at the subroutes
[v_{i_{1}},v_{i_{1}}^{\prime}]
,
\dots
,
[v_{i_{k}},v_{i_{k}}^{\prime}]
, where
i_{1}\leq i_{1}^{\prime}<i_{2}\leq i_{2}^{\prime}\ldots<i_{k}\leq i_{k}^{\prime}
. We call the
resolvents
of
P
and
Q
the paths
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
defined as

\begin{split}P^{\prime}&:=Pv_{i_{1}}Qv_{i_{2}}Pv_{i_{3}}\cdots,\\
Q^{\prime}&:=Qv_{i_{1}}Pv_{i_{2}}Qv_{i_{3}}\cdots\end{split}

where
Pv_{i_{1}}Qv_{i_{2}}Pv_{i_{3}}\cdots
denotes the concatenation of the subroutes
Pv_{i_{1}},v_{i_{1}}Qv_{i_{2}},v_{i_{2}}Pv_{i_{3}},\ldots
finishing with
v_{i_{k}}P
or
v_{i_{k}}Q
depending on the parity of
k
.

We say that
P
and
Q
are in
minimal conflict
if they are in conflict in exactly one subroute
[v_{i},v_{j}]
and the edges of
P
and
Q
that end at
v_{i}
(resp. start at
v_{j}
) are adjacent for the total order
\preceq_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
(resp.
\preceq_{\mathcal{O}_{j}}
).

See Figure
5.8
for an example of the resolvents of a minimal conflict.

Figure 5.8:
Two routes with a minimal conflict (left) and their resolvents (right). Only
1
route is bolded.

Lemma 5.1.33

(
[
GMP+23a
, Lem.5.4]
)
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph.
A function
\height
from the routes of
G
to
\mathbb{R}
is an admissible height function of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
if and only if for any two non-coherent routes
P
and
Q
with resolvents
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
we have

\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime}).

(5.3)

Démonstration.

The proof of Proposition
5.1.31
works the same when
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
are the resolvents of
P
and
Q
as the resolvents are always coherent not only between themselves but also with
P
and
Q
. We thus omit this direction. Suppose that
\height
is an admissible height function for
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
and consider
P
and
Q
two non-coherent routes with resolvents
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
. As
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
are coherent, they are part of at least one clique and
\mathbf{p^{\prime}}=(\mathds{1}_{P^{\prime}}(e))_{e\in E}
and
\mathbf{q^{\prime}}=(\mathds{1}_{Q^{\prime}}(e))_{e\in E}
are the vertices of an edge of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
. Letting
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
be the corresponding points to the routes
P
and
Q
, we have that the point
\mathbf{(}c)=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{p^{\prime}}+\mathbf{q^{\prime}})=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q})
is in the edge
[\mathbf{p^{\prime}},\mathbf{q^{\prime}}]
. As this edge must be lifted to a lower face of the lift of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
via the admissible function
\height
we have that
\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime})
.
∎

Using minimal conflicts we can make this statement stronger.

Lemma 5.1.34

(
[
GMP+23a
, Lem.5.5]
)
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph.
A function
\height
from the routes of
G
to
\mathbb{R}
is an admissible height function of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
if and only if for any two non-coherent routes
P
and
Q
in minimal conflict with resolvents
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
we have

\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime}).

(5.4)

Démonstration.

Let
\height
be a function from the routes of
G
to
\mathbb{R}
such that for any minimal conflict between two routes
P
and
Q
with resolvents
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
, we have
\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime})
. From Lemma
5.1.33
we only need to show that for any two conflicting routes
P
and
Q
, there exist routes
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
such that
P+Q=P^{\prime}+Q^{\prime}
and
\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime})
. We proceed by induction on the number of conflicts between
P
and
Q
.

First, suppose that
P
and
Q
are conflicting at exactly one subroute
[v_{i},v_{j}]
. Let
e_{1}:=e_{P}
and
e_{k}:=e_{Q}
be the respective edges of
P
and
Q
that end at
v_{i}
and
e_{i}
for
i\in[2,k-1]
be all the other edges of
G
such that we have
\mathcal{I}(e_{1})\lessdot\mathcal{I}(e_{2})\lessdot\cdots\lessdot\mathcal{I}(e_{k})
. Letting
R_{1}:=Pv_{i}
and
R_{k}:=Qv_{i}
, we define the partial routes
R_{i}
(resp.
S_{i}
) for
i\in[2,k-1]
from right to left and from
1
to
k-1
. The starting step is the ending edge which is already determined. At step
i
we can choose any other edge unless we arrive at a vertex common to a previously built partial route. In this case we must choose the same edges as in this partial route. This gives
Pv_{i}=R_{1}\prec R_{2}\prec\ldots\prec R_{k}=Qv_{i}
and a similar construction gives
v_{j}Q=S_{1}\prec S_{2}\prec\ldots\prec S_{l}=v_{j}P
.

With this for any
x\in[k-1]
and
y\in[l-1]
we have that the routes
R_{x}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y+1}
and
R_{x+1}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y}
are in minimal conflict, with resolvents
R_{x}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y}
and
R_{x+1}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y+1}
. Our assumption on the height function
\height
implies the inequality :

\height(R_{x}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y+1})+\height(R_{x+1}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y})>\height(R_{x}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y})+\height(R_{x+1}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y+1}).

(5.5)

Adding all these inequalities for all
x\in[k-1]
and
y\in[l-1]
we have that all terms of the form
\height(R_{x}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{y})
cancel out by pairs except for the pairs
(x,y)\in\{(1,1),(k,t),(1,t),(k,1)\}
. This gives us the inequality

\height(P)+\height(Q)=\height(R_{1}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{t})+\height(R_{k}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{1})>\height(R_{1}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{1})+\height(R_{k}v_{i}Pv_{j}S_{t})=\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime}),

where
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
are the resolvents of
P
and
Q
as we wished.

For the induction step suppose that
\height
satisfies
\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime})
where
P
and
Q
are conflicting routes with at most
n
conflicts and resolvents
P^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
. Let
P
and
Q
be conflicting routes with
n+1
conflicts at the subroutes
[x_{1},y_{1}],\ldots,[x_{n+1},y_{n+1}]
.
Since the routes
P
and
Px_{1}Q
(resp.
Q
and
Qx_{1}P
) have
n
conflicts and their resolvents are
Px_{1}P^{\prime}=P^{\prime}
and
Px_{1}Q^{\prime}
(
Qx_{1}P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}x_{1}Q=Q^{\prime}
), the induction hypothesis gives us the inequalities

\begin{split}\height(P)+\height(Px_{1}Q)&>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Px_{1}Q^{\prime}),\\
\height(Q)+\height(Qx_{1}P)&>\height(Qx_{1}P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime}).\end{split}

Now notice that the routes
P
and
Qx_{1}P^{\prime}
only have one conflict and their resolvents are
P^{\prime}
and
Qx_{1}P
. Therefore, we also have the inequalities

\begin{split}\height(P)+\height(Qx_{1}P^{\prime})&>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Qx_{1}P),\\
\height(Q)+\height(Px_{1}Q^{\prime})&>\height(Px_{1}Q)+\height(Q^{\prime}).\end{split}

Adding up these four inequalities yields

\height(P)+\height(Q)>\height(P^{\prime})+\height(Q^{\prime}).\qed

After obtaining a condition for the height function
\height
to be admissible on
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, the authors of
[
DKK12
]
constructed an explicit height function as follows.

Definition 5.1.35
.

Let
P
be a route in a framed graph
(G,\preceq)
given by the edges
e_{i_{1}},\ldots,e_{i_{k}}
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. The height

\height_{\varepsilon}
of
P
is defined as

\height_{\varepsilon}(P):=\sum_{1\leq a<c\leq k}\varepsilon^{c-a}{(\mathcal{I}(e_{a})+\mathcal{O}(e_{c}))}^{2}.

Proposition 5.1.36

(
[
DKK12
, Lem.3]
)
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph. The height function
\height_{\varepsilon}(R)
is an admissible height function for the triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({G},\preceq\nolinebreak)
of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
.

5.1.4 Postnikov-Stanley Subdivisions

Another way to subdivide flow polytopes consists on dividing them into two polytopes that are integrally equivalent to other flow polytopes. We refer the curious reader (out of respect like other flow polytope papers do) to
[
Pos14
]
and
[
Sta00
]
for the birthing place of this subdivision. For an actual detailed view we recommend
[
MM13
]
and
[
MM19
]
. For this subsection we follow
[
MM19
]
.

Definition 5.1.37
.

Let
G
be a graph on vertices
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
with edges
(v_{i},v_{j}),(v_{j},v_{k})\in E(G)
. The
basic reduction
is the creation of the graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
on vertices
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
and edges

\begin{split}E(G_{1})&:=E(G)\setminus\{(v_{j},v_{k})\}\cup\{(v_{i},v_{k})\},\\
E(G_{2})&:=E(G)\setminus\{(v_{i},v_{j})\}\cup\{(v_{i},v_{k})\}.\end{split}

Proposition 5.1.38

(
[
Pos14
]
,
[
Sta00
]
,
[
MM19
, Prop.3.1]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
with a pair of edges
e_{1},e_{2}
on which the basic reduction can be done. Then we have

\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})=\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{ and }\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{\mathcal{P}}_{1}^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{P}_{2}^{\circ}=\varnothing,

where
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is integrally equivalent to
\mathcal{F}_{G_{i}}(\mathbf{a})
and
\mathcal{P}^{\circ}
denotes the interior of the polytope
\mathcal{P}
.

Figure
5.9
contains an illustration of the basic reduction.

The repeated use of basic reductions (called a
compounded reduction
) on
G
together with some extra conditions on when one discards a resulting graph yields a triangulation of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{a})
called a
Postnikov-Stanley triangulation
Indeed, at the end of a compounded reduction one obtains multi-graphs with edges of the form
(v_{i},v_{n})
for
i\in[0,n-1]
. Like in Example
5.1.5
, the flow polytopes of these graphs are simplices of the highest dimension.

Figure 5.9:
The basic reduction of flow polytopes. Figure based on
[
MM19
]
.

The choosing on the vertices or the pairs of edges taken for each step of the compounded reductions can change the final triangulation. In
[
MMS19
]
it was shown that Postnikov-Stanley triangulations coincided in certain cases with DKK triangulations by doing a compounded reduction following the framing of
G
. The triangulations obtained like this are called
framed Postnikov-Stanley triangulations
. In our interest, this includes the case of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})
which we now present.

Definition 5.1.39
.

Let
(G,\preceq)
be a framed graph with netflow
\mathbf{d}
. We define the function

\Omega_{G,\preceq}
between the maximal cliques
\text{MaxCliques}(G,\preceq)
and the integer flows of
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{d})
as

\Omega_{G,\preceq}(C):={\big(n_{C}(e)-1\big)}_{e\in E(G)}

where
n_{C}(v_{i},v_{j}):=|\{P\in C\,:\,(v_{i},v_{j})\text{ is in the prefix }Pv_{j}\}|
.

Proposition 5.1.40

(
[
MMS19
, Thm 7.8]
)
.

Given a framed graph
(G,\preceq)
, the map
\Omega_{G,\preceq}
is a bijection between maximal cliques in
\text{MaxCliques}(G,\preceq)
and integer flows in
\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{d})
.

As a direct consequence of Proposition
5.1.40
we get the following result.

Proposition 5.1.41
.

For a graph
G
and netflow
\mathbf{d}:=(0,d_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},-\sum_{i}d_{i})
where
d_{i}=\indeg_{i}(G)-1
, we have that

\vol\Big(\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{i})\Big)=\Big|\mathcal{F}_{G}(\mathbf{d})\Big|.

5.2  Tropical Geometry

We now move on to describe the bases of tropical geometry and an application of this context to the Cayley trick. We follow heavily
[
Jos17
]
and
[
Jos21
]
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

The
tropical semiring
of the min-plus algebra is the tuple
(\mathbb{T},\odot,\oplus)
formed by
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
together with the
\min
operation as the tropical addition
\oplus
and the usual addition
+
as the tropical multiplication
\odot
. The additive identity is
\infty
and the multiplicative identity is
0
.

Definition 5.2.2
.

An

n
-variate tropical polynomial
is a linear combination of tropical monomials with possibly negative exponents and appear as

F(\mathbf{x})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}\in I}c_{\mathbf{m}}\odot\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}}=\min_{\mathbf{m}\in I}\big(c_{\mathbf{m}}+\langle\mathbf{m},\mathbf{x}\rangle\big)

where
I
is a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
c_{m}\in\mathbb{T}
. The
support
of a polynomial is the set
\supp(F)=\{\mathbf{m}\in I\,:\,c_{\mathbf{m}}\neq\infty\}
. The
degree
of a monomial
x_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{m_{n}}
is
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}
and the
degree
of a polynomial
F
is the maximal degree of its monomials.

Remark 5.2.3
.

Notice that the support of a tropical polynomial can be seen as a point configuration on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
equipped with a height function given by the coefficients
c_{m}
. In the reverse direction, any lattice point configuration together with a lifting function determines a tropical polynomial. Thus, any interest of heightened point configurations can make use of tropical polynomials.

Figure 5.10:
The tropical hypersurfaces of several tropical polynomials.

In
\mathbb{R}
hypersurfaces of varying degree come from seeing where polynomials vanish. In the tropical setting of
\mathbb{T}
this is recontextualized by looking at where the minimum of a tropical polynomial is attained at least twice.

Definition 5.2.4
.

The
tropical hypersurface
determined by
F
is the set

\mathcal{T}(F):=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\text{the minimum of\penalty\ $F(\mathbf{x})$ is attained at least twice}\right\}.

Several tropical hypersurfaces are illustrated in Figure
5.10
.

Proposition 5.2.5
.

For tropical polynomials
F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}
we have that

\mathcal{T}\Big(\bigodot_{i\in[k]}F_{i}\Big)=\bigcup_{i\in[k]}\mathcal{T}(F_{i}).

5.2.1 Polyhedral Constructions

Tropical geometry is of particular use to us since it has a deep relation with polyhedral geometry. In this section we describe two polyhedral constructions we need as tools called the dome and the Newton polytope of a tropical polynomial. We begin with the following crucial remark.

Remark 5.2.6
.

Notice that as
F(\mathbf{x})
consists only of linear combinations and minima,
\mathcal{T}(F)
is an
{n-1}
-polyhedral complex.

Definition 5.2.7
.

For a tropical polynomial
F
, its
dome
is the unbounded
n+1
-polyhedron

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(F)&:=\left\{(\mathbf{x},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},s\in\mathbb{R},s\leq F(\mathbf{x})\right\}\\
&=\bigcap_{\mathbf{x}\in\supp(F)}\left\{(\mathbf{p},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,:\,s\leq c_{\mathbf{m}}+\langle\mathbf{m},\mathbf{x}\rangle\right\}.\end{split}

The dome helps us as it recovers in the polyhedral setting the geometry of the tropical hypersurface.

Proposition 5.2.8

(
[
Jos21
, Cor. 1.6]
)
.

The tropical hypersurface
\mathcal{T}(F)
is the image of the
n-1
-skeleton of its dome
\mathcal{D}(F)
under the projection that forgets the last coordinate.

In other words, if
F
is an
n
-variate polynomial,
\mathcal{T}(F)
is a
(n-1)
-dimensional polyhedral complex and the connected components of its complement are projections of the relative interiors of facets of the dome
\mathcal{D}(F)
