. In particular, each facet of the dome corresponds to a tropical monomial
x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}
and the edges correspond to when the minimum is shared between two monomials. See Figure
5.11
(left) for an example.

Figure 5.11:
The orthogonal projection of the facets of
\mathcal{D}(F)
(left) and
\mathcal{N}(F)
(right) where
F
is the product of the tropical polynomials in Figure
5.10
.

The second polytope related to tropical polynomials is the Newton polytope associated to the support points of
F
. See Figure
5.11
(right) for the Newton polytope of our running example.

Definition 5.2.9
.

Given a tropical polynomial
F(x)
, the convex hull of its support is called the
Newton polytope
of
F
. That is,

\mathcal{N}(F):=\conv\left(\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\,:\,\mathbf{m}\in\supp(F)\right).

The
extended Newton polytope
corresponds to the Minkowski sum

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F):=&\conv\left((\mathbf{m},r)\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,\mathbf{m}\in\supp(F),r\geq c_{\mathbf{m}}\right)\\
=&\conv\left((\mathbf{m},c_{\mathbf{m}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\,:\,\mathbf{m}\in\supp(F)\right)+\cone(\mathbf{e_{n+1}}).\end{split}

Following Remark
5.2.3
, consider
\supp(F)
as a point configuration. The projection downwards of the faces of
\widetilde{N}(F)
is a regular subdivision of the Newton polytope
\mathcal{N}(F)
called the
dual subdivision
and denoted as

\mathcal{S}(F)
.

Remark 5.2.10
.

Notice that the product of tropical monomials is

\begin{split}F(\mathbf{x})&=\bigodot_{i\in[k]}F_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\bigodot_{i\in[k]}\bigoplus_{\mathbf{m}^{i}\in I_{i}}c_{\mathbf{m}^{i}}\odot\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}^{i}}\\
&=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{I}}\bigodot_{i\in[k]}c_{\mathbf{m}^{i}}\odot\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}^{i}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{I}}c_{\mathbf{m}^{1}}+\cdots+c_{\mathbf{m}^{k}}\odot\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{m}^{k}}\\
\end{split}

where
I_{i}\subset\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
\mathcal{I}=I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{k}
and
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbf{m}^{1},\ldots,\mathbf{m}^{k})
. Thus, for the product of tropical polynomials
F=\bigodot_{i\in[k]}F_{i}
, the corresponding Newton polytope is

\mathcal{N}(F)=\mathcal{N}(F_{1})+\cdots+\mathcal{N}(F_{k}).

Moreover, abusing the notation of Minkowski sums, we have that
\supp(F)=\supp(F_{1})+\cdots+\supp(F_{k})
.

It turns out that the dome and the Newton polytope of tropical polynomials are actually dual constructions. Moreover, our constructions
\mathcal{T}(F)
,
\mathcal{D}(F)
,
\mathcal{N}(F)
and
\mathcal{S}(F)
are related in the following way.

Proposition 5.2.11

(
[
Jos21
, Thm.1.13]
)
.

Let
F
be an
n
-variate tropical polynomial.

—

The faces of the dome
\mathcal{D}(F)
have an inclusion-reversing bijection with the bounded faces of the extended Newton polytope
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F)
.

—

The orthogonal projection of the proper faces of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F)
give the cells of the dual subdivision
\mathcal{S}(F)
.

—

The
k
-dimensional cells of
\mathcal{T}(F)
are in bijection with the
(n-k)
-dimensional cells of
\mathcal{S}(F)
for
0\leq k<n
.

In particular, take notice that the bijection of Proposition
5.2.11
sends a vertex
\mathbf{m}\in\mathcal{N}(F)
to the region
\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,c_{\mathbf{m}}+\langle\mathbf{m},\mathbf{x}\rangle=\min_{m^{\prime}\in I}\Big(c_{\mathbf{m}^{\prime}}+\langle\mathbf{m}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}\rangle\Big)\right\}
, and a cell of
\mathcal{S}
to the intersection of the regions corresponding to its vertices. See Figure
5.12
for an example. Due to the dual nature of Proposition
5.2.11
we say that
S(\mathcal{F})
is the
tropical dual
of
\mathcal{T}(F)
.

Figure 5.12:
The orthogonal projection of
\mathcal{D}(F)
(left) with regions labeled by the respective dominant monomial and the (flipped) dual subdivision
\mathcal{S}(F)
with cells labeled by their respective exponent in
\supp(F)
(right) where
F
is the multiplication of the tropical polynomials in Figure
5.10
.

We go a step further and restrict Proposition
5.2.11
to the bounded cells of
\mathcal{T}(F)
.

Lemma 5.2.12

(
[
GMP+23a
, Lem.5.2]
)
.

The bijection of Proposition
5.2.11
restricts to a bijection between the bounded cells of
\mathcal{T}(F)
and the interior cells of
\mathcal{S}(F)
.

Démonstration.

Since
\mathcal{S}(F)
is a regular subdivision of a polytope, it is a pure complex. Thus, we abuse the notation and call the
(d-1)
-dimensional cells the facets of
\mathcal{S}(F)
.
We first show the Lemma for the bounded edges of
\mathcal{T}(F)
and the interior facets of
\mathcal{S}(F)
in a fashion similar to the proof of
[
Jos21
, Theorem 1.13]
.
Let
\mathbf{e}
be an edge of
\mathcal{T}(F)
,
H
its corresponding facet in
\mathcal{S}
via the bijection, and
\widetilde{H}
its lift to
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F)
. We begin by characterizing these objects.

A point
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{e}
exists if and only if the minimum
F(\mathbf{x})
equals
c_{\mathbf{m}}+\langle\mathbf{m},\mathbf{x}\rangle
for each
\mathbf{m}\in H
. This means that taking
(\mathbf{x},1):=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},1)
, we have that the linear form
(\mathbf{x},1)
attains its minimum on
\widetilde{H}
and the vector
-(\mathbf{x},1)
is in the normal cone of
\widetilde{H}
. For
H
, and
\widetilde{H}
, notice that since
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F)
is Minkowski sum of a polytope and the ray
\cone(\mathbf{e_{n+1}})
, a vector is normal to an unbounded face of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F)
if and only if its last coordinate is
0
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{e}
is unbounded, that is,
\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{w}+\cone(\mathbf{v})
for some
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. From before we have that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
-(\mathbf{w}+\lambda\mathbf{v},1)
is in the normal cone of
\widetilde{H}
. Taking the limit of
\lambda\to\infty
of
-\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\mathbf{w}+\mathbf{v},\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)
tells us that
-(\mathbf{v},0)
is also in the normal cone of
\widetilde{H}
. Thus,
H
is in the boundary of
\mathcal{S}
.

Reciprocally, if
H
is in the boundary of
\mathcal{S}
, it means that the normal cone of
\widetilde{H}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(F)
is a
2
-dimensional cone whose extremal rays can be written
-\cone\big((\mathbf{v},0)\big)
and
-\cone\big((\mathbf{w},1)\big)
, for some
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the vector
-(\mathbf{w}+\lambda\mathbf{v},1)
is in this cone, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we have that the point
\mathbf{w}+\lambda\mathbf{v}
belongs to the edge
\mathbf{e}
in
\mathcal{T}(F)
. Thus, this edge is unbounded.

Having that the bijection restricts to a bijection between the bounded edges of
\mathcal{T}(F)
and the interior facets of
\mathcal{S}
, we can extend easily the bijection to other faces. Notice that any cell of
\mathcal{S}
is either maximal in dimension and is sent to a vertex of
\mathcal{T}(F)
, or it is an intersection of (possibly 1) facets of
\mathcal{S}
. A non-maximal cell of
\mathcal{S}
is interior if and only if it is included only in interior facets of
\mathcal{S}
. Thus, by the inclusion-reversing bijection, it is sent to a cell of
\mathcal{T}(F)
containing only bounded edges. Reciprocally, a non-bounded cell of
\mathcal{T}(F)
contains a non-bounded edge, so it is sent to a boundary cell of
\mathcal{S}
. We get the desired result.
∎

5.2.2 An Application of the Cayley Trick

Consider a finite family of point configurations
\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{k}
with associated height functions
h_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
. Following Remark
5.2.3
these point configurations correspond to tropical polynomials of the form
F_{i}(\mathbf{x})=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{a}^{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}h_{i}(\mathbf{a}^{i})\odot\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{a}^{i}}
. Denote their multiplication by
F=\bigodot_{i\in[k]F_{i}}
. Taking their corresponding Newton polytopes
\mathcal{N}(F_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{N}(F_{k})
, we consider their Cayley embedding

\mathcal{C}\big(\mathcal{N}(F_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{N}(F_{k})\big):=\conv\Big(\{\mathbf{e_{1}}\}\times\mathcal{N}(F_{1}),\ldots,\{\mathbf{e_{k}}\}\times\mathcal{N}(F_{k})\Big)

and endow it with the height function on its integer points given by
h_{F}\big((\mathbf{e_{i}},\mathbf{a}^{i})\big)=h_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{i})
. Let

\Sigma(F)
be the regular subdivision of
\mathcal{C}\big(\mathcal{N}(F_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{N}(F_{k})\big)
induced by
h_{F}
following the construction denoted in Definition
1.2.28
.

In this context the Cayley trick (Proposition
1.2.31
) tells us that
\Sigma_{F}
corresponds to a mixed subdivision of
\mathcal{N}(F_{1})+\cdots+\mathcal{N}(F_{k})=:\mathcal{N}(F)
. The following proposition tells us that this mixed subdivision is one that we have already encountered.

Proposition 5.2.13

(
[
Jos21
, Cor.4.9]
)
.

Let
F=\bigodot_{i\in[k]F_{i}}
be a multiplication of tropical polynomials. The mixed subdivision of
\mathcal{N}(F)
corresponding to the subdivision
\Sigma_{F}
of the Cayley embedding
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{N}(F_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{N}(F_{k}))
via the Cayley trick is the dual subdivision
\mathcal{S}(F)
.

We now reformulate Proposition
5.2.11
using the context we have obtained from Proposition
5.2.13
.

Proposition 5.2.14
.

Let
F=F_{1}\odot\cdots\odot F_{k}
be a product of tropical polynomials. The tropical dual of the polyhedral complex
\mathcal{T}(F)
(i.e. the union
\bigcup_{i\in[k]}\mathcal{T}(F_{i})
) is the mixed subdivision
\mathcal{S}(F)
obtained via the Cayley trick.

Chapitre 6  Realizing the
s
-Permutahedron via Flow Polytopes

We devote this chapter to answer Conjecture
2.5.12
of Ceballos and Pons
[
CP19
, Conj.1]
about realizing the
s
-permutahedron
\text{Perm}_{s}
as a polyhedral subdivision of a zonotope in the case where
s
is free of zeros. We make use of the combinatorial and geometrical toolbox of flows on graphs and tropical geometry described in Chapter
5
. This chapter is based directly on
[
GMP+23a
]
. In what follows we present three realizations of
\text{Perm}_{s}
.

6.1  The Flow Polytope Realization

Let us start by defining a graph that encodes the
s
-weak order. Following the tradition of
[
BGH+19
]
and
[
vGMY23
]
where the respective Caracol
\car_{n}
and
s
-Caracol
\car(s)
graphs were defined, we define the
s
-Oruga graph denoted
\oru(s)
. Oruga and caracol are respectively the Spanish words for caterpillar and snail. These names come from the embedding of these graphs when
s=(1,\ldots,1)
as in Figure
7.1
. Examples of this embedding for
\car(s)
and
\oru(s)
are shown in Figure
6.1
.

Figure 6.1:
The
s
-caracol
\car(s)
(left) and
s
-oruga
\oru(s)
(right) graphs.

Definition 6.1.1
.

Let
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
be a composition, and for convenience of notation fix
s_{n+1}=2
. The

s
-oruga graph
denoted

\oru(s)
is the graph on vertices
\{v_{-1},v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
such that for

—

i\in[n+1]
, there are
s_{i}-1

source edges

(v_{-1},v_{n+1-i})
labeled
e^{i}_{1},\ldots,e^{i}_{s_{i}-1}
,

—

i\in[n]
, there are two edges
(v_{n+1-(i+1)},v_{n+1-i})
called
bump
and
dip
labeled
e^{i}_{0}
and
e^{i}_{s_{i}}
.

For the rest of our work we endow
\oru(s)
with the following framing for :

—

the incoming edges of
v_{n+1-i}
are ordered
e^{i}_{j}\prec_{\mathcal{I}_{n+1-i}}e^{i}_{k}
for
0\leq j<k\leq s_{i}
,

—

the outgoing edges of
v_{n+1-i}
are ordered
e^{i-1}_{0}\prec_{\mathcal{O}_{n+1-i}}e^{i-1}_{s_{i-1}}
.

When
s=(1,\ldots,1)
, following Remark
6.1.3
we contract the edge
(v_{-1},v_{0})
and call the resulting graph the
oruga graph
denoted

\oru_{n}
.

Figure
6.2
shows examples of this construction. We choose to draw the graph
\oru(s)
in such a way that the framing of the incoming and outgoing edges at each inner vertex can be read “from top to bottom”.

Figure 6.2:
The graph
\oru(s)
for
s=(2,3,2,2)
with framing shown in red (left) and the graph
\oru(s)
for
s=(1,2,1)
with edge labels (right). Source edges are bolded in blue.

Remark 6.1.2
.

Given a composition
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
, we denote

|s|
the sum of its entries.
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{a})
lives in dimension

\Big|E\Big(\oru(s)\Big)\Big|=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}s_{i}-(n+1)+2n\right)=|s|+n+1

and following Proposition
5.1.10
we have that

\dimension\Big(\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{a})\Big)=|E|-|V|+1=|s|+n+1-(n+2)+1=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}=|s|.

Remark 6.1.3
.

Although the graph
\oru(s)
starts with vertex
v_{-1}
instead of
v_{0}
, all the technology of Section
5.1
can be applied to it. To see this, we can either contract the edge
e_{1}^{n+1}
to obtain a graph
\oru(s)^{\#}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)^{\#}}(\mathbf{a})
is integrally equivalent to
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{a})
, or simply relabel the vertices with
[0,n+1]
. The resulting graph has flows and routes directly in bijection with the flows and routes of
\oru(s)
. Similar to Remark
5.1.3
, this is a particular case of
[
GHMY21
, Cor.2.13]
.

We now start relating the combinatorics of
\oru(s)
to the combinatorics of the
s
-weak order.

Theorem 6.1.4
.

Let
s
be a composition and
\mathbf{d}:=(0,d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d_{i})
where
d_{i}:=\indeg_{i}(\oru(s))
. The set of Stirling
s
-permutations is in bijection with the set of integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\oru(s)
.

Démonstration.

We begin by highlighting the fact that
\mathbf{d}=(0,0,s_{n},s_{n-1},\ldots,s_{2},-\sum_{i=2}^{n}s_{i})
.
Notice that any integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow
f
on
\oru(s)
has zero flow on every source edge. Therefore,
f
is characterized by the fact that the sum of the flows passing by any pair of bump and dip edges satisfies
f(e_{0}^{i})+f(e_{s_{i}}^{i})=s_{n}+\cdots+s_{i+1}
for all
i\in[n-1]
. This means that it suffices to describe the flow in the bump edges
e_{0}^{i}
for all
i\in[n-1]
to describe an integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow on
\oru(s)
.

Given a Stirling
s
-permutation
w
, let
f(e_{0}^{i})
be the number letters strictly greater than
i
that occur before the
i
-block
B_{i}
in
w
.
As for each
j>i
there are at most
s_{j}
such repetitions of
j
, this quantity satisfies
0\leq f_{e_{0}^{i}}\leq s_{n}+\cdots+s_{i+1}
. Meaning that it defines an integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow on
\oru(s)
.

Conversely, given a
\mathbf{d}
-flow on
\oru(s)
, we can build a Stirling
s
-permutation from the flow on the bumps
e_{0}^{i}
for
i\in[n-1]
via an insertion algorithm in the following way. For step
0
we begin with the
s_{n}
consecutive copies of
n
. At step
i
, among the
s_{n}+\cdots+s_{n-i+1}+1
possible positions between letters, insert the
s_{n-i}
consecutive copies of
n-i
in the
f(e_{0}^{n-i})
-th position. After step
n-1
we obtain a permutation of the word
1^{s_{1}}2^{s_{2}}\cdots n^{s_{n}}
. This permutation is
121
-avoiding as all values have been placed in descending order and by blocks.
∎

In Figure
6.3
we illustrate the bijection of Proposition
6.1.4
including the insertion algorithm.

Figure 6.3:
An integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow of
\oru((1,1,2,1,3,1,2))
(left) (only the flow passing by the bumps is shown in blue) and the steps of the insertion algorithm that output the corresponding to the Stirling
s
-permutation
w=33725455716
(right).

Due to Proposition
5.1.41
, as the normalized volume of the flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
is the number of integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows on
\oru(s)
, then we obtain the following enumerative corollary.

Corollary 6.1.5
.

Given a composition
s
, then the volume of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}
is the number of
s
-decreasing trees and the number of Stirling
s
-permutations.

\vol\Big(\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})\Big)=|\mathcal{T}_{s}|=|\mathcal{W}_{s}|=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(1+s_{n-i+1}+s_{n-i+2}+\cdots+s_{n}\right).

Remark 6.1.6
.

It is also possible to give an explicit correspondence between
s
-decreasing trees and integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\oru(s)
even when
s
is a weak composition.

Given an integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow
f
of
\oru(s)
, we construct an
s
-decreasing tree via induction as follows. At step
0
start with a tree with root the node
n
and
s_{n}+1
leaves. At step
i
for
i\in[n-1]
, we have a decreasing tree with labeled nodes
n
to
n+1-i
, and
1+\sum_{k=n+1-i}^{n}s_{k}
leaves that we momentarily label from
0
to
\sum_{k=n+1-i}^{n}s_{k}
along the counterclockwise walk of the tree. Graft to the leaf labeled
f(e^{n-i}_{0})
the labeled node
n-i
with
s_{n-i}+1
leaves. After step
n-1
we obtain a decreasing tree with each node labeled
i
having
s_{i}
children.
Conversely, any
s
-decreasing tree can be built in this way as seen in Proposition
2.5.3
. Therefore, any
s
-decreasing tree is associated to a choice of integers
f(e^{i}_{0})\in[0,\sum_{k=n+1-i}^{n}s_{k}]
for all
i\in[n-1]
. That is, a
\mathbf{d}
-flow of
\oru(s)
.

See Figure
6.4
for an example of the bijection.

Figure 6.4:
An integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow of
\oru((1,1,2,1,3,1,2))
(left) (only the flow passing by the bumps is shown in blue) and its corresponding
(1,1,2,1,3,1,2)
-decreasing tree (right).

The routes of
\oru(s)
play a key role in what follows, so we denote them as

R(k,t,\delta)
. Intuitively this notation comes from the fact that every route of
\oru(s)
starts from
v_{-1}
, lands in a vertex
v_{n+1-k}
via a source edge labeled
e_{t}^{k}
and then follows
k-1
edges that are either bumps or dips meaning a binary choice
\delta
. Figure
6.5
contains an example of this. More formally, we give the following definition.

Definition 6.1.7
.

For
k\in[n+1]
,
t\in[s_{k}-1]
, and
\delta=(\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{k-1})\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}
, we denote by

R(k,t,\delta)
the sequence of edges
(e^{k}_{t_{k}},\,e^{k-1}_{t_{k-1}},\,\ldots,\,e^{1}_{t_{1}})

where

—

t_{k}:=t
,

—

for all
j\in[k-1]
,
t_{j}:=\delta_{j}s_{j}
.

Figure 6.5:
The route
R(5,1,(1,0,1,1))
of
\oru((1,1,2,1,3,1,2))
(bolded in blue) together with the framing orders
\mathcal{I}_{3}
and
\mathcal{O}_{3}
.

Wanting to use the technology of Subsection
5.1.3
and obtain
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, we describe the maximal cliques of routes of
\oru(s)
via Stirling
s
-permutations.

Definition 6.1.8
.

Let
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
be a composition and
w
a Stirling
s
-permutation. Consider
u
to be a (possibly empty) prefix of
w
.
For all
a\in[n]
, we denote by
t_{a}
(or

t_{a}(u)
if
u
is not clear from the context) the number of occurrences of
a
in
u
, and by
c
the smallest value in
[n]
such that
0<t_{c}<s_{c}
. If there is no such value, we set
c=n+1
and
t_{n+1}=1
. Notice that the minimality of
c
implies that either
t_{a}=0
or
t_{a}=s_{a}
for all
a<c
giving us a
\delta\in[c-1]
. We denote

R[u]
the route
R(c,t_{c},\delta)=(e^{c}_{t_{c}},\,e^{c-1}_{t_{c-1}},\,\ldots,\,e^{1}_{t_{1}})
.

For
i\in[|s|]
, we call

w_{i}
the
i
-th letter of
w
, and for
i\in[0,|s|]
we denote by

{w}_{[{i}]}
the prefix of
w
of length
i
, with
{w}_{[{0}]}:=\emptyset
. We denote by

\Delta_{w}
the set of routes
\{R[{w}_{[{i}]}]\,|\,i\in[0,|s|]\}
and abusing notation identify it with the simplex whose vertices are the indicator vectors of these routes.

Remark 6.1.9
.

Notice that the exceptional routes of
(\oru(s),\preceq)
are the two routes that are respectively formed only by bumps
R(n+1,1,(0)^{n})=(e^{n+1}_{1},e^{n}_{0},\ldots,e^{1}_{0})
or by dips
R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})=(e^{n+1}_{1},e^{n}_{1},\ldots,e^{1}_{1})
. Following Definition
6.1.8
we can see that
\Delta_{w}
always contains these two routes as
R[{w}_{[{0}]}]=R(n+1,1,(0)^{n})
and
R[{w}_{[{|s|}]}]=R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})
. See Figure
6.6
for an example of
\Delta_{w}
.

Figure 6.6:
The maximal clique
\Delta_{w}=\{R[{w}_{[{0}]}],\ldots,R[{w}_{[{4}]}]\}
corresponding to the Stirling
(1,2,1)
-permutation
w=3221
. Each route is bolded in blue.

We are now ready to characterize
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
using the
s
-weak order.

Lemma 6.1.10
.

The maximal simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
are exactly the simplices
\Delta_{w}
where
w
ranges over all Stirling
s
-permutations.

Démonstration.

Recall from Proposition
5.1.30
that the maximal simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
are the simplices
\Delta_{C}
, where
C
is a maximal clique of coherent routes of
(\oru(s),\preceq)
. We claim that for a Stirling
s
-permutation
w
, the simplex
\Delta_{w}
is a clique of coherent routes of
(\oru(s),\preceq)
.
Let
1\leq i<j\leq[|s|]
corresponding to two routes
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
and
R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
in
\Delta_{w}
.
As
i<j
, we have the inequalities
t_{a}({w}_{[{i}]})\leq t_{a}({w}_{[{j}]})
for all
a\in[n]
. This means that for any common vertex
v_{n+1-a}
of
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
and
R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
, we have that the incoming (resp. outgoing) edge of
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
is smaller than the incoming (resp. outgoing) edge of
R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
in
\preceq
.
Thus, the routes
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
and
R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
are coherent, and
\Delta_{w}
is a clique of coherent routes. Now, since
\Delta_{w}
has
|s|+1=\text{dim}(\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)})+1
elements, it is a maximal clique and corresponds to a maximal simplex in
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Now let us see that assigning
w
to
\Delta_{w}
is injective. Let
w^{\prime}
be a Stirling
s
-permutation such that
w^{\prime}\neq w
and
i\in[|s|-1]
be the minimal index such that
w_{i}\neq w^{\prime}_{i}
, meaning that
R[w_{[j]}]=R[w^{\prime}_{[j]}]
for all
j<i
. Without loss of generality we suppose that
a:=w_{i}<w^{\prime}_{i}:=b
. We claim that
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{i}]}]
cannot belong to
\Delta_{w}
. Notice that
a
(resp.
b
) is the minimal value
c
in
w_{[i]}
(resp.
w^{\prime}_{[i]}
) such that
0<t_{c}<s_{c}
. If this were not the case then we would have that
w
(resp.
w^{\prime}
) contains the pattern
121
. Thus,
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
contains the edge
e^{a}_{t_{a}({w}_{[{i}]})}
while
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{i}]}]
contains
e^{a}_{t_{a}({w^{\prime}}_{[{i}]})}=e^{a}_{t_{a}({w}_{[{i}]})-1}
as
a<b
. Meaning that
R[w_{[i]}]\neq R[w^{\prime}_{[i]}]
. Finally, for any
j>i
we have that
t_{a}({w}_{[{j}]})\geq t_{a}({w}_{[{i}]})
, so
e^{a}_{t_{a}({w}_{[{i}]})-1}\notin R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
. Therefore,
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{i}]}]\notin\Delta_{w}
and the map
w\mapsto\Delta_{w}
is injective.

Through the chain of bijections from maximal simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, to
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\oru(s)
(Proposition
5.1.40
), to
s
-decreasing trees (Remark
6.1.6
), to Stirling
s
-permutations (Proposition
2.5.14
) we get that this injection is a bijection.
∎

Combining previous results, we now have the following commutative diagram of bijections :

\mathcal{T}_{s}
\mathcal{W}_{s}
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})
\text{MaxCliques}(\oru(s),\preceq)
Prop.
2.5.14
Prop
6.1.4
Prop
5.1.40
Rem.
6.1.6
Lem.
6.1.10

6.1.1 The
1
-Skeleton of the
s
-Weak Order

Theorem 6.1.11
.

Let
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
be a composition and
w
and
w^{\prime}
be two Stirling
s
-permutations.
There is a cover relation between
w
and
w^{\prime}
in the
s
-weak order if and only if the simplices
\Delta_{w}
and
\Delta_{{w^{\prime}}}
are adjacent in
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Démonstration.

Suppose that
w^{\prime}
is obtained from
w
by a transposition along the ascent
(a,c)
. From Corollary
2.5.20
we have that
w=u_{1}B_{a}cu_{2}
and
w^{\prime}=u_{1}cB_{a}u_{2}
where
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are words in
[n]
and
B_{a}
is the
a
-block of the permutations.
We denote by
\ell(u)
the length of a word
u
. Notice that for all
i\in[0,\ell(u_{1})]\cup[\ell(u_{1})+\ell(B_{a})+1,|s|]
, the routes
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
and
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{i}]}]
are equal as the corresponding prefixes consist of the same amounts of the same values.

For the indices
i\in[\ell(u_{1})+1,\ell(u_{1})+\ell(B_{a})-1]
we claim that the routes
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
and
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{i+1}]}]
are equal as well. Notice that for these
i
we have that
t_{b}({w}_{[{i}]})=t_{b}({w^{\prime}}_{[{i+1}]})
for all
b\in[n]\setminus\{c\}
. Also, since we are reading the substring
B_{a}
with these indices, we have that
0<t_{a}({w}_{[{i}]})<s_{a}
. Using that
a<c
, we get that the value
t_{c}({w}_{[{i}]})
(respectively
t_{c}({w^{\prime}}_{[{i+1}]})
) does not play a role in the determination of the edges for the route
R[{w}_{[{i}]}]
(respectively
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{i+1}]}]
). In this way, the vertices of
\Delta_{w}
and
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
differ only in one element. Namely,
R[{w}_{[{\ell(u_{1})+\ell(B_{a})}]}]\in\Delta_{w}
corresponding to the prefix
u_{1}B_{a}
of
w
and
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{\ell(u_{1})+1}]}]\in\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
corresponding to the prefix
u_{1}c
of
w^{\prime}
. As simplices this means that
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
and
\Delta_{w}
share a common facet in
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Reciprocally, suppose that
\Delta_{w}
and
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
are adjacent and thus differ only in one vertex (i.e. one route).
We denote
u_{1}
the longest common prefix of
w
and
w^{\prime}
and
a:=w_{\ell(u_{1})+1}
,
c:=w^{\prime}_{\ell(u_{1})+1}
. Suppose without loss of generality that
a<c
. This gives us that
a\notin u_{1}
as if
a\in u_{1}
then
w^{\prime}
would contain the subword
aca
and thus the pattern
121
. Therefore,
u_{1}B_{a}
is a prefix of
w
and the route
R[{w}_{[{\ell(u_{1})+\ell(B_{a})}]}]
(resp.
R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{\ell(u_{1})+1}]}]
) is in
\Delta_{w}
(resp.
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
) but not in
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
(resp.
\Delta_{w}
).

Thus, the only possibility that
\Delta_{w}
and
\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
differ only on these elements is that
w=u_{1}B_{a}cu_{2}
and
w^{\prime}=u_{1}cB_{a}u_{2}
, where
u_{2}
is their longest common suffix.
This means that there is an
s
-tree rotation along the ascent
(a,c)
between
w
and
w^{\prime}
and Corollary
2.5.20
gives us that
w\lessdot w^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.1.12
.

In this context, we say that the common facet of
\Delta_{w}
and
\Delta_{{w^{\prime}}}
is associated to the transposition of
w
along
(a,c)
. Notice that such a facet lies in the interior of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}
since it separates two interior maximal simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Figure
6.7
shows the graph dual to the DKK triangulation of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
for
s=(1,2,1)
, which corresponds to the (unoriented) Hasse diagram of the
(1,2,1)
-weak order. Notice that in this Figure we omit the routes
R[{w}_{[{0}]}]
and
R[{w}_{[{|s|}]}]
since both appear in
\Delta_{w}
for every
w\in\mathcal{W}_{(1,2,1)}
.

Figure 6.7:
The dual realization of the
(1,2,1)
-permutahedron with vertices indexed by
s
-decreasing trees, Stirling
s
-permutations, maximal cliques of routes (omitting
R(n+1,1,(0)^{n})
and
R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})
), and integer flows.

6.1.2 Higher Faces of the
s
-Permutahedron

We now show that not only the vertices and edges but all the faces of the
s
-permutahedron are also encoded in the triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak).
We begin with some technical results.

Lemma 6.1.13
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation and
R=R(k,t,\delta)
a route of
\oru(s)
.
R
is a vertex of
\Delta_{w}
if and only if the inversion set of
w
satisfies the following inequalities :

1.

|(k,i)_{w}|\geq t
for all
1\leq i<k
such that
\delta_{i}=0
,

2.

|(k,i)_{w}|\leq t
for all
1\leq i<k
such that
\delta_{i}=1
,

3.

|(j,i)_{w}|=0
for all
1\leq i<j<k
such that
(\delta_{i},\delta_{j})=(1,0)
,

4.

|(j,i)_{w}|=s_{j}
for all
1\leq i<j<k
such that
(\delta_{i},\delta_{j})=(0,1)
.

We say that the route
R

implies
these inequalities on inversion sets.

Démonstration.

(
\Rightarrow
) Suppose that
R(k,t,\delta)
is a vertex of
\Delta_{w}
. It means that
R(k,t,\delta)=R[{w}_{[{r}]}]
for a certain
r\in[0,|s|]
. This signifies that
t
corresponds to the number of occurrences of
k
in
{w}_{[{r}]}
, and for all
1\leq i<k
, the number of occurrences of
i
in
{w}_{[{r}]}
is
0
(resp.
s_{i}
) if
\delta_{i}=0
(resp.
\delta_{i}=1
). This gives the announced inequalities on the inversion set of
w
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Reciprocally, suppose that
\inv(w)
satisfies these inequalities. Then there is a prefix
{w}_{[{r}]}
of
w
that contains no occurrence of
i
for
i
such that
\delta_{i}=0
, all
s_{i}
occurrences of
i
for
i
such that
\delta_{i}=1
, and exactly
t
occurrences of
k
. Then this prefix is exactly associated to the route
R[{w}_{[{r}]}]=R(k,t,\delta)
following Definition
6.1.8
.
∎

Definition 6.1.14
.

Let
(w,A)
be a face of
\text{Perm}_{s}
. We define

\Delta_{(w,A)}
as the following intersection of facets of
\Delta_{w}
following Remark
6.1.12
:

\Delta_{(w,A)}:=\bigcap_{(a,c)\in A}\left\{\Delta_{w}\cap\Delta_{w^{\prime}}\,:\,w^{\prime}\text{ is the transposition of\penalty\ $w$ along\penalty\ $(a,c)$}\right\},

and
\Delta_{(w,A)}:=\Delta_{w}
if
A=\emptyset
.

Remark 6.1.15
.

Notice that the
|A|
routes that are in
\Delta_{w}\setminus{\Delta_{(w,A)}}
correspond to the prefixes of
w
that end precisely between two letters of
w
that form an ascent in
A
.

Lemma 6.1.16
.

Let
(w,A)
be a face of
\text{Perm}_{s}
and
w^{\prime}
be a Stirling
s
-permutation. Then,
w^{\prime}
is in the interval
[w,w+A]
if and only if
\Delta_{(w,A)}\subseteq\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
.

Démonstration.

Recall that Theorem
2.5.25
describes
\inv(w+A)
and that
w^{\prime}\in[w,w+A]
if and only if
\inv(w^{\prime})
satisfies
|(c,a)_{w}|\leq|(c,a)_{w^{\prime}}|\leq|(c,a)_{w+A}|
for all
1\leq a<c\leq n
. We proceed to show that these inequalities are exactly the ones implied by the routes that are vertices of
\Delta_{(w,A)}
, in the sense of Lemma
6.1.13
.

Let
(a,c)
be a pair with
|(c,a)_{w}|=:t
.
For each inequality of Lemma
6.1.13
we describe a route
R\in\Delta_{(w,A)}
such that
R\in\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
that implies it.

1.

Consider the inequality
|(c,a)_{w^{\prime}}|\geq t
. We can take the route that corresponds to the prefix of
w
that ends following the
t
-th occurrence of
c
and that does not correspond to an ascent in
A
. Such ending point necessarily lies before the
a
-block since
a<c
.

2.

Consider the inequality
|(c,a)_{w^{\prime}}|\leq t
if and only if
|(c,a)_{w+A}|=t
(i.e. the pair
(a,c)
is not
A
-dependent). This inequality is only implied by routes that contain the edges
e^{c}_{t}
and
e^{a}_{s_{a}}
. Such routes in
\Delta_{w}
correspond to prefixed of
w
that end between the last occurrence of
a
and the
t
-th occurrence of
c
and that are not inside a
b
-block
B_{b}
for any
b<c
. The pair
(a,c)
is
A
-dependent exactly when all such breaks are in
A
, so the corresponding routes are removed in
\Delta_{(w,A)}
.

3.

Consider the inequality
|(c,a)_{w^{\prime}}|\leq t+1
if
t+1<s_{c}
and
|(c,a)_{w+A}|=t+1
(i.e.
(a,c)
is an
A
-dependent pair).
We can take the route that corresponds to the prefix of
w
that ends immediately after the
(t+1)
-th occurrence of
c
. Since
t+1<s_{c}
, other occurrences of
c
appear afterwards. Therefore, this break is not an ascent. Notice that if
t+1=s_{c}
there is no need to check that
|(c,a)_{w^{\prime}}|\leq s_{c}
. ∎

Lemma
6.1.16
also gives us the following alternative characterization of
\Delta_{(w,A)}
.

Corollary 6.1.17
.

\Delta_{(w,A)}=\bigcap_{w^{\prime}\in[w,w+A]}\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
.

Lemma 6.1.18
.

Let
C
be a clique of routes of
(\oru(s),\preceq)
that contains the exceptional routes and at least one route that starts with
e
for each source edge
e
that is not
(v_{-1},v_{0})
, then
\Delta_{C}
is in the interior of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Démonstration.

Suppose that
\Delta_{C}
is a boundary simplex of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
. Since it is contained in a facet that is in the boundary of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, this facet corresponds to a clique of the form
\Delta_{w}\setminus R
, where
w
is a Stirling
s
-permutation and
R
is a route of
\Delta_{w}
that does not correspond to an ascent nor a descent of
w
. Hence, either
R
is an exceptional route or it corresponds to a prefix
{w}_{[{i}]}
such that
w_{i}=w_{i+1}
. In this case,
w_{i}
is the
t
-th occurrence of some
c
in
w
with
t<s_{c}
. In this scenario
R
is the only route of
\Delta_{w}
that starts with the edge
e^{c}_{t}
. As
C\subseteq\Delta_{w}\setminus R
, this means that
C
does not satisfy the condition of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 6.1.19
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation and
A
a subset of its ascents. Then
\Delta_{(w,A)}
is an interior simplex of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Démonstration.

Due to Lemma
6.1.18
it is sufficient to show that
\Delta_{(w,A)}
contains the exceptional routes and at least one route that starts with
e
for each source edge
e
that is not
(v_{-1},v_{0})
. First, it is clear that
\Delta_{(w,A)}
contains the exceptional routes because of Corollary
6.1.17
. Now let
c\in[n]
and
t\in[s_{c}-1]
. In this situation the prefix of
w
that ends with the
t
-th occurrence of
c
corresponds to a route
R
that contains the edge source edge
e^{c}_{t}
. Moreover, there cannot be an ascent of
w
after this prefix since there are still occurrences of
c
afterwards. Thus, the route
R
is not removed from
\Delta_{w}
to
\Delta_{(w,A)}
.
∎

Theorem 6.1.20
.

The application
(w,A)\mapsto\Delta_{(w,A)}
induces a poset isomorphism between the face poset of
\text{Perm}_{s}
and the set of interior simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
ordered by reverse inclusion.

Démonstration.

The fact that all
\Delta_{(w,A)}
are interior simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
is stated in Corollary
6.1.19
. Lemma
6.1.16
gives us the injectivity of this map. We now show that this map is surjective. Let
F
be an interior simplex of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
and
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation that is minimal in the
s
-weak order with respect to the condition that
F\subseteq\Delta_{w}
. Notice that such
w
is unique by the polygonality of
\text{Perm}_{s}
. Then,
F
is an intersection of facets of
\Delta_{w}
. These facets correspond to certain transpositions that can be applied on
w
. We denote
A
the set of ascents corresponding to these transpositions. The minimality of
w
implies that all elements in
A
are ascents of
w
giving us that
F=\Delta_{(w,A)}
.

Finally, let
w,w^{\prime}
be Stirling
s
-permutations and
A,A^{\prime}
the subsets of their respective ascents. Lemma
6.1.16
implies that
[w,w+A]\subseteq[w^{\prime},w^{\prime}+A^{\prime}]
if and only if
\Delta_{(w^{\prime},A^{\prime})}\subseteq\Delta_{(w,A)}
, which proves that the map is a poset isomorphism.
∎

Just as how the atoms of the face poset of
\text{Perm}_{s}
have a characterization as the maximal cliques of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, the coatoms of the face poset also have an explicit characterization in terms of cliques.

Corollary 6.1.21
.

A simplex
\Delta_{C}
of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
corresponds to a maximal interior face of
\text{Perm}_{s}
if and only if
C
is a clique of size
|s|-n+2
that contains the exceptional routes and exactly one route that starts with each source edge that is different from
(v_{-1},v_{0})
.

Démonstration.

We first highlight that for each
i\in[n]
,
\oru(s)
has
s_{i}-1
source edges, implying that there are
\sum_{i=1}^{n}(s_{i}-1)=|s|-n
source edges different from
(v_{-1},v_{0})
. Together with the exceptional routes this gives us that
C
has size
|s|-n+2
. This together with Lemma
6.1.18
tells us that a clique
C
with the above stated properties corresponds with a maximal interior face of
\text{Perm}_{s}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|s|+n+1}
.

Conversely, let
(w,A)
be a facet of
\text{Perm}_{s}
and denote by
C:=\Delta_{(w,A)}
and by
N\subset[0,|s|]
the set of non-ascent positions in
w
, so that
C=\cup_{j\in N}R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
. Since
w
is
121
-avoiding, if it has
n-1
ascents, then the ascents are of the form
(i,c_{i})
where
i<c_{i}
for each
i\in[n-1]
.
Moreover, it is the
s_{i}
-th occurrence of
i
in
w
which produces an ascent pair in
w
. Thus,
N\setminus{\{0,|s|\}}
indexes the first
s_{i}-1
occurrences of
i
in
w
for all
i\in[n-1]
and
|N\setminus{\{0,|s|\}}|=|s|-n
. Now let
j\in N\setminus{\{0,|s|\}}
be a non-ascent position of
w
so that
R[{w}_{[{j}]}]\in C
. If
w_{j}
is the
k
-th occurrence of the value
a
in
w
for some
k\in[s_{a}-1]
, then as before, the route
R[{w}_{[{j}]}]
contains the proper source edge
e^{a}_{k}
.
Since
|N\setminus{\{0,|s|\}}|=|s|-n
, then
C
satisfies the properties of the statement.
∎

Notice that Theorem
6.1.20
does not suffice to answer Conjecture
2.5.12
as this construction lives in an ambient space of dimension
|s|+n+1>n
, and it does not have explicit geometrical coordinates. We now fix the first of these issues using the Cayley trick (Proposition
1.2.31
).

6.2  The Sum of Cubes Realization

To apply the Cayley trick to our triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
, we follow Proposition
5.1.22
to describe
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
as the Cayley embedding of some lower-dimensional polytopes.

Definition 6.2.1
.

Let
p:=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(s_{i}-1)
. We parametrize the space
\mathbb{R}^{|s|+n+1}
of edges of
\oru(s)
as

\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
where
\mathbb{R}^{p}
(resp.
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
) corresponds to the space of source edges (resp. bumps and dips) of
\oru(s)
.

Notice that in the context of
\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, any integer point
f
of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}
(i.e. an integer
\mathbf{i}
-flow of
\oru(s)
) we have that
f(e_{0}^{i})+f(e_{s_{i}}^{i})
is determined by the coordinates
f(e_{t}^{k})
for
k\in[i+1,n+1]
,
t\in[s_{k}-1]
corresponding to source edges. Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}
is affinely equivalent to its projection on to the space
\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
\mathbb{R}^{n}
corresponds to the space of bumps
e^{i}_{0}
for
i\in[n]
.

With this in hand, for a route
R(k,t,\delta)
of
\oru(s)
with
k\in[n+1]
,
t\in[s_{k}-1]
and
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}
, we have that the indicator vector
\mathds{1}_{R(k,t,\delta)}
(Definition
6.1.7
) is
e^{k}_{t}\times\sum_{i\in[k-1],\,\delta_{i}=0}e^{i}_{0}.
Denoting by

\square_{k-1}
the
(k-1)
-dimensional hypercubes with vertices
\{0,1\}^{k-1}\times\{0\}^{n-k+1}
embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we get the following Lemma.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})=\mathcal{C}(\square_{n},\underbrace{\square_{n-1},\ldots,\square_{n-1}}_{\text{$(s_{n-1}-1)$ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{\square_{0},\ldots,\square_{0}}_{\text{$(s_{1}-1)$ times}})

Definition 6.2.3
.

Given a composition
s=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})
, we denote

\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
the fine mixed subdivision of the Minkowski sum of hypercubes
\square_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(s_{i}-1)\square_{i-1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
obtained by intersecting the triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
(projected onto
\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
) with the subspace
{\left\{\frac{1}{p}\right\}}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
as in Remark
1.2.32
.

Applying the Cayley trick (Proposition
1.2.31
) together with the isomorphism between the face poset of
\text{Perm}_{s}
and the interior simplices of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
(Theorem
6.1.20
) gives us the following theorem.

Theorem 6.2.4
.

The face poset of the
s
-permutahedron
\text{Perm}_{s}
is isomorphic to the set of interior cells of
\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
ordered by reverse inclusion. Moreover, Stirling
s
-permutations are in bijection with the maximal cells of
\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
.

Figure
6.8
contains an example of
\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
and the dual graph formed by the adjacency of its interior cells with edges oriented perpendicular to each inner wall.

Figure 6.8:

\text{Subdiv}{\square}((1,2,1))
(left) and its dual graph (right).

Remark 6.2.5
.

Recall from Remark
6.1.3
that the graph
\oru(s)^{\#}
obtained from
\oru(s)
by contracting the edge
(v_{-1},v_{0})
gives a flow polytope integrally equivalent to
\mathcal{F}_{oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
. In our situation of summing cubes, we can use a different parametrization of the space where
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
lives by considering the cube
\square_{n}
(coming from the source edge
e_{1}^{n+1}
) as the Cayley embedding of two hypercubes
\square_{n-1}
(making the bump
e_{0}^{n}
and dip
e_{s_{n}}^{n}
into source edges). We could equivalently intersect
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the hyperplane
x_{n}=\frac{1}{2}
. This allows us to lower the dimension further to
n-1
and obtain a fine mixed subdivision of the Minkowski sum of hypercubes
(s_{n}+1)\square_{n-1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(s_{i}-1)\square_{i-1}
. We use this representation for the figures.

Figure
6.9(a)
shows how given the Stirling
(1,2,1)
-permutation
w=3221
, its maximal clique
\Delta_{3221}
determines the construction of its mixed cell via the Cayley trick. Said mixed cell is highlighted in
6.9(b)
.
Both figures follow the coordinate system
(e^{2}_{0},e^{3}_{0})
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 6.9:
(a) Summands of the Minkowski cell corresponding to
w=3221
with their corresponding routes in the clique
\Delta_{w}
. (b) Mixed subdivision of
2\square_{2}+\square_{1}
corresponding dually to the
(1,2,1)
-permutahedron. The cells are numbered according to Figure
6.7
. The highlighted cell in blue corresponds to
w=3221
as obtained in Figure
6.9(a)
.

Via Theorem
6.2.4
and Remark
6.2.5
we have obtained a realization of
\text{Perm}_{s}
of the correct dimension as in Conjecture
2.5.12
. Still, we want to have explicit coordinates to further prove properties of this polytopal complex. We solve this now using a tropical context.

6.3  The Tropical Realization

Recall that the routes of
\oru(s)
are described in Definition
6.1.7
and consider the DKK triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
constructed in Section
6.1
. Using Definition
5.1.35
and Proposition
5.1.36
we have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

Let
s
be a composition and
\varepsilon>0
a sufficiently small real number.
Consider
\height_{\varepsilon}
to be the function that associates to a route
R=R(k,t,\delta)
of
\oru(s)
the quantity

\height_{\varepsilon}(R)=-\sum_{k\geq c>a\geq 1}\varepsilon^{c-a}(t_{c}+\delta_{a})^{2},

(6.1)

where
t_{c}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\delta_{c}=0,\\
s_{c}&\text{ if }\delta_{c}=1,\end{cases}
for all
c\in[k-1]
.

Then
\height_{\varepsilon}
is an admissible height function for
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Before describing explicit coordinates for
\text{Perm}_{s}
, we take a moment to give an explicit bound for
\varepsilon
such that Lemma
6.3.1
holds.

Theorem 6.3.2
.

For Lemma
6.3.1
, it is enough to take
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{n(1+\sum_{j=2}^{n}(2s_{j}+1))}
.

Démonstration.

Let
P=R(k,t,\delta)
and
Q=R(k^{\prime},t^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
be two routes of
\oru(s)
that are in minimal conflict at a common route
[v_{n+1-y},v_{n-x}]
. Without loss of generality suppose that
Pv_{n+1-y}\prec Qv_{n+1-y}
giving us that
\delta_{x}=1
and
\delta^{\prime}_{x}=0
. Taking
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
as they resolvents consider the quantity
H:=\height_{\varepsilon}(P)+\height_{\varepsilon}(Q)-\height_{\varepsilon}(P^{\prime})-\height_{\varepsilon}(Q^{\prime})
. As
\height_{\varepsilon}
is a height function, Lemma
5.1.34
tells us that
H>0
. Therefore, to determine a bound for
\varepsilon
we compute
H
for the only three cases which can occur between
P
and
Q
as they are adjacent in
\preceq_{\mathcal{I}_{n+1-y}}
and
\preceq_{\mathcal{O}_{n+1-x}}
. Figure
6.10
contains a visual aid corresponding to each of these cases.

Figure 6.10:
The possible cases of minimal conflict between routes. Only one route is bolded in green, and the conflict area is heavily bolded in red.

—

If
k=k^{\prime}=y
,
t\in[s_{y}-2]
, and
t^{\prime}=t+1
we have that in
\height_{\varepsilon}(P)+\height_{\varepsilon}(Q)-\height_{\varepsilon}(P^{\prime})-\height_{\varepsilon}(Q^{\prime})
, all pairs
(a,c)
in formula
cancel out either with
\height_{\varepsilon}(P)-\height_{\varepsilon}(Q^{\prime})
or
\height_{\varepsilon}(Q)-\height_{\varepsilon}(P^{\prime})
except for
(a,c)=(x,y)
. Thus,
H
becomes

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\height_{\varepsilon}(P)+\height_{\varepsilon}(Q)-\height_{\varepsilon}(P^{\prime})-\height_{\varepsilon}(Q^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{y-x}\Big((t+1)^{2}+((t+1)+0)^{2}-(t+0)^{2}-((t+1)+1)^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=2\ \varepsilon^{y-x}>0.

—

If
k>k^{\prime}=y
,
\delta_{y}=0
, and
t^{\prime}=1
, then as in the previous case where
(x,y)
did not cancel out, here
(a,y)
for
x\geq a
do not cancel out. Now, in
\height_{\varepsilon}(P)-\height_{\varepsilon}(Q)
all pairs
(a,c)
cancel out except those for
k\geq x>y
and
x\geq a
whose terms come solely from
\height_{\varepsilon}(P)
. Similarly, for
\height_{\varepsilon}(Q)-\height_{\varepsilon}(P)
all pairs
(a,c)
cancel out except those for
k\geq x>y
and
x\geq a
whose term
t_{c}
comes from
P^{\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{a}
comes from
Q
. Thus, the pairs that do not cancel out in
H
are all pairs
(a,c)
for
k\geq c\geq y
and
x\geq a
and
H
becomes

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\geq a}\varepsilon^{y-a}\Big(\delta_{a}^{2}+(1+\delta^{\prime}_{a})^{2}-{\delta^{\prime}_{a}}^{2}-(1+\delta_{a})^{2}\Big)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq c>y\\
x\geq a\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{c-a}\Big((t_{c}+\delta_{a})^{2}-(t_{c}+\delta^{\prime}_{a})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=2\ \varepsilon^{y-x}-2\ \sum_{x>a}\varepsilon^{y-a}({\delta^{\prime}_{a}}-\delta_{a})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq c>y\\
x\geq a\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{c-a}\Big(2\ t_{c}(\delta_{a}-\delta^{\prime}_{a})+\delta_{a}^{2}-{\delta^{\prime}_{a}}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq 2\ \varepsilon^{y-x}-2\ \sum_{x>a}\varepsilon^{y-a}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq c>y\\
x\geq a\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{c-a}(2s_{c}+1)

\displaystyle\geq 2\ \varepsilon^{y-x}-2\ \varepsilon^{y-x+1}\Big(x-1+x\sum_{k\geq c>y}(2s_{c}+1)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq 2\ \varepsilon^{y-x}\Big(1-\varepsilon\Big(y-2+(y-1)\sum_{k\geq c>y}(2s_{c}+1)\Big)\Big).

Thus, taking
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{n(1+\sum_{j=2}^{n}(2s_{j}+1))}
, gives us
H>0
.

—

If
k^{\prime}>k=y
,
t=s_{y}-1
, and
\delta^{\prime}_{y}=1
, then as in the previous case the pairs that do not cancel out are all pairs
(a,c)
for
k\geq c\geq y
and
x\geq a
. This gives us

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=-\sum_{x\geq a}\varepsilon^{y-a}\Big((s_{y}-1+\delta_{a})^{2}+(s_{y}+\delta^{\prime}_{a})^{2}-(s_{y}-1+\delta^{\prime}_{a})^{2}-(s_{y}+\delta_{a})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}-\sum_{k\geq c>y,\ x\geq a}\varepsilon^{c-a}\Big((t^{\prime}_{c}+\delta^{\prime}_{a})^{2}-(t^{\prime}_{c}+\delta_{a})^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=2\ \varepsilon^{y-x}+2\sum_{x>a}\varepsilon^{y-a}(\delta^{\prime}_{a}-\delta_{a})-\sum_{k\geq c>y,\ x\geq a}\varepsilon^{c-a}\Big(2\ t^{\prime}_{c}(\delta^{\prime}_{a}-\delta_{a})+{\delta^{\prime}_{a}}^{2}-\delta_{a}^{2}\Big).

The final computations are similar to the previous case and so, we omit them. ∎

Remark 6.3.3
.

We disclose to the reader that the bound of Proposition
6.3.2
is not sharp at all. However, the proof of Proposition
6.3.2
, shows that for the cases
s=(1,\ldots,1,k)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
the value
\varepsilon
can be any positive number. For the case
k=1
this is justified in since any two routes in minimal conflict of
\oru(1,\ldots,1)
is the first one where
\varepsilon
just needed to be positive. For the case
k>1
notice that any Stirling
(1,\ldots,1,k)
permutation of length
n
can be obtained from a Stirling
(1,\ldots,1)
permutation of length
n+k
. Passing this idea through our realizations gives us that
\text{Perm}_{(1,\ldots,1,k)}
can be thought as a projection of a
\text{Perm}_{(1,\ldots,1)}
of bigger dimension.

6.3.1 Coordinates for the
s
-Permutahedron

We move on to use the tropical technology of Section
5.2
to obtain explicit points for
\text{Perm}_{s}
. For the remainder of this chapter
\height
is an admissible height function for
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
.

Since in Section
6.2
we used the Cayley trick on the triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
to obtain the mixed subdivision
\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
, the following theorem directly follows from Proposition
5.2.14
.

Theorem 6.3.4
.

The tropical dual of the mixed subdivision
\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
is the polyhedral complex of cells induced by the arrangement of tropical hypersurfaces

\mathcal{H}_{s}(\height):=\left\{\mathcal{T}(F^{k}_{t})\,:\,k\in[2,n+1],\,t\in[s_{k}-1]\right\},

where
F^{k}_{t}(\mathbf{x}):=\bigoplus\height(R(k,t,\delta))\odot\mathbf{x}^{\delta}=\min\left\{\height(R(k,t,\delta))+\sum_{i\in[k-1]}\delta_{i}x_{i}\,:\,\delta\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}\right\}
.

Notice that in this description of the tropical polynomials we avoid using the notation
\langle\delta,\mathbf{x}\rangle
as
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
while
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}
.

Definition 6.3.5
.

We denote by

\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
the polyhedral complex of bounded cells induced by the arrangement
\mathcal{H}_{s}(\height)
.

Theorem 6.3.6
.

The face poset of the geometric polyhedral complex
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
is isomorphic to the face poset of the combinatorial
s
-permutahedron
\text{Perm}_{s}
.

Démonstration.

In Theorem
6.2.4
we saw that the face poset of
\text{Perm}_{s}
is anti-isomorphic to the face poset of interior cells of the mixed subdivision
\text{Subdiv}{\square}(
s
)
. From Lemma
5.2.12
and Theorem
6.3.4
we get that this poset is isomorphic to the poset of bounded cells of
\mathcal{H}_{s}(\height)
, which is precisely the face poset of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
.
∎

Figure
6.11
shows some examples of the
1
-skeleton of such realizations of the
s
-permutahedron using the height function
\height_{\varepsilon}
of Lemma
6.3.1
. Figure
6.12
shows
\text{Perm}_{(1,1,1,2)}(\height_{1})
with its maximal cells unglued. More examples with interactivity can be found in this
website
1
1

1

https://sites.google.com/view/danieltamayo22/gallery-of-s-permutahedra
.

Figure 6.11:
The
1
-skeletons of
\text{Perm}_{(1,1,1,2)}(\height_{1})
(left) and
\text{Perm}_{(1,2,2,2)}(\height_{0.2})
(right) via their tropical realization and the height function
\height_{\varepsilon}
.

Figure 6.12:

\text{Perm}_{(1,1,1,2)}(\height_{1})
with its maximal cells distanced. One cell has been moved further for a better view.

With this polytopal complex in hand, we can describe the explicit coordinates of the vertices of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
as follows.

Definition 6.3.7
.

Let
w\in\mathcal{W}_{s}
be a Stirling
s
-permutation,
a\in[n]
and
t\in[s_{a}]
. We denote

\ell(a^{t})
the length of the prefix of
w
that precedes the
t
-th occurrence of
a
. As in Definition
6.1.8
, this prefix corresponds to the route
R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{t})}]}]
in the maximal clique of coherent routes
\Delta_{w}
.

Theorem 6.3.8
.

The vertices of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
are in bijection with Stirling
s
-permutations.
Moreover, the vertex
\mathbf{v}(w)=(\mathbf{v}(w)_{a})_{a\in[n]}
associated to a Stirling
s
-permutation
w
has coordinates

\mathbf{v}(w)_{a}=\sum_{t=1}^{s_{a}}\left(\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{t})}]}])-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{t})+1}]}])\right).

(6.2)

Démonstration.

We obtain the bijection between vertices of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
and Stirling
s
-permutations directly as a consequence of Theorem
6.3.6
.

Let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation. Its simplex
\Delta_{w}
is associated via Theorem
6.3.4
to the intersection of all regions of the form

\Bigg\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\height(R(k,t,\delta))+\sum_{a\in[c-1]}\delta_{a}x_{a}=\min_{\theta\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}\Big\{\height(R(k,t,\theta))+\sum_{a\in[k-1]}\theta_{a}x_{a}\Big\}\Bigg\},

(6.3)

where
R(c,t,\delta)
is a route in the clique
\Delta_{w}
.
It follows from the bijection between vertices and Stirling
s
-permutations that this intersection is a single point that we denote
\mathbf{v}
.
Let us show that
\mathbf{v}
has the coordinates stated in the theorem.
Let
a\in[n]
. Both routes
R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}]
and
R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1}]}]
are of the form
R(c,t,\delta)
and
R(c,t,\delta^{\prime})
respectively, where
c
is the smallest letter such that the
a
-block
B_{a}
is contained in the
c
-block
B_{c}
in
w
, and
t
denotes the number of occurrences of
c
that precedes
B_{a}
. If the
B_{a}
is contained in no other block we set
c=n+1
and
t=1
(i.e. the routes start with the source edge
(v_{-1},v_{0})
). The indicator vectors
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
satisfy that
\delta^{\prime}-\delta
is the indicator vector of the letters
b\leq a
such that the
B_{b}
is contained in
B_{a}
in
w
.
The fact that both routes belong to
\Delta_{w}
implies that
\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}])+\sum_{b\in[c-1]}\delta_{b}\mathbf{v}_{b}=\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1}]}])+\sum_{b\in[c-1]}\delta^{\prime}_{b}\mathbf{v}_{b}
, thus

\sum_{b\in[c-1]}(\delta^{\prime}_{b}-\delta_{b})\mathbf{v}_{b}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in[a]\\
B_{b}\subseteq B_{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{v}_{b}=\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}])-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1}]}]).

(6.4)

We finish with an induction on
a\in[n]
. If
a=1
, then all terms of Equation
cancel out except
\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}])
and
-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})}]}])
which is exactly Equation
for
\mathbf{v}_{1}
. Suppose we have the vertex description of all coordinates
\mathbf{v}_{b}
for
b<a
. From Equation
and the induction hypothesis we get that

\mathbf{v}_{a}=\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}])-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1}]}])-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in[a-1]\\
B_{b}\subset B_{a}\end{subarray}}\sum_{t=1}^{s_{b}}\left(\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(b^{t})}]}])-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(b^{t})+1}]}])\right).

In this case all terms on the right cancel whenever the value at position
\ell(b^{t})+1\neq a
giving us terms of the form
-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{r})}]}])
for
r\in[2,s_{a}]
or
\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{r})+1}]}])
for
r\in[s_{a}-1]
.
∎

As all maximal cliques
\Delta_{w}
contain the exceptional routes
R(n+1,1,(0)^{n})
and
R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})
(see Remark
6.1.9
), we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.3.9
.

The
s
-permutahedron
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
is contained in the hyperplane

\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\height\Big(R(n+1,1,(0)^{n})\Big)-\height\Big(R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})\Big)\right\}.

(6.5)

As a next step, we show that
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
is a generalized permutahedron as in Definition
2.1.28
.

Theorem 6.3.10
.

Let
1\leq a<c\leq n
and
w
and
w^{\prime}
be Stirling
s
-permutations of the form
u_{1}B_{a}cu_{2}
and
u_{1}cB_{a}u_{2}
respectively, where
B_{a}
is the
a
-block of
w
and
w^{\prime}
. The edge of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
corresponding to the transposition between
w
and
w^{\prime}
is

\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})-\mathbf{v}(w)=\Big(\height(R[u_{1}c])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])-\height(R[u_{1}])-\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}c])\Big)(\mathbf{e}_{a}-\mathbf{e}_{c}).

(6.6)

Démonstration.

Denote
t:=|(c,a)_{w}|+1
meaning that the transposition from
w
to
w^{\prime}
exchanges the
a
-block with the
t
-th occurrence of
c
. Let us describe
\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})-\mathbf{v}(w)
via the expression of the explicit coordinates we obtained in Theorem
6.3.8
. Following the construction of routes
R
from prefixes
u
, notice that any route ending inside
u_{1}
or inside
u_{2}
appears in
\Delta_{w}\cap\Delta_{w^{\prime}}
as it has the same letters the same amount of times meaning that it is cancelled out in
\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})-\mathbf{v}(w)
. Also, all routes ending inside
B_{a}
also appear in
\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})-\mathbf{v}(w)
as in this case
c
is not minimal enough to play the role of defining these routes. Thus, they are also cancelled. The remaining routes that we have are
u_{1}
,
u_{1}c
,
u_{1}B_{a}
and
u_{1}B_{a}c
, which gives the same route as
u_{1}cB_{a}
.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})-\mathbf{v}(w)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})_{a}-\mathbf{v}(w)_{a}\right)\mathbf{e}_{a}+\left(\mathbf{v}(w^{\prime})_{c}-\mathbf{v}(w)_{c}\right)\mathbf{e}_{c}

\displaystyle=\left(\height(R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}])-\height(R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1}]}])-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{1})}]}])+\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1}]}])\right)\mathbf{e}_{a}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(\height(R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{\ell(c^{t})}]}])-\height(R[{w^{\prime}}_{[{\ell(c^{t})+1}]}])-\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(c^{t})}]}])+\height(R[{w}_{[{\ell(c^{t})+1}]}])\right)\mathbf{e}_{c}

\displaystyle=\left(\height(R[u_{1}c])-\height(R[u_{1}cB_{a}])-\height(R[u_{1}])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])\right)\mathbf{e}_{a}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\left(\height(R[u_{1}])-\height(R[u_{1}c])-\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}c])\right)\mathbf{e}_{c}

\displaystyle=\left(\height(R[u_{1}c])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])-\height(R[u_{1}])-\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}c])\right)(\mathbf{e}_{a}-\mathbf{e}_{c}).\qed

Lemma 6.3.11
.

For any strictly decreasing sequence of real numbers
\kappa_{1}>\cdots>\kappa_{n}
, the direction
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\kappa_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
orients the edges of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
according to the
s
-weak order covering relations.

Démonstration.

Using Theorem
6.3.10
, we know that the edges of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
have direction
(\height(R[u_{1}c])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])-\height(R[u_{1}])-\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}c]))(\mathbf{e_{a}}-\mathbf{e_{c}})
. As the routes
R[u_{1}B_{a}]
and
R[u_{1}c]
are in minimal conflict at
[v_{n+1-c},v_{n+1-a}]
and
R[u_{1}]
and
R[u_{1}B_{a}c]
are their resolvents, Lemma
5.1.34
tells us that
\height(R[u_{1}c])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])-\height(R[u_{1}])-\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}c])>0
.
∎

Lemma 6.3.12
.

The support
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height))
(i.e. the union of faces of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
) is a polytope combinatorially isomorphic to the
(n-1)
-dimensional permutahedron. More precisely it can be described as

1.

the convex hull of the vertices
\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma})
where
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
w^{\sigma}
is the Stirling
s
-permutation

w^{\sigma}=\underbrace{\sigma(1)\ldots\sigma(1)}_{s_{\sigma(1)}\text{ times}}\ldots\underbrace{\sigma(n)\ldots\sigma(n)}_{s_{\sigma(n)}\text{ times}},

2.

the intersection of the inequalities

\langle\delta,\mathbf{x}\rangle\geq\height(R(n+1,1,(0)^{n}))-\height(R(n+1,1,\delta)),

(6.7)

\langle\mathbf{1}-\delta,\mathbf{x}\rangle\leq\height(R(n+1,1,\delta))-\height(R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})),

(6.8)

for all
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

Démonstration.

We prove each statement separately.

1.

Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and consider the linear functional
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{a\in[n]}\sigma(a)x_{a}
. We claim that among all faces of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
,
f
is maximized on
\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma})
. To see this let
w
be a Stirling
s
-permutation and
1\leq a<c\leq n
. Notice the following cases.

—

If
w
contains an ascent
(a,c)
such that
\sigma(a)>\sigma(c)
, then
f
is increasing along the edge of direction
\mathbf{e}_{a}-\mathbf{e}_{c}
corresponding to the transposition of
w
along the ascent
(a,c)
.

—

If
w
contains a descent
(a,c)
such that
\sigma(a)<\sigma(c)
, then
f
is increasing along the edge of direction
\mathbf{e_{c}}-\mathbf{e_{a}}
corresponding to the transposition of
w
along the descent
(a,c)
.

—

Otherwise,
w=w^{\sigma}
.

This shows that the vertices of
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height))
have the same normal cones as the
(n-1)
-permutahedron (embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
), hence its normal fan is the braid fan.

2.

From Remark
6.1.9
we know that that all cliques
\Delta_{w}
contain the routes
R(n+1,1,(0)^{n})
and
R(n+1,1,(1)^{n})
. This implies that all vertices of
\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)
are contained in the following intersection of regions given by Equation
6.3
:

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\height(R(n+1,1,(0)^{n}))

\displaystyle=\height(R(n+1,1,(1)^{n}))+\langle\mathbf{1},\mathbf{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=\min_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\big\{\height(R(n+1,1,\delta))+\langle\mathbf{\delta},\mathbf{x}\rangle\big\}\Big\}.

This intersection is precisely the one obtained from the half-spaces defined in Equation
and Equation
.

Moreover, notice that since each
a
-block
B_{a}
in
w^{\sigma}
is a consecutive repetition of the letter
a
, we have that the vertex
\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma})
has coordinates
\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma})_{a}=\height(R[w^{\sigma}_{[\ell(a^{1})]}])-\height(R[w^{\sigma}_{[\ell(a^{s_{a}})+1]}])
. Thus, letting
I:=\{i\in[n]\,:\,\delta_{i}=1\}
for
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, Equation
and Equation
achieve equality with
\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma})
exactly when
\{\sigma(1),\ldots,\sigma(|I|)\}=I
. Meaning that these inequalities define the facets of
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height))
. ∎

Remark 6.3.13
.

With similar arguments we can see that the restriction of the
s
-weak order to a face of
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height))
, associated to an ordered partition, corresponds to a product of
s^{\prime}
-weak orders, one for each part of the ordered partition.

Remark 6.3.14
.

Since the zonotope
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}s_{j}[\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j}]
can be seen to be combinatorially isomorphic to the
(n-1)
-dimensional permutahedron, with Lemma
6.3.12
we have finished answering Conjecture
2.5.12
in the case where
s
is a composition.

We finish by refining Remark
6.3.14
for the case where
\height
is given by Lemma
6.3.1
.

Theorem 6.3.15
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be a small enough real number such that
\height_{\varepsilon}
is an admissible height function for
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
. Then the support
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height_{\varepsilon}))
is a translation of the zonotope
2\sum_{1\leq a<c\leq n}s_{c}\varepsilon^{c-a}[\mathbf{e}_{a},\mathbf{e}_{c}].

Démonstration.

Lemma
6.3.12
and Theorem
6.3.10
tell us that the edges of
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height_{\varepsilon}))
are of the form
[\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma}),\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma^{\prime}})]
, where
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are permutations of
[n]
related by a transposition along an ascent
(a,c)
. Using the DKK height function
\height_{\varepsilon}
from Equation
into Equation
with the modification that the letter
c
is replaced by
s_{c}
occurrences of
c
(or a repeated use of Equation
), we see that the only terms that do not cancel out are those involving the pair
(a,c)
giving us :

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma^{\prime}})-\mathbf{v}(w^{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\Big(\height(R[u_{1}B_{c}])+\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}])-\height(R[u_{1}])-\height(R[u_{1}B_{a}B_{c}])\Big)(\mathbf{e}_{a}-\mathbf{e}_{c})

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{c-a}\Big((s_{c}+1)^{2}+(0+1)^{2}-(0+0)^{2}-(s_{c}+1)^{2}\Big)(\mathbf{e}_{a}-\mathbf{e}_{c})

\displaystyle=2\ s_{c}\ \varepsilon^{c-a}(\mathbf{e}_{a}-\mathbf{e}_{c}).

Thus, all edges with a same direction also have the same length. Since
\supp(\text{Perm}_{s}(\height)[\height_{\varepsilon}])
is combinatorially equivalent to a permutahedron, it follows that it is a zonotope.
∎

6.4  Enumerative Consequences

We finish this chapter showing some enumerative consequences for the elements in the
s
-weak order by calculating volume and the lattice points of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}
via the Baldoni–Vergne–Lidskii formulas (Theorem
5.1.16
).

Corollary 6.4.1
.

Let
s
be a (weak) composition. The number of elements in the
s
-weak order decomposes as

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\Bigl(1+\sum_{r=n-i+1}^{n}s_{r}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bf j}\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}\binom{s_{n-1}+1}{j_{2}}\cdots\binom{s_{2}+1}{j_{n-1}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-i+1)

\displaystyle\,=\,\sum_{\bf j}\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}\cdots\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-i+1)

where the sums range over weak compositions
{\bf j}
of
n-1
such that
\mathbf{j}\succeq(1,1,\ldots,1)
.

We give two proofs. The first proof is geometrical using the flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{\oru_{n}}
while the second is combinatorial using
s
-weak order families.

First proof.

Let
s
be a weak composition. Notice that taking the oruga graph
\oru_{n}
, through Remark
6.1.6
we can see that the flow polytope
\mathcal{F}_{\oru_{n}}(s_{n},s_{n-1},\ldots,s_{2},-\sum_{i}s_{i})
is integrally equivalent to the product of segments
\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}[0,\sum_{r=n-i+1}^{n}s_{r}]
.

Using the Baldoni–Vergne–Lidskii formula for the volume and considering that
\oru_{n}
has shifted outdegrees
o_{i}=1
for
i\in[n-1]
and shifted indegrees
d_{1}=-1
and
d_{i}=1
for
i\in[2,n]
, we have that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\oru_{n}}^{\mathbb{Z}}\Big(s_{n},s_{n-1},\ldots,s_{2},-\sum_{i}s_{i}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bf j}\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}\binom{s_{n-1}+1}{j_{2}}\cdots\binom{s_{2}+1}{j_{n-1}}|\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\oru_{n}}({\bf j}-{\bf 1})|,

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bf j}\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n-1}-1}{j_{2}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}\cdots\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{2}-1}{j_{n-1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}|\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\oru_{n}}({\bf j}-{\bf 1})|,

where the sums range over compositions
{\bf j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-1})
of
n-1
that are
\succeq(1,1,\ldots,1)
. Now, let us count the integer flows in
\mathcal{F}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\oru_{n}}(\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{1})
. For such an integer flow, the incoming flow at vertex
i\in[n-1]
is
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i-1}-(i-1)
. Since the netflow on vertex
i
is
j_{i}-1
, the outgoing flow equals
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-i
. Moreover, there are
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-i+1
possible outgoing integer flows on the two edges
(i,i+1)
for which the choice of flow is independent. Thus, we obtain as wished

\vol\Big(\mathcal{F}_{\oru_{n}}(\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{1})\Big)=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-i+1).\qed

Second proof.

We prove the equality of the (LHS) with each expression on the (RHS) separately.

The first formula using binomials, can be obtained similarly to the proof given in Proposition
2.5.3
enumerating
s
-decreasing trees. For step
0
we begin with the node labeled
n
. At step
1
we choose which of its
s_{n}+1
children become nodes (as opposed to leaves). This gives a coefficient
\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}
, where
j_{1}\in[\min(s_{n}+1,n-1)]
. Among these
j_{1}
nodes, one carries the label
n-1
with
s_{n-1}+1
children. Right before step
i
, we have a partial
s
-decreasing tree with
i
nodes labeled by
[n+1-i,n]
,and
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i-1}-(i-1)
unlabeled nodes, where
j_{k}
is the number of non-empty subtrees of the node
n+1-k
, whose positions were chosen at the step
k
. At step
i
we choose
j_{i}
from the
s_{n+1-i}+1
new children to become new nodes. At this point we get
n_{i}:=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-(i-1)
nodes without labels. To ensure that
n_{i}>0
, we have that constraint to verify that the
j_{k}
chosen so far satisfy that
\sum_{k=1}^{i}j_{i}\geq i
. Afterwards we choose one of these
n_{i}
nodes to carry the label
n-i
. We stop after step
n-1
where we have obtained an
s
-decreasing tree.

The second formula that utilizes multiset binomials can be obtained in the following way of building a Stirling
s
-permutation. Let us say that an
a
-block
B_{a}
covers a
b
-block
B_{b}
in
w
if
a
is the smallest letter such that the
B_{b}\subset B_{a}
. At step
0
we begin with the sequence of
s_{n}
consecutive occurrences of
n
and choose a number
j_{1}
of blocks to be covered by
B_{n}
or appear before or after it in the final multipermutation
w
. Notice that there are
\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n}+1}{j_{1}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}
ways to arrange these blocks among the occurrences of
n
. Choose one of these
j-1
blocks to be the sequence of
s_{(n-1)}
consecutive occurrences of
n-1
. At the beginning of step
i
, we have a partial Stirling
s
-permutation that contains all occurrences of the letters in
[n+1-i,n]
and
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i-1}-(i-1)
unlabeled blocks, where
j_{k}
is the number of blocks covered by the
(n+1-k)
-block, whose positions were chosen at the step
k
. At step
i
choose the number
j_{i}
of blocks that to be covered by the
(n+1-i)
-block and one among the
\mathchoice{\left(\kern-4.43993pt{\binom{s_{n+1-i}-1}{j_{i}}}\kern-4.43993pt\right)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n+1-i}-1}{j_{i}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n+1-i}-1}{j_{i}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}{\bigl(\kern-2.77501pt{\binom{s_{n+1-i}-1}{j_{i}}}\kern-2.77501pt\bigr)}
ways to arrange them between the first and the last occurrence of
n+1-i
. Choose one of the
n_{i}:=j_{1}+\cdots+j_{i}-(i-1)
unlabeled block to be the
(n-i)
-block. After step
n-1
we have inserted
B_{1}
and finished constructing a Stirling
s
-permutation.
∎

Remark 6.4.2
.

When
s
is a composition, Corollary
6.1.5
, tells us that the RHS of Corollary
6.4.1
gives the volume of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
. This together with Proposition
5.1.41
gives us that

\displaystyle\vol\Big(\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(1,0,\ldots,0,-1)\Big)

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}^{\mathbb{Z}}(0,s_{n},s_{n-1},\ldots,s_{2},-\sum_{i}s_{i})\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\oru_{n}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(s_{n},s_{n-1},\ldots,s_{2},-\sum_{i}s_{i})\Big|,

(6.9)

where the second equality follows from our Remark
6.1.3
. Thus, the formulas of Corollary
6.1.5
as decomposition formulas for the volume of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}(\mathbf{i})
as well. This is the approach followed in
[
KMS21
]
to prove geometrically the Lidskii decomposition of the Kostant partition formula given in Theorem
5.1.16
.

Remark 6.4.3
.

Notice that the Lidskii formulas hold for zero also when
s
is a weak composition. Moreover, each term of the RHS of the first Lidskii formula is nonnegative for
s_{i}\geq 0
, however terms of the RHS of the second formula can be negative as in Example
6.4.4
for
s=(1,0,1)
.

Example 6.4.4
.

For
n=3
, Corollary
6.4.1
yields

\displaystyle(1+s_{3})(1+s_{2}+s_{3})

\displaystyle=\binom{s_{3}+1}{1}\binom{s_{2}+1}{1}+\binom{s_{3}+1}{2}\cdot 2,

(6.10)

\displaystyle=\binom{s_{3}+1}{1}\binom{s_{2}-1}{1}+\binom{s_{3}+2}{2}\cdot 2.

(6.11)

We finish with some perspectives stemming out from our work.

Perspective 6.4.5
.

Ceballos and Pons also conjectured (
[
CP19
, Conjecture 2]
) that there exists a geometric realization of
\text{Perm}_{s}
(when
s
is a strict composition) such that the
s
-associahedron can be obtained from it by removing certain facets.
Our realizations seem very promising for providing a geometric relation between
s
-permutahedra and
s
-associahedra, but this is still work in progress.

Perspective 6.4.6
.

Since in this chapter our objective was to answer Conjecture
2.5.12
, we concentrated our efforts on studying the framing of
\oru(s)
described in Definition
6.1.1
to obtain the triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\oru(s)},\preceq\nolinebreak)
which encoded the
s
-weak order. It is natural to ask about other DKK triangulations of
\mathcal{F}_{\oru(s)}
coming from other framings and the poset structures that arise from them. In the case of the caracol graph
\car(s)
, Bell et al.
[
vGMY23
]
studied the length framing and the planar framing whose corresponding DKK triangulation correspondingly realized in their dual graph the
s
-Tamari lattice and the principal order ideal
I(\nu)
in
Young’s lattice
, where
\nu_{i}=1+s_{n-i+1}+s_{n-i+2}+\cdots+s_{n}
.

Perspective 6.4.7
.

Although the
s
-weak order of Ceballos and Pons is defined for weak compositions, all of our realizations are defined uniquely for compositions as
\oru(s)
requires that
s_{i}>0
for
i\in[n+1]
. Moreover, even though Corollary
6.1.5
does not hold for weak compositions, Remark
6.1.6
and the first proof of Corollary
6.4.1
let us see that there is a connection. Computationally we have found certain variations of
\oru(s)
to describe the
s
-weak order for certain families of weak compositions. This is still work in progress.

Chapitre 7  Recovering Permutrees with Flow Polytopes

In this chapter we apply our machinery of flow polytopes to give another answer to Perspective
2.4.55
. That is, we recover permutree lattices from the dual triangulation of a framed graph. This chapter comes from ongoing work
[
GMP+23b
]
.

Our inspiration for the following came from looking at
\car_{n}
and
\oru_{n}
in Figure
7.1
and deducing a way to create other similar graphs.

Figure 7.1:
The oruga
\oru_{n}
(left), caracol
\car_{n}
(middle), and mariposa
\mar_{n}
(right) graphs.

7.1  M-Moves

Taking
\oru_{n}
as a starting point, we provide an operation on the edges of
\oru_{n}
to create new graphs akin to
\car_{n}
.

Definition 7.1.1
.

Let
G
be a graph on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
. The graph obtained by the
M-move
applied on an edge
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
is the graph
M(G)
on
\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{n}\}
with edge set
E(M(G)):=(E(G)\setminus\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\})\cup\{(v_{0},v_{i+1}),(v_{i},v_{n})\}
.

Endow
\oru_{n}
with the framing
e_{0}^{i}\preceq_{\mathcal{I}_{n+1-i}}e_{1}^{i}
(resp.
e_{0}^{i-1}\preceq_{\mathcal{O}_{n+1-i}}e_{1}^{i-1}
) for
i\in[1,\ldots,n-1]
. The graph
M(\oru_{n})
resulting from the M-move on the edge
e_{0}^{i}
(resp.
e_{1}^{i}
) has the new modified framing
\preceq^{\prime}
where the new edges inherit the framing of the removed edge. Figure
7.2
contains examples of the M-moves with the normal embedding of
\oru_{n}
. This framing is called the
inherited framing
of
M(\oru_{n})
.

We call the resulting graph after doing all possible M-moves on
\oru_{n}
the
mariposa graph
denoted
\mar_{n}
. Mariposa is the Spanish word for butterfly and as before, this name comes from the embedding of
\mar_{n}
shown in Figure
7.1
.

Figure 7.2:
The resulting framed graph
M(\oru_{n})
after applying an M-move
M
on the edge
e^{i}_{0}
(top) (resp.
e^{i}_{1}
(bottom)) of
\oru_{n}
. The edges affected by the
M
are bolded.

Notice that any sequence of M-moves gives a graph that can be considered to be between
\oru_{n}
and
\mar_{n}
. Moreover, all M-moves are independent of each other as they manipulate disjoint sets of edges. As we can apply M-moves to none, one, or both of the edges between the vertices
v_{n+1-i}
and
v_{n+1-i}
, the following definition is in order.

Definition 7.1.2
.

Let
\delta\in\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\cdot\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{n-2}\cdot\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
be a permutree decoration. Apply on
\oru_{n}
the sequence of M-moves on the edge
e_{0}^{i}
if
\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
(resp.
e_{1}^{i}
if
\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
). We call the resulting graph the

\delta
-bicho graph
and denote it by
\bic_{\delta}
. Bicho is the closest Spanish word for critter that encompasses caterpillars, snails, and butterflies at the same time.

Remark 7.1.3
.

With Definition
7.1.2
it is clear that
M(\bic_{\delta})=\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}
for a collection
M
of M-moves if and only if
\delta
refines
\delta^{\prime}
. In this context we say that
M(\delta)=\delta^{\prime}
. Similar to Figure
2.21
, this gives us Figure
7.3
containing all possible
\delta
-bicho graphs for decorations
\delta\in\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\cdot\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{2}\cdot\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
.

Figure 7.3:
All
\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\cdot\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{2}\cdot\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
-bicho graphs. The bottom graph is the
oruga graph
, the second graph in the middle is the
caracol graph
, and the top graph is the
mariposa graph
.

With our
\delta
-bicho graphs in hand, we can study their corresponding flow polytopes
\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}(\mathbf{a})
.

Remark 7.1.4
.

Since we obtain
\bic_{\delta}
from
\oru_{n}
via M-moves, we get that its polytope lives in dimension

\Big|E\Big(\bic_{\delta}\Big)\Big|=2n+\Big|\Big\{i\in[n]:\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\}\Big\}\Big|+2\Big|\Big\{i\in[n]:\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}\Big\}\Big|

and

\dimension\Big(\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}(\mathbf{a})\Big)=n+\Big|\Big\{i\in[n]:\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\}\Big\}\Big|+2\Big|\Big\{i\in[n]:\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}\Big\}\Big|.

Similar to the case of
\oru(s)
, we denote routes of
\bic_{\delta}
as

R(k_{1},t_{1},\theta,k_{2},t_{2})
. Intuitively this notation comes from the fact that every route of
\bic_{\delta}
starts from
v_{0}
, lands in a vertex
v_{n+1-k_{1}}
via a source bump or dip, follows
k_{1}-k_{2}-1
edges that can be either bumps or dips (depending on
\delta
) and finally jumps from a vertex
v_{n+1-k_{2}}
to
v_{n}
by a sink bump or dip. In a more formal fashion, we give the following definition.

Definition 7.1.5
.

Let
\delta\in\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\cdot\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}^{n-2}\cdot\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
and denote the sequences of sets

\displaystyle\Theta=

\displaystyle\Big(\emptyset,\Theta_{2},\ldots,\Theta_{n-1},\emptyset\Big)

where

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0\in\Theta_{i}\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\},\\
