1\in\Theta_{i}\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Omega^{\inEdge}=

\displaystyle\Big(\{0,1\},\Omega^{\inEdge}_{2},\ldots,\Omega^{\inEdge}_{n-1},\emptyset\Big)

where

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0\in\Omega^{\inEdge}_{i}\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\},\\
1\in\Omega^{\inEdge}_{i}\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\Omega^{\outEdge}=

\displaystyle\Big(\emptyset,\Omega^{\outEdge}_{2},\ldots,\Omega^{\outEdge}_{n-1},\{0,1\}\Big)

where

\displaystyle\begin{cases}0\in\Omega^{\outEdge}_{i}\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\},\\
1\in\Omega^{\outEdge}_{i}\text{ if }\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}.\end{cases}

We denote the routes of
\bic_{\delta}
as

R(k_{1},t_{1},\theta,k_{2},t_{2})
where
2\leq k_{2}<k_{1}\leq n
,
t_{1}\in\Omega^{\inEdge}_{n+1-k_{1}}
,
t_{2}\in\Omega^{\outEdge}_{n+1-(k_{2}-1)}
, and
\theta\in\prod_{i=n+1-(k_{1}-1)}^{n+1-k_{2}}\Theta_{i}
.

Example 7.1.6
.

Consider the route
R(6,1,(1,0,1),3,0)
of
\bic_{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}}
depicted in Figure
7.4
.
In this case
1\leq 3<6\leq 6
,
1\in\Omega^{\inEdge}_{2}
,
0\in\Omega^{\outEdge}_{6}
, and
\delta\in\Theta_{3}\times\Theta_{4}\times\Theta_{5}
where

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=\emptyset\times\{1\}\times\{0,1\}\times\{0\}\times\{0,1\}\times\emptyset\times\emptyset,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\inEdge}

\displaystyle=\{0,1\}\times\{1\}\times\emptyset\times\{0\}\times\emptyset\times\{0,1\}\times\emptyset,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\outEdge}

\displaystyle=\emptyset\times\{0\}\times\emptyset\times\{1\}\times\emptyset\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}.

Figure 7.4:
The route
R(6,1,(1,0,1),3,0)
of
\bic_{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}}
bolded in blue.

Remark 7.1.7
.

Given a route
R:=R(k_{1},t_{1},(\theta_{n+1-(k_{1}-1)},\ldots,\theta_{n+1-(k_{2})}),k_{2},t_{2})
in
\bic_{\delta}
, the action of doing an M-move
M
on an edge
e_{k}^{i}=(v_{n+1-(i+1)},v_{n+1-{i}})
of
\bic_{\delta}
either transforms
R
into a pair of routes
M(R):=\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
where

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle:=R(i,\theta_{n+1-i},(\theta_{n+1-(i-1)},\ldots,\theta_{n+1-k_{2}}),k_{2},t_{2})

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle:=R(k_{1},t_{1},(\theta_{n+1-(k_{1}-1)},\ldots,\theta_{n+1-(i+1)}),i+1,\theta_{n+1-i})

if
k_{2}\leq i\leq k_{1}-1
and
k=\theta_{n+1-i}
or and does not affect
R
at all and
M(R)=\{R_{0}\}
where
R_{0}:=R
. The routes
R_{0},R_{1},R_{2}
are all shown in the graph
\bic_{M(\delta)}
. See Figure
7.5
for an example of this construction in
\oru_{5}
.

Figure 7.5:
The M-move corresponding to the bump
(v_{2},v_{3})
applied on two routes (left) of
\oru_{5}
and their responding routes after the M-move (right). The edges affected by the M-move are bolded.

7.2  Enumerating Integer
\mathbf{d}
-Flows in
\bic_{\delta}

In this section we draw several parallels between the enumeration of permutrees done in
[
PP18
, Sec.2.5]
and the enumeration of integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows in
\bic_{\delta}
.

Remark 7.2.1
.

Recall that
\mathbf{d}:=(d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{n-1},-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}d_{i})
which in our current context is defined as
d_{i}=\indeg_{i}(\bic_{\delta})-1
. Moreover, notice that for any permutree decoration
\delta
we have that this vector is always
\mathbf{d}=(0,1\ldots,1,-n+1)
.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

Let
\delta
be a permutree decoration and
\delta^{\prime}
be any decoration such that
\delta^{-1}(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}})={\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}})
and
\delta^{-1}(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}})={\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}})
. Then
|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})|=|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})|
.

Démonstration.

Suppose that
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
are permutree decorations with the stated property. Notice that the definition of M-moves and
\bic_{\delta}
is symmetric between choosing the decorations
and
except for the defined framing. Therefore, the graphs
\bic_{\delta}
and
\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}
are isomorphic. Since the counting of integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows depends only on the underlying graph structure, we get our result.
∎

Lemma 7.2.3
.

Suppose that a permutree decoration
\delta
decomposes as
\delta=\delta^{\prime}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\delta^{\prime\prime}
. Then
|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})|=|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta^{\prime}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})|\cdot|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\delta^{\prime\prime}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})|
.

Démonstration.

From Definition
7.1.2
it follows that an M-move corresponding to a decoration
\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}
partitions the set of routes of
\bic_{\delta}
into routes of the form
R(k_{1}^{\prime},t_{1}^{\prime},\theta^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime},t_{2}^{\prime})
where
i+1\leq k_{2}^{\prime}<k_{1}^{\prime}\leq n
and
R(k_{1}^{\prime\prime},t_{1}^{\prime\prime},\theta^{\prime\prime},k_{2}^{\prime\prime},t_{2}^{\prime\prime})
where
2\leq k_{2}^{\prime\prime}<k_{1}^{\prime\prime}\leq i
. This partitions the edges of
\bic_{\delta}
into two sets depending on the type of routes that contain them. In this context any
\mathbf{d}
-flow on
\bic_{\delta}
gets divided into the two sections of the graph. Since the sections form a partition, the flows are independent. As the subgraphs corresponding to this partition are isomorphic to
\bic_{\delta^{\prime}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}}
and
\bic_{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\delta^{\prime\prime}}
after a relabeling of the vertices the result follows.
∎

Remark 7.2.4
.

For
n=1
the graph
\bic_{\delta}
is isomorphic just two vertices with two edges no matter the decoration. In this case
\mathbf{d}=(0,0)
. As such, in this case there is only
1
integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow which is the constant flow
f_{0}
assigning to every edge flow
0
. Similarly, for
n=0
, we have that
\bic_{\delta}
is a point with
\mathbf{d}=(0)
and the same constant flow
f_{0}
is the only integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow.

Theorem 7.2.5
.

For any decoration
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}^{n}
, the sets of integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\bic_{\delta}
and
\delta
-permutrees are in bijection.

Démonstration.

Given
i\in[n]
denote respectively by
e^{n+1-i}_{0}:=(v_{i-1},v_{i})
and
e^{n+1-i}_{1}:=\begin{cases}(v_{i-1},v_{i})\text{ if }\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\\
(v_{i-1},v_{n})\text{ if }\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\end{cases}
the bump edges of
\bic_{\delta}
and the dip edges of
\bic_{\delta}
that can receive any flow when the netflow is given by
\mathbf{d}
. In this context notice that a
\mathbf{d}
-flow
f
is uniquely determined by the values
f(e^{n+1-i}_{0})
as all source edges have
0
flow, and all dip edges have flow that satisfies the recursive relations

f(e^{n+1-i}_{1})=\begin{cases}f(e^{n+1-(i-1)}_{0})+f(e^{n+1-(i-1)}_{1})+1-f(e^{n+1-i}_{0})\text{ if }\delta_{i-1}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\\
f(e^{n+1-(i-1)}_{0})+1-f(e^{n+1-i}_{0})\hskip 76.96475pt\text{ if }\delta_{i-1}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}.\end{cases}

Take
f
an integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow of
\bic_{\delta}
. Let us construct a
\delta
-permutree (with nodes relabeled
\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{n}\}
to avoid confusion). We do this starting from the integers
f(e^{i})
and proceeding like in the proofs of Lemma
3.1.3
and Theorem
3.2.6
using the insertion algorithm as follows. Begin by taking an
n\times n
grid. At step
1
, since
f(e^{n+1-1})=0
, place the vertex
v^{\prime}_{1}
anywhere in the first column. For convenience place it at position
(1,1)
. At step
i
, place the vertex
v^{\prime}_{i}
in position
(i,i-f(e^{n+1-i}))
moving all previous vertices that are at the same or greater height upwards by
1
unit. After step
n
we obtain a permutation table. Decorating this table with
\delta
and applying the permutree insertion algorithm gives us a
\delta
-permutree
T_{f}
such that
f_{T_{f}}=f
. See Figure
7.6
for an example.

Figure 7.6:
The construction of a
-permutree (right) from the integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow characterized by the flow
(0,1,2,1,2)
on the bumps of
\bic_{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}}
(left).

Conversely, let
T\in\mathcal{PT}(\delta)
be a
\delta
-permutree. We define the function
f_{T}(e^{n+1-i})=\big|\{j\in[i-1]\,:\,i\to j\}\big|
. That is, the amount of vertices in
LA_{i}
(resp.
A_{i}
) with label smaller than
i
. We claim that this function is compatible with the netflow
\mathbf{d}
. Indeed, notice that although the definition tells us that
f_{T}(e^{n+1-i})\leq i-1
for
i\in[n]
, we actually have that
f_{T}(e^{n+1-1})=0
and
f_{T}(e^{n+1-i})\leq f_{T}(e^{n+1-(i-1)})+1
for
i\in[1,n]
. This occurs as
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}
implies that either
i-1\to i
and
f_{T}(e^{n+1-i})\leq f_{T}(e^{n+1-(i-1)})
or
i\to i-1
and
f_{T}(e^{n+1-i})=f_{T}(e^{n+1-(i-1)})+1
. So we know that the amount of flow is the correct one and respects the netflow
\mathbf{d}
. Thus,
f_{T}
can be extended to a unique integer
\mathbf{d}
-flow of
\bic_{\delta}
.
∎

Corollary 7.2.6
.

Given a permutree decoration
\delta
, we have that

\big|\mathcal{PT}(\delta)\big|=\big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\big|=\vol\Big(\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}(\mathbf{i})\Big).

Démonstration.

Due to Lemma
7.2.3
and
7.2.2
,
\big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\big|
can be decomposed as a product of
\big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\big|
where
\delta^{\prime}_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}
. As these integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows are in bijection with
\delta^{\prime}
-permutrees through Theorem
7.2.5
and
\delta
-permutrees follow the same decomposition given in Proposition
2.3.8
, we have that
|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})|=|\mathcal{PT}(\delta)|
. We finish by applying Theorem
5.1.41
.
∎

7.3  Permutrees and Cliques of
\bic_{\delta}

As before, the routes of the framed graph
\bic_{\delta}
give us the triangulation
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\bic_{\delta}},\preceq\nolinebreak)
of
\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}(\mathbf{i})
. We proceed to show how simplices of these triangulations relate with each other.

Definition 7.3.1
.

Let
C
be a collection of routes of
\bic_{\delta}
and
M
a sequence of M-moves on a subset of edges of
\bic_{\delta}
. We denote by

M(C)
the set
\bigcup_{R\in C}M(R)
following Remark
7.1.7
.

Lemma 7.3.2
.

If
C
is a set of coherent routes of
\bic_{\delta}
, then
M(C)
is a set of coherent routes of
\bic_{M(\delta)}
.

Démonstration.

It is enough to prove the lemma for the case when
M
is an M-move on a single edge
e^{i}_{k}=(v_{n+1-(i+1)},v_{n+1-i})
. In this case
\delta\lessdot\delta^{\prime}
and
\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}
has the same framing of
\bic_{\delta}
except for
\mathcal{O}_{n+1-(i+1)}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n+1-i}
. Let
P
and
Q
be two routes in
C
. We have the three following cases.

—

If both
P
and
Q
contain
e^{i}_{k}
then
M(P)=\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
and
M(Q)=\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
. Let us see that
\{P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
is a clique.

—

The pairs of routes
\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
,
\{P_{1},Q_{2}\}
,
(Q_{1},P_{2})
and
\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
are all cliques as each pair of routes has no common vertices except
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
. Thus, no possible conflict.

—

For
\{P_{1},Q_{1}\}
notice that their suffixes satisfy
v_{n+1-i}P_{1}=v_{n+1-i}P
and
v_{n+1-i}Q_{1}=v_{n+1-i}Q
and that both their source edges are
P_{1}v_{n+1-i}=Q_{1}v_{n+1-i}
. Therefore, the edges on which
P_{1}
and
Q_{1}
differ are the same as the edges on which
P
and
Q
differ. As
P
and
Q
are coherent,
P_{1}
and
Q_{1}
are coherent. Similar for
\{P_{2},Q_{2}\}
.

Thus, all routes in
\{P_{1},P_{2},Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
are pairwise coherent.

—

If
P
contains
e^{i}_{k}
but
Q
does not, then
M(P)=\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
and
M(Q)=\{Q_{0}\}
.

—

If
Q
contains
e^{i}_{1-k}
then notice that as
Q_{0}=Q
,
v_{n+1-i}P_{1}=v_{n+1-i}P
and
P_{2}v_{n+1-(i+1)}=Pv_{n+1-(i+1)}
, and their framing is inherited from
P
and
Q
, we have that no conflict can arise.

—

If
Q
does not contain
e^{i}_{1-k}
then the common edges of
P_{1}
and
Q_{0}
(resp.
P_{2}
and
Q_{0}
) are common edges of
P
and
Q
meaning that no conflict occurs.

Thus,
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
Q_{0}
are pairwise coherent. The argument is similar with the roles of
P
and
Q
reversed.

—

If neither
P
nor
Q
contain
e^{i}_{k}
then
M(P)=\{P_{0}\}
and
M(Q)=\{Q_{0}\}
. As
P=P_{0}
,
Q=Q_{0}
and their framings are the same as before, we have that
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
are coherent.

As in all cases
M(P)\cup M(Q)
is formed by pairwise coherent routes, we have that
M(C)=\bigcup_{R\in C}M(R)
is a clique of
\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 7.3.3
.

For the exceptional routes
R^{d}=R(n,1,(1)^{n-2},2,1)
and
R^{b}=R(n,1,(0)^{n-2},2,1)
of
\oru_{n}=\bic_{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n}}
, we have that
M(R^{d})\cup M(R^{b})
are the exceptional routes of
\bic_{M\big(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n}\big)}
.

Lemma 7.3.4
.

Let
\delta,\delta^{\prime}
be permutree decorations such that
\delta
refines
\delta^{\prime}
and
M
be the collection of M-moves transforming
\bic_{\delta}
to
\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}
. Then every
C^{\prime}\in\text{MaxCliques}(\bic_{\delta^{\prime}},\preceq)
is of the form
M(C)
for some
C\in\text{MaxCliques}(\bic_{\delta},\preceq)
.

Démonstration.

Suppose that
\delta\lessdot\delta^{\prime}
in the refinement order of permutree decorations. In this case, as an M-move replaces 1 edge by 2 edges, we have that
|E(\bic_{\delta^{\prime}})|=|E(\bic_{\delta})|+1
. Since the number of vertices after an M-move is the same, we also obtain that
\dimension(\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}}(\mathbf{d}))=\dimension(\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}(\mathbf{d}))+1
. Suppose that
C
is a maximal clique of coherent routes of
\bic_{\delta}
. Lemma
7.3.2
tells us that
M(C)
is a clique of coherent routes of
\bic_{\delta^{\prime}}
. We proceed to show that
M(C)
is maximal, that is,
|M(C)|=|C|+1
.

Let
e^{i}_{k}
denote the edge on which
M
is applied. Consider
P,Q
to be routes of
\bic_{\delta}
containing the edge
e^{i}_{k}
and let
z
denote the number of such routes. Following Remark
7.1.7
we have that if
v_{n+1-i}P=v_{n+1-i}Q
then
P_{1}=Q_{1}
(resp. if
Pv_{n+1-(i+1)}=Qv_{n+1-(i+1)}
then
P_{2}=Q_{2}
). Thus, each path from
v_{0}
to
v_{n+1-(i+1)}
and each path from
v_{n+1-i}
to
v_{n}
appearing in a route of
C
containing the edge
e^{i}_{k}
contributes a route to
|M(C)|
. The fact that each route containing
e^{i}_{k}
contains
2
such paths together with how coherent paths interact after an M-move shown in the proof of Lemma
7.3.1
gives us that there is a total of
z+1
such paths in
C
. That is, there are
z+1
routes in
M(C)
contributed from routes containing
e^{i}_{k}
. Any route
R
that does not contain
e^{i}_{k}
contributes just
1
to the cardinality of
|M(C)|
as
M(R)=\{R_{0}\}
. Since there are
|C|-z
such routes we are done.

The general case follows from the fact that all M-moves affect different edges coming from
\oru_{n}
.
∎

Notice that Lemma
7.3.4
agrees with the fact that
\dim(\oru_{n})=n
and
\dim(\mar_{n})=3n-4
as between
\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n}
and
\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}^{n}
there are
2(n-2)
coarsening covering relations in the order of permutree decorations.

Theorem 7.3.5
.

Let
\delta
be a permutree decoration. The set of
\delta
-permutrees
\mathcal{PT}(\delta)
is in bijection with the set of maximal cliques of coherent routes
\text{MaxCliques}(\bic_{\delta},\preceq)
.

Démonstration.

Recall from Lemma
2.3.7
that the number of edges of a
\delta
-permutree is precisely
n+1+|\{i\in[n]:\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}\}\}|+2|\{i\in[n]:\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}\}|
. This coincides with the number of elements of a maximal clique of coherent routes of
\bic_{\delta}
being
1
more than the dimension calculated in Remark
7.1.4
.

Let
M
denote the collection of M-moves such that
M(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n})=\delta
. We now proceed to label the edges of a
\delta
-permutree
T
with routes in
\bic_{\delta}
by doing an enhanced version of the permutree insertion algorithm (see Definition
2.3.4
).

Consider a decorated table corresponding to
T
. Below the table place the all-dip route
R^{d1}_{1}:=R(n,1,(1)^{n-2},2,1)
. Now, in increasing order for
i\in[2,n-1]
, if
\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
(resp. if
\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
) draw a red wall below (resp. above) the coordinate decorated with
\delta_{i}
and apply an M-move
M_{i}
on the edge
e^{n+1-i}_{1}
of
R^{di}_{1}
as per Definition
7.1.2
to obtain
M_{i}(R^{di})=\{R^{d(i+1)}_{1},R^{d(i+1)}_{2}\}
. At the bottom of the left (resp. right) zone created by the red wall place
R^{d(i+1)}_{2}
(resp.
R^{d(i+1)}_{1}
). This gives at the bottom of each zone delimited by the red walls an exceptional route of
\bic_{\delta}
as given by
M(R^{d})
.

For step
0
, from the bottom of each zone generate a string labeled with the route of that zone. At step
i
extend these labeled strings by a unit of
1
. If the height of a subset of labeled strings is the same as the height of a decorated coordinate and are not separated by a red wall, the decoration at the coordinate catches the labeled strings and then releases a new set of labeled strings through the following rules.

—

If
\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}
, then change
e^{n+1-i}_{1}
by
e^{n+1-i}_{0}
in the entering route.

—

If
\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}
, then undo the M-move on
e^{n+1-i}_{1}
from the two entering routes to get a single new route and change its edge
e^{n+1-i}_{1}
by
e^{n+1-i}_{0}
.

—

If
\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}}
, then change
e^{n+1-i}_{1}
by
e^{n+1-i}_{0}
in the entering route and apply the M-move of
e^{n+1-i}_{0}
.

—

If
\delta_{i}=\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}
, apply rule
and then rule
.

The rules are depicted in Figure
7.7
. The algorithm ends when the strings have lengths
n+1
. See Figure
7.8
for an example of a
\delta
-permutree obtained through this altered insertion algorithm.

Figure 7.7:
The release and catching rules of the modified permutree insertion algorithm.
A
(resp.
B
) represents a path from
v_{0}
to
v_{n+1-(i+1)}
(resp. from
v_{n+1-i}
to
v_{n}
).

Denote by
\mathcal{R}(T)
the collection of routes that label the edges of
T
. We claim that
\mathcal{R}(T)
is a clique of coherent routes of
\bic_{\delta}
. Moreover, we claim that
\mathcal{R}(T)=M(C)
where
C
is the clique of routes that labels the
\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n}
-permutree with same table as
T
. Suppose that
\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n}\lessdot\delta
and the general case follows through any sequence of coarsening relations between
\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}^{n}
and
\delta
. Following the proof of Lemma
6.1.10
, the maximal clique
C
in
\oru((1,\ldots,1))=\oru_{n}
is endowed with a total order on the routes dictated by its corresponding permutation. As each of the routes
R\in C
is transformed to
M(R)
, these covering relations are translated into the covering relations represented by the rules
,
, and
. Following the cases in the proof of Lemma
7.3.2
we get that
\bigcup_{R\in C}M(R)=:M(C)
and
\mathcal{R}(T)=M(C)
which is a set of coherent routes according to Lemma
7.3.4
.

Since
\mathcal{R}(T)
has the same cardinality as the edges of
T
, it follows that
\mathcal{R}(T)
is a maximal clique of coherent routes of
\bic_{\delta}
. As M-moves are independent between themselves and the rules in Figure
7.7
are also independent as they interact with different edges of
T
, it follows that this is an injection from
\delta
-permutrees to maximal cliques of routes of
\bic_{\delta}
. Corollary
7.2.6
tells us that these sets have the same cardinality, so we obtain a bijection.
∎

Figure 7.8:
A
-permutree with edges labeled by its maximal clique of flows.

Remark 7.3.6
.

The routes at the top of the zones of a
\delta
-permutree
T
are exactly the exceptional routes
M(R^{b})
ordered from left to right via the M-moves on
e^{i}_{0}
if
\delta_{i}\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/upp}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}}\}
. Thus, we can also describe the modified permutree insertion algorithm via strings going down.

Corollary 7.3.7
.

Let
T_{1},T_{2}
be different
\delta
-permutrees. The simplices
\Delta_{T_{1}}
and
\Delta_{T_{2}}
are adjacent in
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\bic_{\delta}},\preceq\nolinebreak)
if and only if there is a covering relation between
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
in the
\delta
-permutree rotation order.

Démonstration.

Notice that an edge
i\to j
with
i<j
in a
\delta
-permutree is equivalent to doing rule
\delta_{i}
followed by rule
\delta_{j}
. Since these rules act on disjoint sets of routes in
\bic_{\delta}
, we have that the rules satisfy
\delta_{i}\circ\delta_{j}=\delta_{j}\circ\delta_{i}
applied to the maximal route in
RD_{i}
.

Suppose now that there is an
ij
-rotation from
T_{1}
to
T_{2}
with
i<j
. As the
ij
-rotation keeps the structure of
T_{1}
intact except for the edge
i\to j
changed to
j\to i
, we have by Theorem
7.3.5
and the start of our proof that this is equivalent to
\Delta_{T_{1}}
and
\Delta_{T_{2}}
differing exactly in
1
route. This route corresponds to applying rule
\delta_{j}
before
\delta_{i}
in the modified permutree insertion algorithm.
∎

In Figure
7.9
we present the
3
initial covering relations of the
-permutree rotation lattice.

Figure 7.9:
The rank
1
covering relations of the
-permutree rotation lattice with each permutree having edges labeled by their maximal clique of flows.

We finish this thesis with several perspectives that would be interesting to study stemming from the work of this last chapter.

7.4  Perspectives and Conjectures

With Theorem
7.2.5
we opted to define a direct bijection between
\delta
-permutrees and integer
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\bic_{\delta}
when
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}
instead of proving the full decomposition shown in Proposition
2.3.8
for permutrees of said decorations. This leads us to the following conjecture.

Conjecture 7.4.1
.

Let
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}
. The number of
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\bic_{\delta}
satisfies the recursive formula

\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\Big|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\delta^{-1}\big(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\big)\\
J\subseteq\delta^{-1}\big(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\big)\end{subarray}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta_{[i-1]\setminus{J}}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\Big|\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta_{[i+1,n]\setminus{J}}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\Big||J|!.

Lemmas
7.2.2
and
7.2.3
together with Conjecture
7.4.1
would give us the following general recursive decomposition of the number of
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\bic_{\delta}
as follows.

Conjecture 7.4.2
.

For any permutree decoration
\delta
, the number of
\mathbf{d}
-flows of
\bic_{\delta}
satisfies the recursive formula

\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\Big|=\prod_{k\in[\ell]}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[b_{k-1},b_{k}]\cap\delta^{-1}\big(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\big)\\
J\subseteq[b_{k-1},b_{k}]\cap\delta^{-1}\big(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}}\big)\end{subarray}}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta_{[b_{k-1},i-1]\setminus{J}}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\Big|\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\bic_{\delta_{[i+1,b_{k}]\setminus{J}}}}^{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbf{d})\Big||J|!

where
\{b_{0}<\cdots<b_{\ell}\}=\{0,n\}\cup\delta^{-1}(\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/uppdownn}})
.

Perspective 7.4.3
.

The proof given in Theorem
7.2.5
also raises several interesting questions. A concrete one being if it is possible to extend this bijection from the case
\delta\in\{\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/nonee}},\raisebox{-1.94444pt}{\includegraphics[height]{includes/figures/downn}}\}
to all possible permutree decorations. A more abstract one is that the proof makes use of an insertion algorithm construction based on a vector like in the proofs of Lemma
3.1.3
and Theorem
3.2.6
. As these proofs characterized certain properties of permutrees linked to inversion and cubic vectors respectively, we wonder if there is some other vector for permutrees at play here. At first glance it would appear to be the bracket vector of
\delta^{\hskip 1.42271pt\updownarrow\hskip-6.544pt\leftrightarrow}
-permutrees where
\delta^{\hskip 1.42271pt\updownarrow\hskip-6.544pt\leftrightarrow}
denotes the decoration obtained after flipping all decorations and reversing the order of
\delta
.

Perspective 7.4.4
.

Corollary
7.3.7
gives us a realization of the
1
-skeleton of the permutreehedron
\PPT(\delta)
as the dual poset of the maximal interior faces of
\text{Triang}_{DKK}({\bic_{\delta}},\preceq\nolinebreak)
with the inherited framing of
\oru_{n}
. It would be nice to extend this to get a full realization of
\PPT(\delta)
as the dual of this triangulation. Furthermore, Proposition
5.1.22
tells us that the Cayley trick might give us an interesting family of sums of polytopes somewhere between summations of cubes and summations of simplices. Finally, applying the tropical toolbox described in Subsection
5.2
together with the DKK framing should give us explicit coordinates and a new explicit realization of
\PPT(\delta)
.

Perspective 7.4.5
.

In this chapter we concentrated on the M-moves applied onto
\oru_{n}
since the lattice structure of
\delta
-permutrees was already given in
[
PP18
]
. It would be interesting to study M-moves on
\oru(s)
. The source edges created by the M-move correspond to adding a 1 to a coordinate of
s
, but it is unclear what role the new sink edges play. The hope is that M-moves define lattice congruences of the
s
-weak order. If so, this could give a path for permutrees to find their place in the
s
and
\nu
world.

Perspective
7.4.4
of obtaining explicit polytopes for these lattices applies to this case as well to obtain

s
-permutreehedra
. This is of particular interest as if
\PPT_{s}(\delta)
are obtained by removing facets from
\text{Perm}_{s}
, we would have a positive answer to the conjecture
[
CP19
, Conj.2]
about obtaining the
s
-associahedron by removing certain facets from the
s
-permutahedron when
s
is free of zeros.

Perspective 7.4.6
.

Permutrees only define a certain subset of lattice congruences on the weak order. To study the whole set of lattice congruences of a lattice one needs to understand the join irreducible elements of the lattice as done in
[
PS17
]
for the weak order. In our case that would mean extending the arc diagram characterization of join irreducibles of permutations to
s
-arc diagrams for Stirling
s
-permutations or even
s
-decreasing permutrees for the most general case.

Perspective 7.4.7
.

Moreover, it would be interesting to define and understand

(s,\delta)
-permutrees
and develop their combinatorics as in
[
PP18
]
for permutrees. That is, finding

(s,\delta)
-permutree congruences
, an

s
-insertion algorithm
,

s
-permutreehedra
, and even

(s,\delta)
-permutree Hopf algebras
.

Perspective 7.4.8
.

In
[
MM13
]
the concepts of graphs, flows, and the Kostant partition functions were given not only for type
A
but also for types
B
and
D
. Since the definition of
\oru_{n}
seems to have a certain connection with the Coxeter graph of type
A
, it may be worth it to look for similar graphs for types
B
and
D
and specify M-moves on them. This could also help generalize the Cambrian phenomenon found in all Coxeter types to the context of permutrees.
